The Civilization Newbie's Bible

Learn to play Civilization VI and VII from zero, and learn the real economics, politics, geopolitics and strategy the game is built on.

Part 0

Start Here

Why this game is worth your time, and what it may do to your judgment. Then how each lesson works, which game to play, and a map of every real-world concept you will meet.

Before You Play: A Warning

In October 1962, by the best-known account, one officer on a Soviet submarine refused to approve firing a nuclear torpedo. This game can show you how that day might have gone the other way.

Why play a game about empires

Civilization hands you a people, a patch of land and one instruction: win. It never asks you to be good. Within a few evenings you will find yourself doing things you would condemn in a newspaper. You will settle land a neighbor wanted, break a promise when keeping it costs too much, and attack a friend who has grown weak. The game often rewards all of it, and it never shows you a single grieving family.

That is the reason to play. Many schoolbooks describe the world as it should work. Treaties hold, borders are respected, and the good side wins. Civ shows you the world from the chair of a ruler who wants to survive and grow. Rules exist in it, and they matter, but power decides whether they hold. In 416 BC Athenian envoys told the island of Melos, which asked to stay neutral, that "the strong do what they can and the weak suffer what they must" (Richard Crawley's translation of Thucydides). Athens then took the island. In Civ you will hear yourself making the same argument.

Niccolò Machiavelli, a Florentine diplomat sacked and tortured by the family he later advised, saw the gap in 1513. In chapter 15 of The Prince he wrote that "how one lives is so far distant from how one ought to live, that he who neglects what is done for what ought to be done, sooner effects his ruin than his preservation" (W. K. Marriott's translation). Civ is a machine for teaching what is done. This book adds what ought to be done, and shows you where the two split.

The warning

A game with one scoreboard teaches one goal. The Roman philosopher Cicero called the highest good a life should aim at the summum bonum. Civ has one too, and it is victory. Peace, justice and mercy count only when they help you win. When they get in the way, the game makes it easy to drop them.

Watch what this does to your judgment. In Civ VI a nuclear strike kills part of a city's Population and poisons the land around it for up to 20 turns. On screen, a city's number drops by a few points. After an hour of play, that number stops meaning people. The same habit, in a real leader, is how terrible choices start to feel like sensible ones. That habit forms in the player, and it can outlast the game. Notice the moment you start treating cities as counters.

Where the dark view goes too far

The cynic has a case. The strong, it says, write the rules and drop them when the stakes are high enough. Athens told Melos that right counts only between equals in power. Even in Civ VI, anger over a broken promise fades, and a superpower can shrug it off. So rules serve power, and the will to win beats everything.

Yet the game also pushes back. Break promises in Civ VI and other leaders hold Grievances (a running record of who wronged whom) against you and become harder to deal with. In a contest where you face the same rivals again and again, a reputation for keeping your word is worth more than one betrayal. Robert Axelrod's tournaments, in the Diplomacy lesson, show why.

The real world agrees. Strong countries write rules that suit them. Those rules then bind them too, and they often accept that, because rules are cheaper than force. Sometimes winning and doing right even point the same way, as they did with the Marshall Plan. The honest picture is darker than the schoolbook and brighter than the cynic.

The luck since 1945

Only two nuclear weapons have been used in war, at Hiroshima and Nagasaki in August 1945. That record has had close calls.

  • 27 October 1962. US destroyers dropped signaling depth charges to make the Soviet submarine B-59 surface near Cuba. By the best-known account, its captain thought war might have begun and wanted to fire a nuclear torpedo. A launch needed all three senior officers to agree, and Vasily Arkhipov refused. He persuaded the captain to surface. Vadim Orlov, an officer aboard, later said the captain lost his temper but calmed down.
  • 26 September 1983. A Soviet warning system reported one American missile launch, then four more. The duty officer, Stanislav Petrov, judged it a false alarm and waited for confirmation that never came. The satellites had malfunctioned.
  • November 1983. A NATO exercise called Able Archer 83 rehearsed nuclear escalation. Its new realism, at a tense time, led some Soviet leaders to believe it was cover for a real first strike. Scholars disagree on how close it came.
  • 25 January 1995. Russian radar mistook a Norwegian science rocket for a possible attack. The nuclear briefcase, used to authorize a launch, was switched on and brought to President Boris Yeltsin. It is the only known case. The rocket was heading out to sea, and no launch was ordered.

In the 2003 film The Fog of War, Robert McNamara, US defense secretary in 1962, said of the Cuban crisis: "at the end we lucked out. It was luck that prevented nuclear war." Luck was not the whole story. After Cuba the two powers set up a direct hotline in 1963, and in 1969 began talks to limit their nuclear arsenals. Some scholars, most prominently Nina Tannenwald, argue that a "nuclear taboo" grew up, though others doubt it. Still, about eighty years is a short run. At least twice, the record may have rested on one officer who would not take a frightening signal as proof of war. That is cause for gratitude, and for unease.

Should every leader play?

Armies have trained officers with games for two centuries. In 1824 a junior Prussian officer, Georg von Reisswitz, showed his war game, Kriegsspiel, to the king and his senior generals. The chief of the General Staff called it schooling for war. The king ordered a set for every regiment. So should every politician and official play Civ?

The case for. Civ lets you feel, in an evening, lessons that cost real states centuries. You learn how a sensible defensive move frightens a neighbor into arming, how a war you meant to keep short drags on, and how fast you can talk yourself into anything. A leader who has caught that habit in a game may spot it sooner in a crisis.

The case against. Civ also teaches some lessons that would be dangerous in a leader. Its citizens are numbers. Its ruler never faces an election, a free press or a funeral. It knows every rule exactly, lives for six thousand years, and is never jailed for a war. The Six Assumptions covers gaps like these. A leader who learned only from Civ would learn the worst of it.

This book's answer is to play it, and to keep asking what the game leaves out.

Two guardrails

First, a description of how the world is does not tell you what to do about it. The Scottish philosopher David Hume made that point in 1740, and a book of facts about power is no license to use it. Second, the book never hands you a verdict. Many lessons carry a box called The hard question: a real case where winning and doing right pulled apart, the strongest honest case on each side, and a question back to you. They lead to the capstone lesson, Winning and Doing Right.

Sources: Thucydides, History of the Peloponnesian War, trans. Richard Crawley, 5.89; Machiavelli, The Prince, trans. W. K. Marriott, chapter XV; Summum bonum; Nuclear weapons (Civ6); Grievances (Civ6); Vasily Arkhipov; Stanislav Petrov; Able Archer 83; Norwegian rocket incident; The Fog of War transcript (2003); Moscow–Washington hotline; SALT; Nuclear taboo; Kriegsspiel; "David Hume," Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy.

How to Use This Book

A game about building empires turns out to be a fast, safe place to feel ideas that real states and businesses learn slowly and at great cost. This book teaches both, and asks one question the game never does.

How the book works VI VII

Turn 1. A Settler (the unit that founds a city) stands on an unknown map, and the game is waiting for you to found a city. What you decide in the next 60 turns shapes the rest of the game. That is the first course: how to win Civ VI on Prince (the default difficulty), and how to find your way around Civ VII. The second course runs underneath it. About sixty real-world concepts, from opportunity cost to the security dilemma, are each tied to a game plan, so you feel the idea before you learn its name.

Every lesson has the same shape.

  1. The rule. The game mechanic, with real numbers and a source.
  2. The concept. A true story about real people first, then the idea it shows, who named it, and when.
  3. The map. A two-column table: what happens in the game, and what happens in the world.
  4. Where the map breaks. The ways the game gets the idea wrong. This part is never skipped.
  5. The hard question. In many lessons, a short box on a real case where winning and doing right pulled apart. It gives both sides and no verdict.
  6. The lab. A short in-game experiment: predict, play, measure. A debrief explains why your result might differ; a bridge shows the pattern in the world.
  7. Check yourself. Three questions, on the game, the concept and the world. Answer before you look: the PDF prints the answers at the back, and the web hides each behind a "Show answer" button.
  8. Takeaways. One line for the game and one for life.

Badges and stamps. VI marks a Civ VI rule, with the Gathering Storm (the second Civ VI expansion) expansion and all free updates. VII marks Civ VII. Civ VII still changes, so its numbers carry the patch they were checked against, such as 1.5.0.

Concept cards. Each concept gets one full card, in the lesson where it fits best. Elsewhere a one-line concept lens links back to it.

Three ways to read this book

Play tonight. Read these, start a game, and come back to the rest when a question comes up. On the first evening you can skip the purple concept cards and the labs.

  1. What Kind of Game Is This?
  2. Setting Up Your First Game
  3. Reading the Screen and Taking a Turn
  4. Where to Settle
  5. Your First 60 Turns
  6. One-Page Checklists

The course. Read in order, run each lab, and answer the questions before you check. The web edition adds a concept flashcard deck and three live models: a combat calculator, a growth-and-housing simulator and an expansion compounding model.

Never played Civ? You can read this book without ever starting the game. Read each lesson's opening, its concept cards (a true story, the idea in one line, and where the game gets it wrong) and its hard questions. Skip the labs and the numbered step lists, which are for a player at the keyboard. Part 10 and Part 11 stand on their own. On the web edition, the "Ideas only" button does the sorting for you: it hides the game rules, labs and step lists.

Why a game book teaches real concepts

Teachers already do it. In 2011 Kimberly Weir and Michael Baranowski of Northern Kentucky University reported teaching international politics with Civilization. At the University of Georgia, Jeffrey Berejikian's course INTL 4265 has student teams run governments in Civilization IV and explain their moves with ideas such as complex interdependence. Kurt Squire's 2004 dissertation studied students learning world history through Civilization III.

Naming is what transfers. You can play a hundred games without noticing that your opening was compounding. The concept cards name the idea, and the labs make you test it. The two cards below explain why that step matters.

The breaks are lessons too. Knowing where Civ's version of an idea fails keeps you from mistaking the game for history. The Six Assumptions turns this on the whole game.

The claim is modest. Civ will not make you a better manager, investor or diplomat. It gives you a fast, safe place to feel ideas that are slow and costly to feel in real life, and naming them gives that feeling a chance to carry over.

Sources: Weir & Baranowski, "Simulating History to Understand International Politics," Simulation & Gaming 42(4) (2011); INTL 4265 syllabus; Squire, Replaying History (2004).

Learning science

Transfer of learning

"Brain training", computer puzzles sold to sharpen the whole mind, had grown into a multimillion-pound industry when a team led by Adrian Owen put it to the test, publishing in 2010. For six weeks, 11,430 volunteers trained online several times a week on tasks built to improve reasoning, memory, planning and attention. They got better at every task they practiced. On tasks they had not practiced, even closely related ones, the team found no evidence of any gain. A skill stays close to where it was learned unless something carries it across.

Transfer of learning: using what you learned in one setting in another. Low-road transfer happens by itself when the new setting looks like the old. High-road transfer takes deliberately pulling out the principle and asking where else it applies.

Open in the PDF, page 8

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Each game plan is paired with a named concept card.Bridging: pulling out the principle on purpose, for high-road transfer.
Labs replay the same 20 or 30 turns with one thing changed.Hugging: practice kept close to the real situation, for low-road transfer.

Where the map breaks

  • Far transfer, from games to unrelated real skills, has weak support. A 2017 review by Sala and Gobet found that the better the study, the smaller the effect.
  • Civ's rules were designed, not discovered. A principle that works in the game may only reflect a designer's choice.
  • The feeling of "getting it" is not the test. Doing a new task better is.

Read next

  • Accessible: Sala & Gobet, "Does Far Transfer Exist?" (2017); Owen et al., "Putting Brain Training to the Test," Nature 465 (2010).
  • Primary: Perkins & Salomon, "Teaching for Transfer" (1988).

David N. Perkins & Gavriel Salomon, "Teaching for Transfer," Educational Leadership 46(1): 22–32 (1988)

Learning science

Experiential learning cycle

Edwin Link learned about pumps, valves and bellows in his father's piano and organ company, and in 1929 he used them to build a cockpit on the ground that pitched and rolled like an airplane. In the Depression he sold some to amusement parks. Then the Army Air Corps took over carrying the air mail, and 13 of its airmen died in 78 days. Few had much practice flying by instruments in bad weather. In 1934 Link flew to a meeting with the Air Corps through fog its officers thought unflyable. They ordered six trainers at $3,500 each. In the box a pilot could fly blind, get it wrong, see why, and go again. More than 500,000 American pilots trained on Link's machines.

Experiential learning cycle: Kolb's loop of four stages. Have a concrete experience, reflect on it, form an idea that explains it, then test the idea by acting, and the next experience looks different.

Open in the PDF, page 9

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Playing a gameConcrete experience
Looking back after the game, then reading the concept cardReflection, then the abstract idea that explains it
The lab: replay with one changeActive experimentation

Where the map breaks

  • Kolb's cycle is a description, and a weakly tested one. The "learning styles" inventory that grew out of it has poor evidence behind it, and this book does not use it.
  • Hidden AI difficulty bonuses can teach you the wrong reason for a loss, so you can reflect your way to a false lesson.
  • Reflection is the stage players skip. Go straight to the next game and the cycle does nothing.

Read next

  • Accessible: James Madison University's two-page summary of Kolb's model.
  • Primary: Kolb, Experiential Learning (1984).

David A. Kolb, Experiential Learning: Experience as the Source of Learning and Development (Prentice-Hall, 1984)

Lab: Can you build a bridge?

Predict first: of the sentences you will write in this lab, guess how many you can rewrite with no game words: most, about half, or few.

  1. Read the opportunity cost card.
  2. Play 20 turns. Each time you pick what a city builds next (its production choice), write one sentence naming what you gave up.
  3. Close the game. List three real decisions from this week where the same sentence applies.

Measure: how many of your in-game sentences you can rewrite without any game words.

What you should see: most rewrite easily once you have named the idea. The ones that don't usually point to a place where the game's map breaks.

If your result differs: some costs exist only in the game, such as one of the 3 charges a Builder (the unit that farms and mines land) uses up; a sentence about those marks a place the map breaks. If you wrote very few sentences, your cities finished few items in 20 turns, so play on until you have ten.

In the world: a household budget runs on the same sentence. The money for a takeout meal is money not saved or spent elsewhere. Write down one purchase this week and what it pushed out.

In the game: read the rule, play the lab, then read the card.

In the world: after any experience, name the principle and one other place it applies, because naming is the step that makes learning carry over.

Which Game Should You Play?

Two games carry the name. One has stood still since 2023 and matches the numbers in this book. The other is newer, still changing with each patch, and may not.

Sources: SteamCharts Civ VI; SteamCharts Civ VII; Steam store Civ VI; Steam store Civ VII; Civilization VI (fandom); Civilization VII (Wikipedia); Platinum Edition; Anthology; Modding (Civ6); 2K support (1.2.2); 1.4.0 notes; CivFanatics on Earthrise.

The recommendation

Learn on Civ VI, then read Part 7 for Civ VII. Civ VI's rules have not moved in years, so this book will match your screen, and its player base is nearly three times larger.

Civ VII is worth playing, but its systems changed as recently as 2026, and Part 7's numbers may drift with the next patch. Its cities, tiles, diplomacy and war will feel familiar after VI.

Concept lens: the first game you learn shapes your habits in every later one. Early choices lock in a path, so learn on the stable game. Path dependence

In the game: Civ VI with Gathering Storm (the second Civ VI expansion) for your first ten games; then Part 7 and Civ VII.

In the world: learn a skill on a stable version of the tool before you chase the newest one.

What Kind of Game Is This?

In 1993 a magazine preview gave a whole kind of game its name, "4X", after four words: explore, expand, exploit, exterminate. They describe a good deal of history too. Here is what they mean and how you win.

Explore, expand, exploit, exterminate VI VII

The label "4X" comes from a 1993 Computer Gaming World preview by Alan Emrich on the space game Master of Orion. The four X's are the four things you do.

  • Explore: send units (your movable pieces) into the unknown map to find land, resources and other civilizations, or civs.
  • Expand: train Settlers (the units that found cities), the unit that founds a city, and found new cities.
  • Exploit: work the land around your cities for yields. Food grows a city; Production (the yield that builds units and buildings) builds things; Gold, Science, Culture and Faith (the yield spent on religion) pay for the rest.
  • Exterminate: fight, or deter, your rivals.

Turns. You give your orders and press End Turn; then the computer players (the AI) move. A Standard-speed Civ VI game runs up to 500 turns. Civ VII splits a game into three ages: Antiquity, Exploration and Modern 1.5.0.

Ways to win. In your first game you need not pick one; aim for three or four cities by turn 60 (Your First 60 Turns). Part 5 has the detail; the Glossary defines terms such as Tourism (cultural pull that wins a Culture victory).

GameVictoryIn one line
VIScienceBe first through the space-race projects.
VICultureYour visiting tourists outnumber each rival's domestic tourists.
VIDominationControl the original capital of every other civ.
VIReligiousYour religion holds more than half the cities of every civ still in the game.
VIDiplomaticReach 20 Diplomatic Victory Points, earned mostly in the World Congress.
VIScoreHave the highest score when the turn limit is reached.
VII 1.5.0Military, Cultural, EconomicLead second place in Dominion points (a military score), Tourism or GDP by a set multiple (6× late in Exploration, falling to 1.25× at the end) for 5 turns.
VII 1.5.0ScienceReach 100 points, then keep a Launch Pad (a space-race building) active for 5 turns.
VII 1.5.0Domination or scoreEliminate every rival, or have the highest score when the Modern age ends.

Sources: Alan Emrich (Wikipedia); Victory (Civ6); Speed (Civ6); Civ VII patch 1.4.0 notes.

Concept lens: picking a victory early and lining every system up behind it is grand strategy: matching ends, ways and means. Grand strategy

Check yourself

  1. In Civ VI, a rival's religion holds more than half the cities of every civ except yours. It now holds 5 of your 10 cities. How many more must it convert to win?

    Show answer

    One. A Religious Victory needs more than half the cities of every civ still in the game. 5 of 10 is exactly half, so a sixth city completes it.

  2. You are going for a Science Victory, yet you keep spending Production (the yield that builds units and buildings) on an army you never use. Which concept names the mismatch?

    Show answer

    Grand strategy: matching ends, ways and means. The end is the space race. Production is a means, and an unused army spends it on a way that serves no end you picked.

  3. A student faces four exams, a sports tryout and a part-time job in the same month. What would picking a "victory" first do for her?

    Show answer

    It is grand strategy on a small scale. She names the end that matters most, then lines up her hours (the means) behind it, instead of giving every task an equal share.

In the game: explore early, expand fast, exploit your land, and fight only when it serves your plan.

In the world: sixty ideas sound like a lot, yet you will have felt each one in play before you read its name.

Part 1

Your First Game of Civ VI

From the setup screen to turn 60. You will pick settings that let you learn, read the screen, found good cities, and run an opening that gives you three or four cities.

Setting Up Your First Game

On the hardest level the computer is no smarter than on the easiest. It just starts with 3 Settlers (the units that found cities) to your 1 and double Production (the yield that builds units and buildings). Pick a level where you can still see why you lost.

The rule: difficulty is a set of AI bonuses VI

Civ VI has eight difficulty levels. The AI does not get smarter as you go up. It gets bigger numbers: extra yields, extra combat strength (the fighting number every unit has) and extra starting units. On the three lowest levels you get the bonuses instead.

LevelAI Science, Culture, FaithAI Production, GoldAI combat strengthYour combat strengthAI starting Settlers
Settler+0%+0%−1+31
Chieftain+0%+0%−1+21
Warlord+0%+0%−1+11
Prince (default)+8%+20%001
King+16%+40%+101
Emperor+24%+60%+202
Immortal+32%+80%+302
Deity+40%+100%+403

From Prince (the default difficulty) up, the AI also gets free tech and civic boosts (1 at Prince, 5 at Deity; Reading the Screen explains boosts), and from King up it gets extra starting Warriors (basic fighting units). Forum data-miners disagree on whether Prince already has the first +8%/+20% step, so treat Prince as "roughly even".

Source: Difficulty level (Civ6), built from the game files via CivFanatics.

Which difficulty, and why VI

Play your first game on Chieftain, then move to Prince (the default difficulty). Below Prince you get a combat bonus. Damage grows exponentially with the gap in combat strength, so on Settler your +3 and the AI's −1 make a 4-point swing, and your hits do about 17% more damage and theirs about 15% less. A first game is for seeing every system, so survive long enough to reach them.

Climbing the Ladder covers when to move up.

Map, speed and help VI

SettingPick thisWhy
RulesetGathering StormThe full modern game. This book assumes it.
Map typeContinents or PangaeaPangaea is "one massive landmass", so you meet neighbors early and rarely need boats. Continents is "a few large landmasses".
Map sizeSmall6 civs and 9 city-states (minor powers that cannot win) by default. Standard has 8 and 12, which is more to track.
SpeedStandard (500 turns)Every turn number in this book assumes Standard. Faster speeds scale all costs down.
Advisor"New to Civilization"The advisor explains the basics as they come up. Set it in Game Options.

To set this up, choose Single Player, then Create Game, not Play Now (which picks Small Continents, Prince and a random leader). The leader list starts on "?", Random Leader, so pick Trajan, then set the difficulty to Chieftain. For a guided start, try the Tutorial on the main menu.

Do you own Gathering Storm (the second Civ VI expansion)? If you do, Choose Ruleset at the top of the Create Game screen offers "Expansion: Gathering Storm". If it is stuck on Standard Rules, open Additional Content on the main menu and check that the expansions are enabled (Steam community advice). Without it, play anyway. A few numbers differ (base-game Eurekas pay 50%, not 40%), and the book names Gathering Storm where it matters.

Sources: Starting a new game (Civ6); Map (Civ6); Speed (Civ6); official manual, pp. 6–9 and 20–21; Steam discussion on enabling expansions (2024); Boost (Civ6).

Your leader

Each civ is played as one historical leader, and both bring bonuses. This book's default is Trajan of Rome VI. Every city you found starts with a free Monument (a Culture building) and a road to your capital if it is close enough. Screen Rant's list for new players also names Sumer, Scythia, Greece and Russia.

Sources: Screen Rant, "Civilization 6: Best Leaders for New Players" (2020); fandom pages Trajan, Roman.

Leave mods (fan-made add-ons) off for your first game. When you want them, Resources lists the ones worth installing.

Concept lens: research on expert performance finds that skill grows fastest from practice set just beyond your current level, with fast feedback. Too easy and you learn nothing; too hard and you cannot see why you lost. Deliberate practice

Lab: How big is the handicap?

Predict first: at turn 40, how many more points will the best AI lead you by on Prince than on Chieftain?

  1. When you create the game, open Advanced Setup and write down the Map Random Seed, the number that generates the map. Start on Chieftain as Trajan.
  2. Play to turn 40. Note your score and the highest AI score on the World Rankings screen (top right).
  3. Start again with the same seed and leader on Prince. Make the same choices where you can and play to turn 40.

Measure: the gap between your score and the best AI score in each run.

What you should see: a bigger AI lead on Prince with identical play. Only the difficulty bonuses changed, so the extra gap is the handicap.

If your result differs: forum data-miners dispute whether Prince's first +8%/+20% yield step applies, so the AI's edge may be small. Chieftain's +2 combat strength for you and −1 for the AI never shows if you fought no battles. And any different choice makes the two runs unequal.

In the world: a golf handicap or a chess "odds" game works the same way. Change only the handicap and the score gap shows its size. Play a friend at a game you both know, once even and once with one of you giving up a piece.

Check yourself

  1. You play on Settler, where you get +3 combat strength and the AI gets −1. How much harder do your units hit, and how much softer do theirs?

    Show answer

    The two bonuses make a 4-point gap. Damage grows exponentially with the gap, so your hits do about 17% more damage and the AI's about 15% less.

  2. After ten easy wins on Chieftain, your games feel the same and you rarely lose a unit. Which concept explains why you stopped improving, and what should you change?

    Show answer

    Deliberate practice: skill grows fastest just beyond your current level, with fast feedback. Chieftain is now too easy, so move up to Prince, where neither side gets a combat bonus.

  3. One beginner guitarist only replays three songs she knows. Another attempts a piece far too hard and quits. What would deliberate practice suggest to each?

    Show answer

    Pick a piece just past her current level and get fast feedback, such as recording herself. The first is repeating what she knows; the second cannot see why she fails.

In the game: first game on Chieftain, Small Pangaea or Continents, Standard speed, Trajan, advisor on.

In the world: set practice just past your current level, where you can still see why you failed.

Reading the Screen and Taking a Turn

Every turn offers more choices than anyone can weigh. Strong players do not try. They spend their thinking on the few that matter and take a good-enough answer for the rest.

The rule: the screen, piece by piece VI

The top bar. A strip across the top shows what your whole empire earns each turn: Science (pays for technologies), Culture (pays for civics), Faith (founds a religion and buys religious units) and Gold (buys things outright and pays upkeep). It also shows your stock of strategic resources (such as Horses and Iron, which some units need) and the turn number.

Research and civics. A tracker on the left shows the technology and the civic you are working on. Open the full tech tree and civics tree (social ideas) from the buttons at the top left. Most tree items have a boost: do the listed task, such as "kill a unit with a Slinger" (an early ranged unit), and you get 40% of its cost at once. A tech boost is a Eureka, a civic boost an Inspiration.

Government and policies. Your first civic, Code of Laws, unlocks the Government screen and your first government, Chiefdom. A government has slots; you fill them with policy cards, small rules such as Urban Planning (+1 Production in every city). You can swap cards for free whenever you finish a civic. At most other times swapping costs Gold (Governments and Policies lists the exceptions).

Lenses. The Lenses button sits above the mini-map at the bottom left. A lens recolors the map to show one thing. The one you need now is the Settler (the unit that founds a city) lens (hotkey 4), covered in Where to Settle.

The city panel. Click a city's banner to see its yields, growth and Production (the yield that builds units and buildings) list, where you choose units, buildings and districts (specialist zones built on tiles near the city, such as a Campus for Science).

Unit actions. Select a unit and its actions appear at the bottom right.

ActionWhat it does
MoveRight-click a tile. Long moves continue over several turns.
AttackMelee units: move onto the enemy's tile. Hover over the enemy first to see the Combat Preview.
Ranged AttackSlingers and Archers: select the unit, choose Ranged Attack, then click a target in range. The attacker takes no damage back.
Found CitySettler only. Founds a city on its tile and uses up the Settler.
Build ImprovementBuilder only. Stand on the tile and click Farm, Mine or another improvement. Each use spends one of its 3 charges.
Automate ExplorationThe unit, usually a Scout, explores on its own every turn until you cancel.
FortifyMilitary only (most melee and ranged units). The unit digs in for a defense bonus: +3 after one turn, +6 after two or more. It stays put until you select it again.
SleepFor units that cannot fortify (civilians, cavalry, ships). Stays put until you wake it. No defense bonus.
Skip TurnDoes nothing this turn only. It will ask again next turn.

Notifications and End Turn. The large button at the bottom right ends your turn. While something still needs a decision it shows that instead: "Choose Research", "Choose Production", "Unit Needs Orders". Click it to jump to the thing that needs you.

Sources: Lens (Civ6); Government (Civ6); Unit actions (Civ6); Combat (Civ6); Boost (Civ6); official manual, pp. 27, 50, 55, 71–72, 75; Gold (Civ6); Pixel Twelve beginner guide.

How a turn works VI

  1. 1 Read the notifications. Answer anything that blocks the turn: research, civic, production.
  2. 2 Glance at the top bar. Is Gold per turn positive? If not, delete a unit you no longer need (most units cost Gold upkeep; Warriors, Slingers and Scouts are free) or slot God King (+1 Gold in the capital).
  3. 3 Move units. Sleep or fortify idle ones so they stop asking.
  4. 4 If you finished a civic, check your policy cards. Swaps are free right now.
  5. 5 Press End Turn. The AI moves, yields arrive, and the next turn starts.

Decision science

Bounded rationality and satisficing

In 1934–35 Herbert Simon, a student writing a term paper, studied how his home city of Milwaukee ran its playgrounds. The school board and the public works department shared the job. They seemed to agree on the goals, yet argued constantly over how much to spend on upkeep and how much on play supervisors. Simon's economics books said to weigh what one more dollar bought in each. Nobody could measure that. To public works a playground was "a green oasis in the crowded gray city"; to the recreation staff it was a place for children to play with adult guidance. Each side judged by the goal it could see. The puzzle shaped a career that won Simon a Nobel Prize in 1978.

Bounded rationality: real decision-makers have limited information, time and brainpower, so they cannot find the best option. They satisfice instead: set a good-enough bar and take the first option that clears it.

Open in the PDF, page 19

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Each turn offers dozens of choices: builds, research, moves, swaps. Nobody computes the best combination.Limited computing power. A manager with forty decisions a week cannot optimize them all.
Build orders and "always keep one more unit than my neighbor" rules.Heuristics and aspiration levels: a good-enough rule frees attention for the choices that matter.

Where the map breaks

  • Civ shows you exact numbers: yields, turns to finish, a combat preview. Real decision-makers rarely get figures that clean.
  • The game waits for you. You can think about one turn for an hour, and you can reload a save. Real time pressure and irreversibility are what make bounds bite.
  • Aspiration levels move. What counts as good enough on Chieftain will lose on Deity (the hardest difficulty).

Read next

  • Accessible: Simon's Nobel lecture (1978), which tells the Milwaukee story; his 1953 RAND paper, "A Behavioral Model of Rational Choice" (RAND P-365).
  • Primary: Simon 1955, Quarterly Journal of Economics 69(1): 99–118.

Herbert A. Simon, "A Behavioral Model of Rational Choice," Quarterly Journal of Economics 69(1): 99–118 (1955); "satisficing" named in "Rational Choice and the Structure of the Environment," Psychological Review 63(2) (1956)

Lab: Can you play a turn in one minute?

Predict first: which run will have more Production per turn at turn 20, and by how much?

  1. Save on turn 1. Play 20 turns with a 60-second timer per turn. When it rings, press End Turn.
  2. Reload. Play the same 20 turns with no timer.
  3. In the timed run, jot down each decision you skipped or guessed.

Measure: Science, Culture and Production per turn at turn 20 in each run.

What you should see: a small gap, if you had a simple plan (the opening in Your First 60 Turns). Most of the turns that "needed" thought did not change much. The few that did are the ones worth slowing down for.

If your result differs: if the timed run fell far behind, look for units left standing (a Scout that stood still found fewer Tribal Villages), a missed free policy swap after a civic (Urban Planning alone is +1 Production in every city), or no simple plan, so every turn needed fresh thought.

In the world: timed exams often split the same way. Many marks come from questions you can answer quickly, and a few hard ones repay extra minutes. Time yourself on a practice paper and note which questions actually changed your score.

Check yourself

  1. You make 5 Science a turn. You trigger the Eureka for a tech that costs 50 Science before you start it. How many turns does that save?

    Show answer

    A Eureka pays 40% of the cost at once, which is 20 Science. At 5 a turn that saves 4 turns: the tech takes 6 turns instead of 10.

  2. On turn 3 you spend twenty minutes choosing between two nearly identical moves for your Warrior. Which concept says you should stop, and what would you do instead?

    Show answer

    Bounded rationality. Time and attention are limited, so satisfice: set a good-enough bar, such as "heads for unexplored land and stays safe", take the first move that clears it, and save thought for settling.

  3. A family is choosing a dentist from 40 nearby options. How would a satisficer choose, and when is a longer search worth it?

    Show answer

    Set a bar, such as close, open on Saturdays and well reviewed, and take the first that clears it. Search longer when the choice is hard to reverse, the way founding a city is.

In the game: clear the blockers, move units, check policies after civics and end turn, saving deep thought for settling, war and wonders.

In the world: decide what "good enough" means before you start searching, then stop when you find it.

Where to Settle

Your Settler (the unit that founds a city) can found a city on almost any open tile, and the choice is permanent. One step can mean 5 Housing (a city's soft population cap) or 2. Scholars still argue over how much the ground under a society decides its fate.

The rule: what makes a good city site VI

Turn 25. Your second Settler stands on a dry plain, and the next tile touches a river. Found here and growth slows from size 1. Step to the river first and it slows only at size 4.

The map is made of six-sided tiles, or hexes. A city works tiles up to 3 hexes from its center, so judge the neighborhood. Check these in order.

1. Water, which sets Housing. Housing is how many people a city can hold before growth slows. Your water source sets the starting value.

City center is next toHousing
Fresh water: River, Lake or Oasis5
Coast (no fresh water)3
Neither2

Growth runs at full speed with 2 or more spare Housing, half speed with 1 spare, and quarter speed once Population (the city's number of citizens, who work its tiles) reaches Housing. So a dry city grows at half speed from its first citizen, unless it is your capital (its Palace adds 1 Housing). Housing and Amenities covers how to add more.

2. Coast. A city on the coast can build ships. A city within 2 hexes of Coast or Lake can build a Harbor (a sea district) once that water is inside its borders. Founding a coastal city also gives the Eureka (a 40% research discount for a related task) for Sailing.

3. Hills. A city on Hills gets a defensive bonus, and units defending on Hills get +3 combat strength. A city center is raised to at least 2 Food and 1 Production (the yield that builds units and buildings); Plains Hills gives 2 Food and 2 Production, the best center you can get without a resource.

4. Resources in reach. A luxury resource (Wine, Silk and so on) gives +1 Amenity, a measure of contentment, to up to 4 of your cities once improved. A second copy of the same luxury adds nothing, so it is best traded away. Strategic resources (Horses, Iron, later Coal and Oil) add to your stockpile every turn in Gathering Storm (the second Civ VI expansion), and many appear only after you research the matching tech.

5. Distance. A new city center must be at least 4 hexes from any other city center, leaving 3 tiles between them. If water separates the two, the minimum drops to 3 hexes.

Settling removes Woods, Rainforest or Marsh from the tile with no bonus yield.

Sources: City (Civ6); Housing (Civ6); Amenities (Civ6); Combat (Civ6); Sailing (Civ6).

The Settler lens VI

It turns on when you select a Settler; press 4 to show it any time. Dark green tiles have fresh water (5 Housing), light green are coastal (3), gray have neither (2), and red cannot be settled.

Source: Lens (Civ6).

Geopolitics

Geography and development

Yali, a politician in New Guinea, once asked the American writer Jared Diamond why white people had developed so much "cargo", the local word for manufactured goods, and brought it to New Guinea, while his own people had so little. Neither man thought Europeans were born superior. Diamond's answer, in a 1997 book, was the ground itself. Eurasia had more wild plants and animals that could be farmed, and its long east–west axis let crops and herds spread through lands with similar seasons. The Americas and Africa run north–south, where a crop from one latitude struggled at the next. Farming brought more people, bigger states, better tools and, from life beside livestock, deadlier germs.

Geography and development: the theory that a region's plants, animals, continental shape and diseases, more than its people, explain why some societies built states and conquered others. Critics give institutions and colonial history more weight.

Open in the PDF, page 22

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
A river start gives 5 Housing; a dry start gives 2, which cuts growth to half and then a quarter within a few citizens.Early farming societies clustered on rivers: the Nile, the Tigris and Euphrates, the Indus, the Yellow River.

Where the map breaks

  • Civ's map generator gives every civ a playable start, and you can tell it to balance them. Real geography made no such promise.
  • Diamond's scale is continents over 13,000 years. Civ's is a city over a few hundred turns.
  • Civ VI has no disease. The single biggest factor in Diamond's account of the conquest of the Americas does not exist in the game.
  • Your settlers walk onto empty land. Real settlers almost never did. The Six Assumptions takes this up.

Read next

  • Accessible: Diamond, Guns, Germs, and Steel (1997), which is also the primary source.
  • Primary, the institutions case: Acemoglu, Johnson & Robinson, "The Colonial Origins of Comparative Development," American Economic Review 91(5): 1369–1401 (2001).

Jared Diamond, Guns, Germs, and Steel: The Fates of Human Societies (1997); critics J. M. Blaut, Eight Eurocentric Historians (2000), and Acemoglu, Johnson & Robinson, "The Colonial Origins of Comparative Development," American Economic Review 91(5) (2001)

The hard question: is "empty land" ever empty?

In Civ, unclaimed land is free. At noon on April 22, 1889, about 50,000 people raced into Indian Territory to claim nearly two million acres. The Creek and Seminole had owned it until 1866, when the United States made them cede it as a penalty for their treaties with the Confederacy. No tribe was assigned it after that. The Creek got 30 cents an acre, for land their treaty said would house other Indians and freedmen.

The case for the run: the land had been sold by treaty and left vacant for 23 years, and in 1889 the Creek were paid over $2.2 million more to give up their remaining claims; the Seminole made a similar deal. Many who raced in were poor farmers hoping to earn 160 acres under the Homestead Act.

The case against: a cession forced on the losers of a war is a sale under pressure. Some Creek had fought for the Union, and their nation lost half its land anyway. Land meant for other Indians and freedmen went to non-Indian settlers. A map shows land as empty only after its owners have been made to sign it away.

Your call: if land was taken under pressure a generation ago, does a family that settles it in good faith owe anything for how it was taken?

Lab: How much is a river worth?

Predict first: 30 turns after founding, how many more citizens will the river city have than the dry one?

  1. Around turn 20, with a Settler ready, find two legal sites: one dark green on the Settler lens, one gray.
  2. Save. Found on the dark green site. Play 30 turns with the same build in the new city (Warrior, then Builder, then Monument).
  3. Reload. Found on the gray site and repeat.

Measure: the new city's Population and Production per turn 30 turns after founding.

What you should see: the river city is larger. The dry city drops to quarter-speed growth once it reaches 2 Population. Then ask how much of the gap you could close with play (a Farm, or a Granary for +2 Housing) and how much is the site itself.

If your result differs: if the gap is small, check the dry site's color again. Light green is coastal, with 3 Housing, not 2. Or neither city had much spare Food: each citizen eats 2 Food a turn, so both grew slowly and the Housing brake barely mattered.

In the world: open a map of any country and count how many of its largest cities sit on a river or coast. Then ask the lesson's second question: which grew because of later choices, such as a port, a railway or a capital placed there?

Check yourself

  1. You can found a city beside a river or on a dry inland site. Neither will be your capital. At Population 3, how fast does each grow?

    Show answer

    River: 5 Housing minus 3 citizens leaves 2 spare, so full speed. Dry: Population 3 is already past its 2 Housing, so quarter speed.

  2. Your rival started on a river with Plains Hills; you started dry and flat. At turn 50 they lead. Which concept frames this, and what would its critics want you to check?

    Show answer

    Geography and development: the starting endowment shaped the lead. Critics such as Acemoglu, Johnson and Robinson stress institutions and choices, so check what each of you built, and when, before blaming the land.

  3. Two towns were founded the same year, one on a navigable river, one inland and dry. A century later the river town is far larger. What two explanations should you test?

    Show answer

    Geography (water, transport, farmland) and choices or institutions (who governed, what was built). The lesson's warning applies: be wary of any story that uses just one of the two.

In the game: dark green first, then coast and hills, then a new luxury, with 3 tiles between it and your other cities.

In the world: geography sets the starting hand and choices decide how it is played, so be wary of any story that explains everything with just one of the two.

Your First 60 Turns

Found your second city 20 turns earlier and it outproduces its later twin all game, and helps pay for the next. The first 60 turns are where small choices compound.

The rule: the early pieces VI

You start with one Settler (the unit that founds a city) and one Warrior. Everything else you build.

ItemProductionWhat it is for
Scout303 moves. Finds Tribal Villages (a free reward for the first unit in), city-states and neighbors.
Slinger35Cheap ranged unit (ranged strength 15). Killing a unit with it gives the Archery Eureka.
Warrior40Basic melee unit (strength 20).
Builder50Builds Farms, Mines and other improvements, one per charge, 3 charges. Civ VI has no Worker unit.
Monument60+1 Culture.
Settler80Founds a city. Needs Population 2 and costs 1. Each one raises the next one's cost.

First civics. Code of Laws (cost 20) gives you Chiefdom, with one Military and one Economic policy slot. Good first cards are Urban Planning (+1 Production in every city) or God King (+1 Faith, +1 Gold in the capital), plus Discipline (+5 combat strength against barbarians). Craftsmanship's Inspiration (a 40% civic discount for a related task) is "improve 3 tiles", and it unlocks Ilkum (Babylon's own Builder) (+30% Production toward Builders). Early Empire's Inspiration is "6 total Population", and it unlocks Colonization (+50% Production toward Settlers).

Useful early Eurekas (40% research discounts for related tasks). Writing (a technology): meet another civ. Bronze Working: kill 3 barbarians. Irrigation: farm a bonus resource. Astrology: find a natural wonder.

Barbarians (hostile raiders from camps). Barbarians are raiders who belong to no civ. Their camps send out a scout. If it finds your city and gets home, the camp sends 3 to 6 raiders. Kill barbarian scouts on sight.

Pantheon (an early faith bonus). 25 Faith (the yield spent on religion) founds a pantheon, a small permanent bonus (see Religion).

Sources: fandom pages Settler, Scout, Slinger, Builder, Monument, Code of Laws, Craftsmanship, Early Empire, Barbarian, Pantheon, Era.

The opening VI

There is no single correct opener. Community consensus (CivFanatics) is some order of Scout (a fast unit for exploring), Slinger (an early ranged unit), Settler (the unit that founds a city), with a Builder (a unit that builds farms, mines and the like) when you have good tiles to improve. Below is a sound default for Chieftain or Prince (the default difficulty), with rough turn numbers.

  1. T1 Found your capital where your Settler stands, unless a dark green tile is one move away. Set production to Scout and your civic to Code of Laws. For research, hover over nearby tiles to see which resources they hold. Pick Animal Husbandry (a technology) for Sheep or Cattle (its Pastures improve them), Mining for Stone or hills, and Pottery if the capital has no fresh water (it unlocks the Granary, +2 Housing). If unsure, pick Mining: Mines work on any bare hill, and Mining lets Builders chop (clearing Woods for a lump of Production) woods for Production (the yield that builds units and buildings).
  2. T1–10 Scout the unknown and pick up every Tribal Village (a hut with a free reward for the first unit in). Keep the Warrior near home.
  3. T5–15 Build a Slinger. Finish Code of Laws and slot the cards above.

    Concept lens: every turn spent on a unit is a turn not spent on a Settler. That trade-off is opportunity cost. Opportunity cost

  4. T10–25 Build your first Settler as soon as the capital reaches Population 2. Escort it if barbarians are near. Put a Warrior on its tile and choose Create Escort Formation; a Settler caught alone can be captured. A Builder before or after is fine if you have bonus resources to farm or mine.

    Concept lens: a city founded on turn 20 outproduces one founded on turn 40 for the rest of the game, and pays for more cities sooner. Compounding

  5. T15–30 Head the civics tree toward Craftsmanship (improve 3 tiles for its Inspiration) and Early Empire. If a barbarian scout appears, kill it with the Slinger and Warrior before it gets home.

    Concept lens: barbarians are raiders with no stake in your land, which is why clearing their camps pays. Stationary vs roving bandit

  6. T20–35 Found city 2 on the best dark green site within a few tiles. Train Settlers in the capital whenever it reaches Population 2 or more, and use Builders or Monuments to fill the gaps while it regrows. When Early Empire finishes, slot Colonization.
  7. T35–50 Found cities 3 and 4. Research Writing for your first Campus (a science district) and Astrology for a Holy Site (a Faith district) if you want a religion. A city can hold 1 specialty district at Population 1 and another at 4, so do not expect many yet. Skip wonders (one-of-a-kind buildings) until your third city; Wonders and Great People explains why.

    Concept lens: the first district (a specialized city tile that holds buildings) you place locks in where later districts get their adjacency (extra yield from what sits next door) bonuses, the extra yield a district gets from its neighbors. Path dependence

  8. T45–60 Head for Political Philosophy (a step on the culture tree) (Inspiration: meet 3 city-states). It unlocks the first full governments: Classical Republic, Oligarchy and Autocracy. If a neighbor's army is growing, add a unit or two of your own.

    Concept lens: your neighbors are watching your army and you are watching theirs. Too few units invites attack; too many alarms them. Security dilemma

Sources: CivFanatics, "What is your opener and why?"; District (Civ6); Political Philosophy (Civ6); fandom pages Animal Husbandry, Mining, Pottery, Mine, Pasture; Pixel Twelve (tile tooltips); official manual p. 50 (escorts).

Pop-ups you'll see in the first 60 turns VI

  • Choose a pantheon (at 25 Faith). Pick Religious Settlements if it is offered. In Gathering Storm (the second Civ VI expansion) it gives a free Settler, and faster border growth helps every plan.
  • Meeting a leader. You may greet them politely without revealing anything. If they offer a delegation, accept it: you get 25 Gold.
  • A Eureka (a 40% research discount for a related task) or Inspiration (a 40% civic discount for a related task). Nothing to answer. 40% of that tech or civic is already paid.
  • An envoy to place. Send it to a city-state (a minor power that cannot win) you have met. One envoy gives your capital a small bonus, such as +2 Science from a scientific city-state.

Sources: Religious Settlements (Civ6); Diplomacy (Civ6); Envoy (Civ6).

Checkpoints VI

Rough targets from experienced players on CivFanatics. A first game that misses them is still a good first game.

By aboutRough target
Turn 503–4 cities founded
Turn 50Political Philosophy civic finished
Turn 1004–5 cities and a Tier 1 government, one of those three (a lower bar)

Source: CivFanatics, "Benchmarks" (posts by greenOak, Victoria, XVeris).

Systems

Compounding

In 1785 Charles-Joseph Mathon de la Cour, a French mathematician who admired Benjamin Franklin, wrote a friendly parody of Franklin's almanac. In it a man leaves five small sums in his will to earn interest for one to five centuries, then pay for grand, impossible projects. Franklin, then 79, thanked him for the idea and did it for real. He left £1,000 to each of Boston and Philadelphia, to be lent out at interest for 200 years. By 1990 Boston's share, worth about $4,400 at the start, had grown to almost $5 million.

Compounding: growth that is added to the base, so each period grows from a bigger base than the last. Steady growth of r percent doubles a quantity about every 70 ÷ r periods, so early gains matter most.

Open in the PDF, page 27

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
A second city on turn 25 produces for every remaining turn and helps pay for cities 3 and 4.Reinvested returns: a firm's profits buy the machines that make next year's profits.
Each citizen eats 2 Food a turn but also works a tile, so early growth feeds later growth.A growing workforce raises output, which supports a larger workforce.

Where the map breaks

  • Civ growth is not a steady percentage. Food thresholds rise with each citizen and Housing (a city's soft population cap) caps growth, so the curves bend into an S-shape.
  • Compounding cuts both ways. In Civ, lost cities and war weariness (unhappiness that grows the longer you fight) (Amenities lost to fighting) snowball too.
  • The game ends. A slow plan with a big late payoff can still win if the payoff lands in time.

Read next

  • Accessible: Donella Meadows, Thinking in Systems (2008), on reinforcing loops.
  • Primary: Bartlett, "Forgotten Fundamentals of the Energy Crisis" (1978).

No single originator. Standard teaching treatment: Albert A. Bartlett, "Forgotten Fundamentals of the Energy Crisis," American Journal of Physics 46(9): 876–888 (1978)

Systems / Economics

Path dependence

In the 1830s Isambard Kingdom Brunel set the Great Western Railway's rails 7 feet ¼ inch apart, a broad gauge he found better for speed and comfort. Most other lines used 4 feet 8½ inches, so where they met, as at Gloucester, goods had to be moved from one train to the other. A Royal Commission of 1845 agreed that Brunel's gauge was technically superior, then backed the narrow one anyway: most track already used it, and converting that would cost too much. From 1846 most new lines had to be narrow, and in May 1892 the Great Western converted the last of its own. The better gauge lost to the one with more track.

Path dependence: when each new adopter makes an option more attractive to the next, small early events can lock a system onto one path, and leaving it later costs too much, even when another path was better.

Open in the PDF, page 28

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
You found your capital on turn 1 with almost no map revealed, and it can never move.Early, poorly informed choices (a port, a capital, a standard) become fixed.
Districts cannot be moved, and each one gives +0.5 adjacency to districts placed next to it later.Increasing returns: each new firm in a cluster makes the cluster more attractive to the next.

Where the map breaks

  • You can reload a save. History cannot.
  • Most of Civ's lock-in comes from explicit rules (a district cannot move). Arthur and David studied lock-in that emerges from millions of separate choices.
  • Whether QWERTY was really worse than its rivals is disputed among economists. The theory is stronger than its most famous example.

Read next

  • Accessible: David, "Clio and the Economics of QWERTY" (1985), six readable pages.
  • Primary: Arthur, "Competing Technologies, Increasing Returns, and Lock-In by Historical Events" (1989).

W. Brian Arthur, "Competing Technologies, Increasing Returns, and Lock-In by Historical Events," The Economic Journal 99(394): 116–131 (1989); Paul A. David, "Clio and the Economics of QWERTY," American Economic Review 75(2): 332–337 (1985)

Lab: Settler first or Settler fourth?

Predict first: will Run A's lead in total yields per turn be bigger at turn 60 than at turn 30, smaller, or the same?

  1. Start a Chieftain game, note the Map Random Seed, and save on turn 1.
  2. Run A: Settler as the first thing you build once the capital reaches Population 2. Then Scout, Slinger, Builder.
  3. Run B: reload. Scout, Slinger, Builder, then Settler.
  4. Otherwise play the two runs the same way.

Measure: city count and total Food + Production + Science + Gold per turn at turn 30 and again at turn 60.

What you should see: a bigger gap at turn 60 than at turn 30. The early Settler bought more than one city's worth of output. Then check what Run A gave up for it.

If your result differs: if the gap shrank, barbarians may have hit Run A while its Slinger came late. The capital also lost a citizen to the Settler (each costs 1 Population) and regrew slowly. Or city 2 landed on a dry site with 2 Housing, so it grew at half speed or slower.

In the world: compare two savers. One sets aside a little from the first paycheck; the other waits five years, then saves more each month. A free online compound-interest calculator shows how long the late saver needs to catch up.

Check yourself

  1. A Settler costs 80 Production and your capital makes 8 a turn. How many turns does it take, and how many with Colonization slotted?

    Show answer

    10 turns at 8 a turn. Colonization adds 50% Production toward Settlers, making 12 a turn, and 80 ÷ 12 is about 6.7, so it finishes in 7 turns.

  2. Your first Campus (the science district) went on a poor spot, and later districts keep clustering beside it for adjacency (extra yield from what sits next door). Which concept is this, and how does Civ's version differ from the real one?

    Show answer

    Path dependence: an early choice locks in later ones. In Civ the lock-in is an explicit rule, since districts cannot move. Arthur and David studied lock-in that emerges from millions of separate choices.

  3. A neighborhood savings club earns 7% a year and reinvests it. Roughly how long until its money doubles, and why should members join early?

    Show answer

    About 10 years, by the rule of 70 (70 ÷ 7). Money added early has more doublings ahead of it, so it ends larger than the same sum added later.

In the game: Scout, Slinger, Settler; a new city every time the capital can spare a citizen; three or four cities by turn 60.

In the world: start early on anything that compounds, and treat your first few hard-to-reverse choices with the most care.

Part 2

Building Your Empire

A Civ VI city grows, builds and researches by simple rules. Each has a twin in the real economy, with a true story behind it.

Tiles and Growth

Every citizen works one tile, so every tile you pick is one you passed up. A president once priced a bomber in schools.

The rule VI

The map is made of hexagonal tiles, and each produces yields, the resources the game counts: Food, Production (the yield that builds units and buildings), Gold, Science, Culture and Faith (the yield spent on religion). A tile's yield stacks three layers: base terrain, any feature (Woods, Rainforest, Marsh), and any resource or improvement.

Base terrainFoodProductionGold
Grassland200
Plains110
Tundra100
Desert, Snow000
Coast, Lake101
Ocean100
Any Hills versionsame as the flat tile, plus 1 Production
Woods / Rainforest / Marsh+1 Production / +1 Food / +1 Food
Mountainscannot be worked

A citizen is one point of a city's population. Each citizen eats 2 Food every turn and works one tile inside the city's borders, up to three tiles from the city. The City Center (the tile the city was founded on) is worked free on top of that, and the game raises it to at least 2 Food and 1 Production. A city of size 3 therefore works four tiles and eats 6 Food.

Whatever Food is left after eating goes into the city's food basket. When the basket fills, the city gains a citizen and the basket empties. The basket gets bigger every time: a size-1 city needs 15 Food to grow, a size-10 city needs 114, a size-30 city needs 403. If a city eats more than it makes, the basket drains, and when it is empty a citizen starves.

The game assigns tiles automatically; Manage Citizens in the city panel lets you lock a citizen onto a tile. Improvements raise a tile's yield: a Farm adds 1 Food and a Mine on Hills adds 1 Production, and both get better with later techs and civics.

Sources: List of terrains in Civ6; Population (Civ6); Farm (Civ6); Mine (Civ6).

Growing a city from size 1 to 10 takes 504 Food in total; from 10 to 20 takes 1,731, more than three times as much.

A worked example VI

Your size-3 city sits on a Plains Hill next to a river. The City Center (the tile the city was founded on) gives 2 Food and 2 Production (the yield that builds units and buildings). Your three citizens work a farmed Grassland (3 Food), a plain Grassland (2 Food) and a mined Plains Hill (1 Food, 3 Production). Total Food is 8. The citizens eat 6, so 2 goes in the basket. The city needs 33 Food to reach size 4, so it grows in 17 turns.

Move the miner onto a second plain Grassland. Food becomes 9, the surplus becomes 3, and the city now grows in 11 turns. You paid for those six turns with 3 Production every turn. Neither choice is always right.

Economics

Opportunity cost

In April 1953 President Dwight Eisenhower, who as a general had led the D-Day invasion, gave a speech on the arms race. He priced a bomber in schools: "The cost of one modern heavy bomber is this: a modern brick school in more than 30 cities." It was also two hospitals, he said, or some 50 miles of highway. That was its real price.

Opportunity cost: the value of the best alternative you give up when you choose, even when no money changes hands.

Open in the PDF, page 31

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
A citizen on a 2-Food tile is not on a 2-Production tileAn hour studying is an hour not earning
A Builder charge on a Mine is a Farm not builtA bomber bought is a school not built

Where the map breaks

  • Civ prices most choices in Production. Eisenhower weighed bombers against schools, with no exchange rate.
  • Civ shows every option on one screen. In life the best alternative often goes unnoticed.

Read next

  • Accessible: Timothy Taylor, "Dissecting the Concept of Opportunity Cost," Conversable Economist (2016).

David I. Green, "Pain-Cost and Opportunity-Cost," Quarterly Journal of Economics 8(2) (1894).

Economics

Marginal analysis

At Christmas 1894 Samuel Insull, head of the money-losing Chicago Edison, visited Brighton in England. To his surprise, shops were shut but every light was on. Brighton's power company charged less at quiet hours: its plant was built for the busiest hour, so an extra unit off-peak cost it little. By 1897 Insull offered Chicago two-tier rates, and many homeowners' bills fell 32% within a year.

Marginal analysis: judge one more unit by its own extra benefit and extra cost, ignoring averages and totals.

Open in the PDF, page 32

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Should the next citizen work Food or Production?Should a firm hire one more worker?
Amenity need is population ÷ 2, rounded up, so only some new citizens cost oneCosts that jump at thresholds, such as a second shift

Where the map breaks

  • Civ's margins are whole numbers: the next unit often adds exactly 0 or 1.
  • For all-or-nothing rewards, like a wonder or a boost, compare totals.

Read next

  • Accessible: Jevons (1871), chapter III, free scan.

William Stanley Jevons, The Theory of Political Economy (1871); Carl Menger, Grundsätze der Volkswirthschaftslehre (1871).

Systems

Stocks, flows and feedback loops

For 500 years Newfoundland lived on cod. From the 1950s new trawlers fished wider and deeper, and in 1968 the catch peaked at 810,000 tons. The catch, a flow, was easy to count. The fish left in the sea, the stock, were not. In 1992 the minister, John Crosbie, closed the fishery: the northern cod were down to 1% of historic levels, and about 37,000 people lost their jobs.

Stocks and flows: a stock is what has built up, like water in a bath; flows fill or drain it. Feedback loops link the two, amplifying or stabilizing.

Open in the PDF, page 33

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
The food basket is a stock; surplus Food per turn is its inflowA savings account and its deposits
Housing slows the inflow near the cap (balancing)A thermostat

Where the map breaks

  • Civ shows every stock. The 1992 cod quota was 187,969 tonnes; fishers had caught only 129,033 the year before.
  • Stocks refill slowly. Commercial cod fishing reopened only in 2024.

Read next

  • Accessible: Meadows (2008).
  • Primary: Forrester (1961).

Donella H. Meadows, Thinking in Systems: A Primer (2008). Earlier: Jay W. Forrester, Industrial Dynamics (1961).

Concept lens: More citizens make more Food, which makes more citizens, so an early lead snowballs. Compounding has its full lesson in Part 1.

Lab: Food first or Production first?

Predict first: on turn 40, which run will have the bigger capital, and which will have built more units?

  1. Start a game on Prince (the default difficulty), Standard speed. Found your capital and save on turn 1.
  2. Open the city screen, press Manage Citizens and lock every citizen onto the highest-Food tiles. Play to turn 40 building only Warriors, Builders (units that build farms, mines and the like) and Settlers (the units that found cities).
  3. Reload. This time lock citizens onto the highest-Production tiles and play to turn 40 with the same build order.

Measure: on turn 40 in each run, write down the capital's size, how many units you have built, and how many cities you have.

What you should see: the Food run has the bigger capital, the Production run finishes items sooner. Settlers need both (a Settler costs Production and uses up a citizen), so a balanced run often founds more cities. The next point of Food is worth most when the city is short of Food.

If your result differs: the Food run may have run short of Housing (a city's soft population cap), so its extra Food counted only 50% or 25% (see Housing). Each Settler used up a citizen, so the run that trained more Settlers shows a smaller capital. Both teach one thing: extra Food is worth only the growth the city can turn it into.

In the world: look back at your own week. An extra hour of paid work and an extra hour of study buy different things, and the fifth hour of either usually buys less than the first. Write down what your last hour of each bought.

Check yourself

  1. Your size-2 city needs 24 Food to grow and makes 7 Food a turn. Ignoring Housing, how many turns until size 3? What if one citizen moves to a tile with 1 less Food?

    Show answer

    Two citizens eat 4, leaving 3. Size 2 needs 24 Food, so 8 turns. With 1 less Food the surplus is 2, so 12 turns. One Food a turn cost four turns of growth.

  2. A size-7 city needs 4 Amenities (city contentment from luxuries and entertainment). Does its 8th citizen need another one? Which concept says to judge that one step rather than the average?

    Show answer

    No. Need is population ÷ 2, rounded up, so size 8 still needs 4, while the 7th citizen added one. Marginal analysis judges each next unit by its own extra cost, and Civ's costs jump at thresholds.

  3. A student can work a Saturday shift that pays 60 or study for an exam. She studies. What did studying cost her?

    Show answer

    The 60 she did not earn, plus anything else the shift would have given her. Opportunity cost is the best alternative given up, even when no money leaves your pocket.

In the game: before you move a citizen, ask what the city loses. That loss is the price of where they stand.

In the world: the cost of anything is the best thing you gave up for it, and the next unit matters more than the total.

Housing and Amenities

Your city has Food to spare and still stops growing. Two invisible brakes are at work, and the same kind of brake held most of humanity near hunger until about 1800.

Housing VI

Turn 40. Your second city sits on a river and has been growing every few turns. At size 4 it slows. At size 5 it crawls. Nothing is starving, and the Food is still on the tiles. So where did it go?

It hit the Housing (a city's soft population cap) brake. Housing is the number of citizens a city has room for. Food decides whether a city can grow. Housing decides how much of that Food counts.

Housing minus populationShare of surplus Food that counts toward growth
2 or more spare100%
1 spare50%
0 to 4 over25%
5 or more over0%

So a city can never grow past its Housing plus 5, and it slows to a crawl long before that. Losing Housing never kills citizens. It only slows or stops growth.

Where Housing comes from:

  • Water at the City Center (the tile the city was founded on). Fresh water (a river, lake or oasis next to the center) gives 5. Coast without fresh water gives 3. Anywhere else gives 2, so a dry city other than the capital is braked from size 1.
  • Buildings. A Granary (Pottery) gives +2. A Sewer gives +2 later. The Palace gives +1 in your capital.
  • The Aqueduct (a district that brings water and Housing). A district is a city tile with one job. The Aqueduct adds +2 to a fresh-water city, or raises any other city's water Housing to 6. It must touch the City Center and a river, lake, oasis or Mountain.
  • Improvements. Each Farm, Pasture, Plantation, Camp or Fishing Boats gives +0.5.
  • Later. Neighborhoods (districts that add Housing) (+2 to +6, by the tile's Appeal, a score for how attractive its surroundings are), the Dam (+3), policy cards and wonders.

That is what happened to your river city. Its water gives 5 Housing. At size 3 it has 2 spare and grows at full speed. At size 4 its surplus counts half. At size 5 it counts a quarter. A Granary built before size 4 keeps it at full speed for two more citizens.

Source: Housing (Civ6).

Amenities VI

Housing decides how many people a city can hold. Amenities (city contentment from luxuries and entertainment) decide whether they are glad to be there. Each city needs one Amenity for every two citizens, rounded up: a size-7 city needs 4.

Amenities vs needLevelEffect (Gathering Storm)
5 or more aboveEcstatic+20% all yields
3 to 4 aboveHappy+10% all yields
0 to 2 aboveContentno effect
1 to 2 belowDispleased−10% non-Food yields, −15% growth
3 to 4 belowUnhappy−20% non-Food yields, −30% growth
5 to 6 belowUnrest−30% non-Food yields, rebels appear
7 or more belowRevolt−40% non-Food yields, more rebels

Where Amenities come from:

  • Luxury resources. Improve a luxury (Silk, Wine, Whales and so on) and it gives +1 Amenity to up to 4 of your cities. A second copy of the same luxury adds nothing, so trade spares to other civs for ones you lack.
  • Entertainment. The Entertainment Complex district (a specialized city tile that holds buildings) gives +1, and its Arena gives +2 more. Its later Zoo and Stadium reach every City Center within 6 tiles.
  • Later: policies, governments, beliefs, wonders and National Parks.

Source: Amenities (Civ6).

The City Details panel lists every Housing and Amenity source a city has. Check it first when a city slows.

Economics

Diminishing marginal returns

Fred Brooks ran the team that built the software for IBM's System/360 computers in the 1960s. When the project ran late, the obvious fix was more programmers. It made things worse. Each newcomer had to be trained by someone who was already busy, and every extra person added more conversations to keep everyone in step. In 1975 Brooks wrote the rule down: adding people to a late software project makes it later.

Diminishing returns: keep adding one input while the others stay fixed, and each extra unit eventually adds less than the one before.

Open in the PDF, page 36

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Citizens take the best tiles first, so each new citizen adds less surplus than the lastExtra workers on a fixed field
Housing counts 100%, then 50%, then 25%, then 0% of surplus FoodProgrammers added to a project whose design and deadline are fixed

Where the map breaks

  • In Civ the returns fall because the map has only so many good tiles. Brooks's team slowed because people have to talk to each other, and Civ citizens never do.
  • Lumpy jumps, like a new district slot every three citizens, reverse the trend for a while.
  • Growing every input at once (more cities, not bigger ones) does not diminish in Civ. That is one reason wide beats tall (community consensus).

Read next

  • Accessible: Frederick P. Brooks, The Mythical Man-Month (1975), chapter 2. Stanley L. Brue, "Retrospectives: The Law of Diminishing Returns," Journal of Economic Perspectives (1993).
  • Primary: Turgot (1767); David Ricardo, On the Principles of Political Economy and Taxation (1817).

First stated by Anne-Robert-Jacques Turgot (1767). Applied to land and rent in 1815 by Malthus, Ricardo, West and Torrens.

Economics / history

The Malthusian trap

In 1348 the Black Death reached England and killed perhaps half its people. For the survivors, life got better. Workers were scarce, so landlords had to compete for them. Parliament tried to freeze wages at their old level by law in 1351, and the law failed: farm wages roughly doubled over the next century. Then the population recovered, and wages fell again. The plague had brought no new technology. It had made each worker rarer.

The Malthusian trap: when population grows faster than food, any gain in living standards is eaten by more mouths, until people are back to bare survival. It held until technology broke the link.

Open in the PDF, page 37

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Food surplus turns into citizens, and each citizen eats 2 FoodBetter harvests turning into more children
A city at its Housing cap keeps making Food that buys no growthA pre-industrial society at the limit of its land
A Granary or Aqueduct lifts the capNew farming methods and, after about 1800, sustained technical change

Where the map breaks

  • Malthus wrote about income per head. Civ has no wages, so its trap is only a growth stall.
  • In Civ you escape the trap with one building. Humanity needed centuries and an industrial revolution.

Read next

  • Accessible: Gregory Clark, A Farewell to Alms (2007), chapters 1 to 3.
  • Primary: Malthus (1798), free at Econlib.

Thomas Robert Malthus, An Essay on the Principle of Population (1798).

The hard question: when a theory says suffering is natural, do you still act?

In the game a city at its cap is a number to fix. In Ireland in 1845 a blight destroyed the potato, the main food of the poor. About a million people died and over a million emigrated. Food still left the country for Britain, although at the worst of the famine four times as much wheat came in as went out. Charles Trevelyan, the Treasury official who ran much of the relief, wrote in 1848 that the famine was "a direct stroke of an all-wise and all-merciful Providence".

The case for holding back: officials feared that free food would ruin the private grain trade, teach people to depend on handouts and leave the country weaker for the next bad year. Most economists of the day believed markets fed people better than governments did.

The case against: markets feed people who have money, and the starving had none. People were dying that winter, and the long-run benefits were a guess. A theory that asks other people to suffer costs its believers nothing.

Your call: when a theory tells you that suffering is necessary, how much proof should you demand before you act on it?

Lab: Watch the Housing brake bite

Predict first: write down how many turns the city will take to grow at each size, from 2 spare Housing down to 0, and then after the Granary.

  1. Pick a city with 2 spare Housing and a steady Food surplus.
  2. Each time it grows, note its surplus and its "turns to grow", until it reaches its Housing number.
  3. Now build a Granary (+2 Housing) and watch the number again.

Measure: turns to grow at each size, next to the Food surplus shown.

What you should see: the surplus stays roughly flat while turns to grow jumps, first because the basket is bigger and then because Housing cuts what counts to 50% and 25%. Extra Food on the tiles barely helps. Extra Housing helps at once.

If your result differs: something else moved the surplus. A newly worked tile changes the Food, and a city that drops to Displeased grows 15% slower. Farms and other improvements add +0.5 Housing each, so two new ones delay the brake by a citizen. City Details lists all of these, so check it first.

In the world: watch a busy café at lunch. Once every seat is taken, a faster kitchen serves barely more people, and more seats would serve many more. A business, like a city, grows only as fast as its tightest limit allows.

Do

  • Settle on fresh water when you can, for 5 Housing from turn 1.
  • Build a Granary before a city's population reaches Housing minus 1.
  • Improve each new luxury type as soon as you can, and trade duplicates.

Avoid

  • Piling Food into a city that is already over its Housing.
  • Letting a city sit Displeased. The penalty hits every yield except Food.

Check yourself

  1. Your size-5 city has 5 Housing in total and +4 surplus Food. How much Food a turn counts toward growth, and what does a Granary change?

    Show answer

    Housing minus population is 0, so 25% counts: 1 Food a turn. A Granary adds 2 Housing, leaving 2 spare, so the whole surplus counts again.

  2. A city sits at its Housing (a city's soft population cap) cap. You move citizens onto higher-Food tiles, and Food rises but growth barely moves. Which concept is this, and what was the real-world escape?

    Show answer

    The Malthusian trap, which Civ reduces to a growth stall: at the cap, extra Food buys little growth. A Granary or Aqueduct lifts it at once. The real escape was new farming methods and, after about 1800, technical change.
