  3. A café has 20 seats and is full every lunchtime. The owner hires a third cook. Why might lunch takings barely rise, and what should she check first?

    Show answer

    Seats are the fixed input, like Housing. Extra cooks add less and less once seats bind, which is diminishing returns. Find the binding limit first: more seats or faster table turnover may pay more than a cook.

In the game: when a city slows, find the binding limit first (Food, Housing or Amenities), then fix that one.

In the world: adding more of one input gives less and less when another input is fixed, so find the constraint before you spend.

Builders and Chopping

A forest is a pile of Production (the yield that builds units and buildings) now or a trickle each turn you work it. A forester priced that trade in 1849.

Builders and improvements VI

Civ VI has no Workers; its improvement unit is the Builder (a unit that builds farms, mines and the like). A Builder has 3 build charges. Building an improvement is instant and uses one charge. Repairing a pillaged improvement or removing an improvement is free. When the last charge is used, the Builder disappears.

Ways to get more charges: the Pyramids wonder (+1 for all your Builders), Governor Liang's starting title (+1 for Builders trained in her city), the Serfdom and Public Works policy cards (swappable bonuses slotted into your government) (+2 for new Builders), and Qin Shi Huang's leader ability (+1).

ImprovementGoes onEffect
Farmflat Grassland, Plains, Floodplains, grain resources+1 Food, +0.5 Housing; +1 Food more with two adjacent Farms (Feudalism)
Minefeatureless Hills, mineral resources+1 Production (+1 more with Apprenticeship)
Pasture, Plantation, Camp, Quarry, Fishing Boatsthe matching resourceunlocks the resource; all but the Quarry also give +0.5 Housing

Sources: Builder (Civ6); Tile improvement (Civ6); Housing (Civ6).

Harvesting and chopping VI

Your capital needs a Settler (the unit that founds a city). One Builder click turns the Woods next door into Production, and the Woods are gone for good.

A Builder can also spend a charge to remove a feature or harvest a bonus resource. The tile loses the feature or resource for good, and the owning city gets a one-time lump of yield. Players call removing Woods "chopping".

  • Woods give Production. You can remove them after researching Mining.
  • Rainforest gives Food and Production (after Bronze Working). Marsh gives Food (after Irrigation).
  • Bonus resources such as Stone, Rice or Deer give Food, Production or Gold, depending on the resource.

The lump grows with how far you are through the tech and civic trees, and with game speed. A community-derived formula (not official) multiplies it by up to ten over a full game, as with district (a specialized city tile that holds buildings) costs. Any Production beyond what the current build needs carries over to the next item in the queue.

Three costly details:

  • If you place a district or wonder on Woods or Rainforest, the feature is cleared and you get nothing. Chop first, then place.
  • Woods you plant yourself (Builders can plant them after the Conservation civic) give no Production when removed.
  • Magnus, the Steward Governor (a named official assigned to a city), adds 50% to every harvest and chop in his city with his starting title, Groundbreaker (Gathering Storm; it was 100% in Rise and Fall). He needs 5 turns to settle into a city before it works.

Sources: Builder (Civ6); Woods (Civ6); Magnus (Civ6); CivFanatics formula thread (community-derived).

Chopping trades a stream for a lump. Kept Woods give +1 Production every turn you work them; a chop gives a pile today and nothing from that tile again. With made-up round numbers, if a chop gave 30 and the Woods 1 a turn, the stream would need 30 turns to match. But a Settler (the unit that founds a city) built today pays you back at once, so "30 versus 1 a turn" understates the chop.

Economics

Time value and discounting

In 1849 Martin Faustmann, a German forester, tackled a basic question: when should you cut a forest? Waiting gives bigger trees and a bigger sale. But timber sold sooner is money sooner, and the land can be replanted sooner. Once Faustmann priced the waiting with an interest rate, the best time to cut came earlier than tree growth alone suggested. His formula is still in wide use.

Time value: a benefit now is worth more than the same benefit later, because it can be put to work at once. Discounting converts future benefits into today's value.

Open in the PDF, page 40

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Chop the Woods: a lump of Production nowCash in hand today
Keep the Woods: a small yield every turn you work itAn annuity paid over years

Where the map breaks

  • Civ has no interest rate, so your discount rate is a guess.
  • Civ ends, so a payoff after the last turn is worth nothing. Faustmann's formula counts every future harvest.

Read next

  • Primary and readable: Fisher (1930), chapters I to IV, free at FRASER.

Irving Fisher, The Theory of Interest (1930).

Lab: What is one forest worth?

Predict first: guess the ratio now, as a number of turns, and whether it will be bigger or smaller when you repeat the lab 80 turns later.

  1. Pick a city building something big (a Settler or a district). Note the Production bar and the turns left.
  2. Move a Builder onto a Woods tile in that city's territory and remove the Woods (you need the Mining tech).
  3. Look at the Production bar again. The jump is your chop value. Call it C.

Measure: C, and C divided by the Production the Woods tile would have given per turn if worked. That ratio is how many turns of working the tile the chop is worth.

What you should see: early in the game the ratio is modest. Do it again 80 turns later and C will be noticeably larger, because harvest yields grow with your progress. Late chops can finish whole buildings.

If your result differs: a very large C may mean Magnus is established in that city (+50%). A C of zero means a Builder planted those Woods. If the bar filled, the rest of C carried over to the next item, so the jump understates it. Each is a rule to check before you chop.

In the world: look at any payment plan that charges interest. The payments add up to more than the price paid today. That gap is what the lender charges you for waiting, the same trade between a lump now and a stream later that a chop makes.

Do

  • Chop Woods or Rainforest into Settlers and districts in the early game.
  • Put Magnus in the city where you plan to chop the most.

Avoid

  • Chopping every forest near a city with no hills. Woods may be its only Production.

Check yourself

  1. A Builder with 1 charge left stands on Woods where you want a Campus (the science district). Magnus is established in this city. What order do you act in, and what happens to the Builder?

    Show answer

    Chop first: Magnus adds 50%, and Production beyond the current build carries over. Then place the Campus, since placing it on Woods clears them for nothing. The chop uses the last charge, so the Builder disappears.

  2. A chop gives 30 Production now; the worked Woods would give 1 a turn. A friend says the chop only pays if the game ends within 30 turns. What has he missed?

    Show answer

    Time value. Production now can build a Settler now, and that city pays back every turn after. Discounting makes early yields worth more, so 30 now beats 30 spread over 30 turns.

  3. A shop owner can sell her old delivery van for cash today or keep it earning a little each month. What should she compare, and why does cash today count for more?

    Show answer

    The lump today against the monthly earnings, discounted to today's value. Cash now can be put to work at once, say on stock that sells this month, so a sum today beats the same sum later.

In the game: before you place your next district on Woods, chop the Woods first.

In the world: a sum today beats the same sum later, because it can go to work at once.

Districts

Districts side by side make each other better. A teenager's 1895 bedspread is why one Georgia town became the world's carpet capital.

What districts are VI

In Civ VI a city spreads across the map. A district (a specialized city tile that holds buildings) is a specialized neighborhood built on its own tile near the city. Most buildings go inside a district: the Library goes in a Campus (the science district), the Market in a Commercial Hub (the Gold and trade district). A district tile stops producing its old terrain yield and instead gives an adjacency (extra yield from what sits next door) bonus from what is next to it.

DistrictMain yieldUnlocked by
CampusScience, Great Scientist pointsWriting
Theater SquareCulture, Writer/Artist/Musician pointsDrama and Poetry
Holy SiteFaith, Great Prophet pointsAstrology
Commercial HubGold, Great Merchant pointsCurrency
HarborGold, naval units, Great Admiral pointsCelestial Navigation
Industrial ZoneProduction, Great Engineer pointsApprenticeship
Encampmentmilitary units, Great General pointsBronze Working
Entertainment Complex / Water ParkAmenitiesGames and Recreation / Natural History
Government Plaza (one per civ)Governor titles, adjacencyState Workforce
Aqueduct, Dam, CanalHousing, water, passageEngineering, Buttress, Steam Power
NeighborhoodHousingUrbanization
Aerodrome, Spaceportair units, space raceFlight, Rocketry

Many civs have a unique district that replaces one of these at half the cost.

How many districts a city can hold VI

A city can hold 1 specialty district at size 1, 2 at size 4, 3 at size 7, and one more for every 3 citizens after that. The Aqueduct (a district that brings water and Housing), Dam, Canal, Neighborhood (a district that adds Housing) and Spaceport (the district that builds space projects) do not count against this limit.

District costs rise as you progress. The game multiplies a base cost (54 Production for most districts on Standard speed) by 1 plus 9 times the share of the tech tree or civic tree you have finished, whichever is larger. A district placed at the end of the game costs ten times what it cost at the start.

Sources: District (Civ6); List of districts in Civ6.

Adjacency VI

Adjacency bonuses come in three sizes: major (+2), standard (+1) and minor (+0.5). Minor bonuses are rounded down, and each kind of source is rounded down on its own. One Rainforest next to a Campus gives +0.5, which rounds to 0. Every district gives a minor bonus to every district next to it, and the Government Plaza (a one-per-empire district for government bonuses) adds a standard +1 on top.

DistrictGets adjacency from
Campus+1 per Mountain; +2 per Reef or Geothermal Fissure; +0.5 per Rainforest; +0.5 per district
Holy Site+2 per natural wonder; +1 per Mountain; +0.5 per Woods; +0.5 per district
Theater Square+2 per wonder; +2 per Entertainment Complex or Water Park; +0.5 per district
Commercial Hub+2 if next to a river; +2 per Harbor; +0.5 per district
Harbor+2 from the City Center; +1 per coastal resource; +0.5 per district
Industrial Zone+2 per Aqueduct, Dam or Canal; +1 per Quarry; +1 per strategic resource; +0.5 per Mine or Lumber Mill; +0.5 per district

The placement lens shows each tile's bonus and its sources.

Sources: Adjacency bonus (Civ6); List of districts in Civ6.

A third district on the Grassland tile would leave the district line at +1 (three halves round down to one), so the Campus (the science district) would stay at +3.

Specialists VI

A citizen does not have to work a tile. Once a district has buildings, it has Specialist (a citizen working a building instead of a tile) slots, up to three per district. A Specialist eats 2 Food like any citizen and gives a fixed yield instead of a tile's yield:

DistrictSpecialist yield (base)
Campus+2 Science
Theater Square+2 Culture
Holy Site+2 Faith
Commercial Hub+4 Gold
Industrial Zone+2 Production
Harbor+1 Food, +2 Gold
Encampment+1 Production, +2 Gold

Specialists matter most in big cities short of good tiles.

Source: Specialists (Civ6).

Economics / geography

Agglomeration economies and clusters

In 1895 Catherine Evans, a Georgia teenager, sewed a tufted bedspread as a wedding gift. Others wanted one. By the 1930s nearly 10,000 "tufters" worked on porches around Dalton. After World War II machines began tufting carpet, and the industry settled in Dalton, where workers with the skill already lived. Today more than 90% of the world's functional carpet is made within 65 miles of the city.

Agglomeration: firms and workers are more productive near each other, through shared suppliers, shared skills and ideas spread by contact. Concentration then feeds itself.

Open in the PDF, page 44

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Every district gives +0.5 to each district next to itFirms gaining from neighbors
The Government Plaza gives +1 to adjacent specialty districtsA university anchoring a cluster

Where the map breaks

  • Civ's adjacency (extra yield from what sits next door) is a fixed table; real clusters run on people learning from each other.
  • Clusters have costs Civ never charges. Dalton's mills have taken up to a third of a river's summer flow.

Read next

  • Accessible: Krugman (1991), NBER working paper version.

Alfred Marshall, Principles of Economics (1890); Paul Krugman, "Increasing Returns and Economic Geography," Journal of Political Economy 99(3) (1991); Michael E. Porter, The Competitive Advantage of Nations (1990).

Economics

Division of labor

William Klann of Ford came back from a Chicago slaughterhouse with an idea: there, carcasses moved along a line, and each worker removed the same piece over and over. In 1913 Ford started a moving assembly line for the Model T, split into 45 steps. Labor per car fell from 12.5 hours to 1 hour 33 minutes. The price fell from $825 in 1908 to $360 in 1916.

Division of labor: splitting work into specialized tasks raises output per worker. Adam Smith's limit: it pays only if the market can absorb the output.

Open in the PDF, page 44

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
A Specialist works in a Campus instead of a fieldA worker who does one task all day
Cities specialize, and trade routes move the yieldsRegions that specialize and trade

Where the map breaks

  • Civ's cities need no buyers, so Smith's market limit barely exists.
  • Civ's Specialists (citizens working buildings instead of tiles) never quit. Ford had to hire 963 men to add 100, until in 1914 it more than doubled most pay, to $5 a day.

Read next

  • Primary and readable: Smith (1776), Book I, chapters I to III, free at the Internet Archive.

Adam Smith, An Inquiry into the Nature and Causes of the Wealth of Nations (1776), Book I, chapters I to III.

Wide or tall? VI

Wide means many cities; tall means a few large ones. Civ V V punished extra cities, since each one added Unhappiness. Civ VI has no direct penalty, and each new city brings its own City Center (the tile the city was founded on), district slot and production queue. Community consensus on CivFanatics is that wide beats tall in Civ VI, and that tall works mainly for specific civs or maps. Since districts unlock by population in steps of three, three size-4 cities hold six districts between them while one size-12 city holds four.

Source: CivFanatics, "Tall versus wide" (community consensus).

Lab: Cluster or scatter?

Predict first: which layout gives the bigger total adjacency, the triangle or the scattered best tiles, and by how much?

  1. In one city, plan three districts in a tight triangle, each touching the other two. Use the placement lens to read each one's adjacency.
  2. Reload and plan the same three districts each on its best single tile, ignoring the others.
  3. Add up the total adjacency of all three in each layout.

Measure: total adjacency yield per turn for each layout.

What you should see: each district in the triangle gets +1 from its two neighbors. On flat, featureless land the triangle usually wins. Next to Mountains, terrain often wins.

If your result differs: terrain can beat neighbors. Each Mountain gives a Campus +1, twice what a district gives. A single Rainforest next to a Campus gives +0.5, which rounds down to 0. A tile touching the Government Plaza gets a standard +1 extra. Adjacency is a sum of separately rounded parts, so read the lens tile by tile.

In the world: search a map app for car dealers or phone repair shops near you. They often bunch along one road. Ask what each shop gains from rivals next door: passing customers, shared suppliers, staff who already know the trade.

Check yourself

  1. A Campus tile touches 1 Mountain, 3 Rainforests and 1 other district. What is its adjacency bonus?

    Show answer

    Mountain +1. Rainforests 3 × 0.5 = 1.5, rounded down to 1. The district gives +0.5, rounded down to 0. Each kind rounds on its own, so the Campus gets +2 Science.

  2. Civ says packing districts together always pays. Name the concept, and give one reason a real city might not cluster its firms.

    Show answer

    Agglomeration economies: neighbors raise each other's output. Real clusters also bring congestion, such as high rents, traffic and pollution. Civ models none of these, so clustering always pays in the game and only sometimes in life.

  3. Three bakeries in a small town each make bread, cakes and pastries. What could they gain if each specialized in one, and what would limit it?

    Show answer

    Division of labor: each baker gets faster and can buy better tools for one product. Smith's limit is market size. Specializing pays only if the town buys enough of each product to keep a bakery busy.

In the game: read the adjacency on every candidate tile before you place; each kind of half-bonus rounds down on its own.

In the world: where things sit and who does what both shape output. Neighbors share skills, and specialists get fast.

Tech and Civics

Kill a unit with a Slinger (an early ranged unit) and Archery costs 40% less. Civ pays you to do before you study, and so do real economies.

Two trees VI

Science pays for the technology tree: new units, buildings, improvements and districts (specialized city tiles that hold buildings). Culture pays for the civics tree: governments, policy cards (swappable bonuses slotted into your government), Governor (a named official assigned to a city) titles and more districts and wonders. You research one tech and one civic at the same time. Science comes mainly from Campuses (science districts) and their buildings, Culture from Theater Squares (culture districts) and the Monument, a cheap Culture building every city can build in its center.

Eurekas and Inspirations VI

Most techs have a Eureka and most civics an Inspiration: a task in the world that, once done, pays part of the cost. Since Rise and Fall (the first Civ VI expansion) a boost is worth 40% of the tech or civic's cost (it was 50% in the base game). Babylon's Eurekas give the whole tech. Each boost fires once. Animal Husbandry, Mining, Pottery and Code of Laws have none.

Tech or civicBoost task
ArcheryKill a unit with a Slinger
MasonryBuild a Quarry
Bronze WorkingKill 3 Barbarians
SailingFound a city on the Coast
WritingMeet another civilization
CurrencyMake a trade route
Craftsmanship (civic)Improve 3 tiles
Early Empire (civic)Reach 6 total population
State Workforce (civic)Build any district
Political Philosophy (civic)Meet 3 city-states

Tribal villages, some Great Scientists (famous scientists you recruit for one-time boosts) and spies also give boosts.

Sources: Boost (Civ6); List of boosts in Civ6.

The one rule that matters most VI

Do not finish researching a tech or civic before you trigger its boost. Once it is done, its boost is gone for good, and you paid 100% for something you could have had for 60%.

A boost can land any time before the research completes, and if your progress plus the 40% reaches the full cost, the tech completes on the spot. So research other things while you work on the boost. If you must start the tech first, stop when its bar reaches about 60%, switch to something else, and let the boost finish it. The game draws a hollow bar on the tech icon to show where the boost would take you.

With a Slinger and Barbarians nearby, research Bronze Working or Animal Husbandry first and let the Slinger make a kill. That hands you 40% of Archery.

Economics

Learning by doing

In 1941 American shipyards began building the Liberty, a plain cargo ship of one design. The yards had never built welded ships, and the workforce was newly trained. At one new yard, Calship, the first Liberty took 1.73 million hours of labor. In mid-1943 the same ship took 406,000. By 1945, 18 yards on three coasts had built 2,710 of them.

Learning by doing: the more of something an economy has made, the cheaper the next one, and what one producer learns spills over to others.

Open in the PDF, page 47

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Build a Quarry and Masonry costs 40% lessWorkers who learn a craft by practicing it
Improve 3 tiles: Craftsmanship costs 40% lessA shipyard faster with every hull

Where the map breaks

  • Arrow's learning keeps going. A Eureka (a 40% research discount for a related task) fires once.
  • The economist Peter Thompson traced most of Calship's gains to new equipment and lower quality: more ships cracked.

Read next

  • Accessible: SFI Press commentary on Arrow (1962).

Kenneth J. Arrow, "The Economic Implications of Learning by Doing," Review of Economic Studies 29(3) (1962).

Economics

Exogenous and endogenous growth

In 1957 Robert Solow, an MIT economist, split American growth into its causes. From 1909 to 1949, output per hour in private non-farm business roughly doubled. The obvious cause, more machines per worker, explained only about one-eighth. The other seven-eighths was "technical change," which his model measured but could not explain. In 1990 Paul Romer argued that people make it on purpose, through research.

Exogenous and endogenous growth: in Solow's model, investment lifts income to a higher level, then hits diminishing returns. Lasting growth needs new technology, which Romer modeled as the product of research.

Open in the PDF, page 48

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
More Farms and Mines raise yields onceMore machines raise output once (Solow)
Campuses yield Science, which buys new techsFirms researching to create ideas (Romer)

Where the map breaks

  • Civ's tech tree is finite and the same for everyone. Romer's ideas are unlimited.
  • Civ shows where every point of Science comes from. Solow's seven-eighths was a leftover.

Read next

  • Story: Robert M. Solow, "Technical Change and the Aggregate Production Function," Review of Economics and Statistics 39(3) (1957).

Robert M. Solow, "A Contribution to the Theory of Economic Growth," Quarterly Journal of Economics 70(1) (1956); Paul M. Romer, "Endogenous Technological Change," Journal of Political Economy 98(5) (1990).

Technology

Diffusion of innovations

In 1747 James Lind, a navy surgeon on HMS Salisbury, tested several cures on sailors with scurvy. Oranges and lemons worked best. Then little happened: his result filled a few paragraphs of a long book, and leading doctors blamed other causes. In 1794 the Suffolk sailed for India with lemon juice and arrived four months later without scurvy. Commanders demanded lemons, and by June 1795 the Navy ordered a daily lemon-juice ration for every warship.

Diffusion of innovations: new ideas spread along an S-curve, slowly among early adopters, fast through the majority, then slowly among holdouts. Ideas people can see working spread faster.

Open in the PDF, page 48

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
A Eureka pays 40% once you try the thing, like a Quarry for MasonryPeople adopt faster once they can try an idea small
Religion spreads city to city by pressure (Part 3)Ideas spreading through networks

Where the map breaks

  • Research a tech and every city has it at once. The Navy needed until 1800 to supply every ship.
  • Rogers's adopters choose. Civ's cities change by arithmetic.

Read next

  • Accessible: Griliches (1957), which has clear graphs.

Everett M. Rogers, Diffusion of Innovations (1962). Classic evidence: Zvi Griliches, "Hybrid Corn: An Exploration in the Economics of Technological Change," Econometrica 25(4) (1957).

Technology

General-purpose technologies

In 1899 electric motors supplied under 5% of the power driving American factory machines. For twenty years factories added motors but often kept the old layout, with overhead shafts and belts. Productivity hardly moved until the early 1920s, when firms built new plants around the motor: one per machine, on a single floor. By then the first central power station was four decades old.

General-purpose technology: one used across many sectors that keeps improving and enables other inventions, like electricity. It pays off late, because users must reorganize around it first.

Open in the PDF, page 49

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Apprenticeship and Industrialization each add +1 Production to every MineElectricity raising output across many industries
Writing unlocks the Campus, which feeds every later techPrinting, which sped up later learning

Where the map breaks

  • Real GPTs can take decades to pay. Civ pays the turn the tech completes.
  • Bresnahan and Trajtenberg stress coordination between inventors and users. Civ has one decision-maker.

Read next

  • Story: Paul A. David, "The Dynamo and the Computer," American Economic Review 80(2) (1990).

Timothy F. Bresnahan and Manuel Trajtenberg, "General Purpose Technologies: 'Engines of Growth'?," Journal of Econometrics 65(1) (1995).

The hard question: do you judge the science or the scientist?

In Civ a tech has no inventor, only a cost. In 1909 the German chemist Fritz Haber made ammonia from the nitrogen in air, and from 1913 Carl Bosch scaled the process up for industry. Vaclav Smil, who studies energy and food, estimated that by 2000 about 40% of the world's people depended on its fertilizer for food. In World War I Haber proposed chlorine gas as a weapon and helped supervise its first mass release, at Ypres on 22 April 1915. The 1907 Hague Convention, which Germany had signed, banned "poison or poisoned weapons." In 1919 he received the 1918 Nobel Prize in Chemistry, for ammonia.

The case for judging the science: the fertilizer feeds billions whatever its inventor did, and the prize was for the discovery alone. Every major power in that war used poison gas. Haber is reported to have said that in peace a scientist belongs to the world, and in war to his country.

The case against: Haber was not a bystander whose idea someone else misused. He proposed the gas attack and helped run it. Honoring him without that record tells future scientists that a great discovery excuses a terrible act.

Your call: should a prize honor a discovery when its discoverer did something you believe was wrong?

Lab: Can boosts win the research race?

Predict first: which game finishes all three techs first, and by how many turns?

  1. On turn 1, write down the Science cost of Archery, Masonry and Writing.
  2. In one game, research them in order as soon as you can, ignoring boosts.
  3. In a second game, research other things first and do each boost task before researching that tech.

Measure: the turn on which all three are done, in each game.

What you should see: the boosted game spends 40% less Science on those three techs and puts the rest into others.

If your result differs: meeting another civ fires Writing's Eureka by itself, so the raw game may have had a boost anyway. If the boosted game was slower, a task kept you waiting, such as having no Slinger target. Babylon gets the whole tech from a Eureka, and China gets 50%. Boosts always save Science, but not always turns.

In the world: cook the same new recipe three times and time each attempt. The later attempts are usually faster, because doing teaches. Aircraft makers in the 1930s and wartime shipyards showed the same curve.

Check yourself

  1. Archery is 30% researched when your Slinger kills a unit. You are not playing Babylon or China. Where does Archery's bar end up?

    Show answer

    The Eureka adds 40% of the cost, so 30% + 40% = 70%. The tech does not complete, and you research the last 30% as normal. From 60% or more, it would have completed on the spot.

  2. In Civ, the moment you research a tech, every city has it. Which concept does this skip, and what would the real version look like?

    Show answer

    Diffusion of innovations. Real ideas spread along an S-curve: slowly among early adopters, fast through the majority, then slowly among holdouts, as hybrid corn did across US states.

  3. Your first attempt at a new recipe takes 60 minutes and your second takes 45. Name the concept, and say what Arrow added to it.

    Show answer

    Learning by doing: experience makes the next unit cheaper. Arrow added spillover. What one producer learns spreads to others, which is why he treated it as a source of growth for a whole economy.

In the game: before you pick your next tech, read its boost and ask whether you can do it first.

In the world: knowledge grows from doing things and from deliberate investment, and it spreads slowly before it spreads fast.

Governments and Policies

Same land, same people, different rules, different lives. In Civ a government is a few card slots. In the world, rules may decide who grows rich.

The rule VI

Your government gives you a set of policy card (a swappable bonus slotted into your government) slots and one or two bonuses. Policy cards are small rules you choose, like "+1 Production (the yield that builds units and buildings) in every city". There are four kinds of slot: Military, Economic, Diplomatic and Wildcard. A Wildcard slot takes any card. You must fill every slot to end your turn.

GovernmentTierCivicMilEconDipWild
Chiefdom0Code of Laws1100
Autocracy1Political Philosophy1111
Oligarchy1Political Philosophy2101
Classical Republic1Political Philosophy0211
Monarchy2Divine Right2112
Theocracy2Reformed Church2211
Merchant Republic2Exploration1221
Communism3Class Struggle3311
Democracy3Suffrage1322
Fascism3Totalitarianism4112
Digital Democracy4Distributed Sovereignty1135
Synthetic Technocracy4Optimization Imperative1315
Corporate Libertarianism4Venture Politics3115

Tier 1 governments arrive in the Classical Era, tier 2 in the Medieval and Renaissance Eras, tier 3 in the Modern Era, and tier 4 (Gathering Storm only) in the Information Era.

Your first real choice is a tier-1 government (Rise and Fall and Gathering Storm values):

  • Classical Republic (a government type): +1 Housing (a city's soft population cap) and +1 Amenity in every city with a district (a specialized city tile that holds buildings), and +15% Great Person (a famous figure you recruit for one big effect) points. Suits peaceful building.
  • Oligarchy (a government type): +4 Combat Strength for land melee, anti-cavalry and naval melee units, and +20% unit experience. Suits an early war.
  • Autocracy (a government type): +1 to all yields in a city for each Palace, Government Plaza (a one-per-empire district for government bonuses) building and Diplomatic Quarter (the diplomacy district) building in it, and +10% Production toward wonders.

When you can change VI

You can swap your government and all your cards for free whenever you complete a civic. You can also change when your number of slots changes, when a new era begins (Rise and Fall onward), and when you finish a Government Plaza building. At any other time it costs Gold.

Good early cards: Urban Planning (+1 Production in every city) and God King (+1 Gold and +1 Faith in the capital), both from Code of Laws (a step on the culture tree); Discipline (+5 Combat Strength against Barbarians); Colonization (+50% Production toward Settlers, from Early Empire); Ilkum (Babylon's own Builder) (+30% toward Builders) and Agoge (+50% toward early melee, ranged and anti-cavalry units), both from Craftsmanship.

Sources: Government (Civ6) and its pages for each government; List of policies in Civ6.

Political economy

Institutions: the rules of the game

On 2 January 1672 Charles II of England stopped repaying the goldsmith bankers who had lent him money. After the revolution of 1688, Parliament gained a veto: the Crown could no longer change loans or taxes alone. Tied hands made a better borrower. In nine years of war the debt grew from £1 million to nearly £17 million, and long-term rates fell from 14% to 6 to 8%.

Institutions: the rules people make to shape how they deal with each other, written or unwritten, and how they are enforced. Organizations are the players; institutions are the rules.

Open in the PDF, page 52

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Your government and policy cards set the payoff of every later moveLaws and taxes that make some activities pay
Colonization makes Settlers cheaper, so you expandA subsidy that pulls investment into one sector

Where the map breaks

  • North stresses that enforcing rules is costly and imperfect. Civ enforces every rule perfectly and free.
  • You rewrite your rules whenever a civic completes. England's rewrite took a civil war and a revolution.

Read next

  • Accessible: Phil Agre's review of North.

Douglass C. North, Institutions, Institutional Change and Economic Performance (1990).

Political economy

Inclusive and extractive institutions

A fence cuts the city of Nogales in half. In 2012 Daron Acemoglu and James Robinson reported that the average household in Nogales, Arizona, earned about $30,000 a year, and most adults had finished high school. On the Sonora side, households earned about a third as much, and most adults had not. The land and weather are the same. The rules, the authors argue, are not.

Inclusive and extractive institutions: inclusive rules spread power and protect property widely. Extractive rules funnel power and income to an elite that blocks change; the authors argue such growth fades.

Open in the PDF, page 53

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Cards that raise yields in every cityRules that let everyone keep the gains from investing
Cards that pull yields toward the capital or the armyRules that funnel income to a ruling group

Where the map breaks

  • In Civ you are the whole elite, and no government is tied to long-run growth.
  • The thesis is contested. Critics point to fast growth under extractive rule, as in China, and to geography and culture.

Read next

  • Evidence base: Acemoglu, Johnson and Robinson, "The Colonial Origins of Comparative Development" (2001).

Daron Acemoglu and James A. Robinson, Why Nations Fail (2012).

The hard question: is order and prosperity worth limits on freedom?

In Civ you swap governments like tools. Singapore had one prime minister, Lee Kuan Yew, from 1959 to 1990. Over those years GNP per head rose from $1,240 to $18,437, unemployment fell from 13.5% to 1.7%, and public flats rose from 22,975 to 667,575. His government also sued critics for defamation and detained opponents without trial: 113 left-wing activists in Operation Coldstore in 1963, and a former opposition MP, Chia Thye Poh, for 23 years. Of his rules on private life, Lee said: "We decide what is right. Never mind what the people think."

The case for Lee's way: in 1965 Singapore was a small island with no natural resources, newly separated from Malaysia and facing threats from communists and from Indonesia. Lee argued that stability had to come first, and the results are real: jobs, homes and clean government. When he died in 2015, about 1.7 million people paid their respects.

The case against: no one can rerun history to show the detentions were needed. People value the right to speak and to vote their rulers out for its own sake, whatever it does for wealth. And a system that trusts one wise ruler has no defense against an unwise one.

Your call: how much freedom would you trade for safety and a rising income?

Lab: Swap cards to fit the job

Predict first: how many turns will Colonization save on one Settler (the unit that founds a city), and how many will Ilkum save on one Builder (a unit that builds farms, mines and the like)?

  1. When you plan two Settlers in a row, slot Colonization. Note the turns to build a Settler.
  2. When the Settlers are done and you start Builders, wait for your next civic, then swap Colonization for Ilkum.
  3. Note the turns to build a Builder with and without Ilkum.

Measure: turns saved per item from each card.

What you should see: Colonization cuts a Settler's build time by about a third, Ilkum a Builder's by about a quarter, and neither helps anything else. Cards are tools for the job in front of you.

If your result differs: the city's Production changed between measurements. It grew, a citizen moved tile, or it fell to Displeased (−10% to non-Food yields). A chop's overflow may also have landed in one item. A card multiplies the Production you have, so hold everything else still while you measure.

In the world: a shop's "buy two, get one free" sign changes what people put in their baskets, though nobody is forced. Stand near one such offer and count how many shoppers take three. The rule did the work.

Check yourself

  1. Early Empire (a step on the culture tree) has just completed and you plan three Settlers in a row. What do you do before ending the turn, and how much faster is each Settler?

    Show answer

    Open the Government screen and slot Colonization; completing a civic makes the swap free. +50% Production cuts build time to about two-thirds, so each Settler takes about a third fewer turns.

  2. A player calls Autocracy "a tool for a wonder push." Why would Acemoglu and Robinson object, and why does Civ let it pass?

    Show answer

    They argue extractive rule, with power held by a narrow elite, cannot sustain growth. Civ has no domestic politics and ties no government to long-run growth, so there Autocracy really is only a tool. That is where the map breaks.

  3. A city council makes downtown parking free on Saturdays. Using North's idea of institutions, predict one change in behavior, and say who might defend the rule later.

    Show answer

    Rules set payoffs, so more people drive downtown on Saturdays. Shops that gain trade now have a stake in the rule and would defend it, which is why North says institutions change slowly.

In the game: each time a civic finishes, ask what the next ten turns are for and slot cards for that.

In the world: rules shape what people find worth doing, so to change behavior, look at the rules first.

Governors

In Civ your officials do exactly what you want. Real ones have their own interests, as a 1790 contract to ship convicts to Australia showed.

The rule VI

Governors (named officials assigned to cities) arrived with Rise and Fall (the first Civ VI expansion). A Governor is a named official you assign to one city. Every city with a Governor gets +8 Loyalty (how firmly the city stays yours) per turn from the moment you assign them. Their other powers start only once they are established, which takes 3 turns for Victor and 5 turns for everyone else. Moving a Governor to a new city restarts that count.

GovernorStarting title
Reyna, the FinancierLand Acquisition: the city gains new tiles faster
Victor, the CastellanRedoubt: +5 Combat Strength for the city's garrison
Amani, the DiplomatMessenger: can sit in a city-state and count as 2 Envoys (the tokens that win a city-state's favor)
Magnus, the StewardGroundbreaker: +50% from harvests and chops
Moksha, the CardinalBishop: religious pressure from the city +100%, +2 Faith per specialty district
Liang, the SurveyorGuildmaster: Builders trained in the city get +1 charge
Pingala, the EducatorLibrarian: +15% Science and Culture in the city

Suleiman of the Ottomans gets an eighth, Ibrahim, who establishes in 3 turns.

Titles and promotions VI

You spend Governor titles on Governors. Each title either appoints a new Governor or buys one promotion for an existing one. Each Governor has a starting title plus five promotions arranged in a small tree, so later promotions require earlier ones. Titles come from 13 civics, 4 from the Government Plaza (a one-per-empire district for government bonuses) (1 for the district, 1 for each of its three building tiers), the repeatable Future Civic, and a few Great People (a famous figure you recruit for one big effect) and wonders.

Appointing and fully promoting all seven Governors would take 42 titles. The civics and the Government Plaza give 17. So you must choose between many Governors with basic powers and a few with deep powers. Promotions cannot be undone.

Sources: Governor (Civ6) and each Governor's page.

Political economy

The principal–agent problem

In 1790 Britain paid a private firm a flat £17 7s 6d per convict shipped to Australia, whether they landed alive or not. Only two of the crew answered to the Crown. Of 1,038 convicts embarked, 273 died at sea, a record for the route. The guard's commander wrote that the masters profited from every ration they withheld. From 1792 a naval surgeon took charge of the convicts, and deaths fell sharply.

Principal–agent problem: when someone acts for you with their own interests and better information, you bear agency costs: monitoring, incentives and the losses that remain.

Open in the PDF, page 55

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
You assign Magnus to a city to chop for youA shareholder hiring a manager
Automated units follow their own simple goalsAn agent who does the job their own way

Where the map breaks

  • Civ's Governors never shirk, steal or misreport, so the problem's hidden information is gone.
  • Jensen and Meckling studied contracts. Civ has none to get wrong.

Read next

  • Accessible: ProMarket on the paper's influence.

Michael C. Jensen and William H. Meckling, "Theory of the Firm: Managerial Behavior, Agency Costs and Ownership Structure," Journal of Financial Economics 3(4) (1976).

Lab: What is establishment worth?

Predict first: by what percentage will the capital's Science and Culture rise when Pingala establishes, and how many turns of that bonus will one move cost?

  1. Assign Pingala to your capital. Note its Science and Culture on the turn you assign her.
  2. Note them again on the turn she is established, 5 turns later.
  3. Later, move her to a second city and count the turns of Librarian you lose to re-establishing.

Measure: Science and Culture per turn before and after, and the yield lost during the move.

What you should see: a 15% jump when she establishes, and a 5-turn hole every time you move her. Governors reward picking a home and leaving them there.

If your result differs: the city changed during the wait. It grew, a citizen moved tile, or its Amenity level shifted (Happy adds +10% to all yields). Moving her again within the 5 turns restarts the count. Change one thing at a time to see what the Governor alone is worth.

In the world: a sports team that changes coach mid-season needs time before the new coach's ideas show in results. Any handover has a settling-in cost, so count it before you move a good person.

Check yourself

  1. You move Pingala from your capital to a new city. For how many turns does the new city go without her Librarian bonus, and what does it get at once?

    Show answer

    Moving restarts establishment, so 5 turns pass with no +15% Science and Culture. The +8 Loyalty per turn starts the moment she is assigned, so the city gets that at once.

  2. You hand your cities to Governors, yet Civ shows no principal–agent problem. Why not?

    Show answer

    The problem needs an agent with its own interests and better information. Civ's Governors never shirk, steal or misreport, so there are no hidden actions to watch and no agency costs to pay.

  3. A café owner hires a manager on a fixed wage, whatever the takings. Name one agency cost the owner faces and one way to reduce it.

    Show answer

    She cannot watch every shift, so she pays to monitor, such as checking the till. Or she can change incentives: a share of profits lines the manager's interests up with hers.

In the game: spend your first title on the Governor whose starting power your capital needs most, and do not move Governors often.

In the world: whenever you hand someone a job, ask what they gain and what they know that you don't.

Wonders and Great People

Wonders are races you can lose, and Great People (a famous figure you recruit for one big effect) are prizes you can buy. The hard part is quitting a race you have already paid into.

Wonders VI

Twenty turns into the Pyramids, you get the news: a rival has finished them first.

A wonder is a one-of-a-kind building, so building one is a race against every other civ. In Civ VI a wonder takes its own tile near the city and has placement rules: the Pyramids need flat Desert, Stonehenge needs to be next to Stone, and so on.

A wonder pays you in two ways: its own effect, and Tourism (the score behind a Culture Victory) later in the game. Finishing any wonder triggers the Inspiration (a 40% civic discount for a related task) for Drama and Poetry.

If a rival finishes the wonder first, sources disagree on what you get back: the Civ VI wiki says 50% of the Production (the yield that builds units and buildings) you put in; many players on the Steam forums report getting nothing. Plan as if most of it is lost.

Sources: Wonder (Civ6); Steam Civ VI discussion.

When is a wonder worth it? VI

Community consensus on CivFanatics is that most early wonders are a poor trade for a beginner. The Production (the yield that builds units and buildings) for one ancient wonder could have been a Settler (the unit that founds a city) or two, and a new city keeps paying for the whole game. The exceptions are wonders whose effect fits your plan and your land, such as Stonehenge when you start beside Stone and want a religion.

Source: CivFanatics, "Are wonders worth it?" (community consensus).

Great People VI

Great People are named historical figures, each with a one-time or lasting power. There are nine kinds: Great General (a famous general who strengthens nearby units), Admiral, Engineer, Merchant, Prophet, Scientist, Writer, Artist and Musician. Each named person can be earned once per game, by whoever gets there first.

You earn them with Great Person points of the matching kind. Points pool across your whole empire. They come mostly from districts (a Campus makes Great Scientist points, a Theater Square makes Writer, Artist and Musician points), plus some buildings, policy cards (swappable bonuses slotted into your government) and district projects.

Great Person's eraBase points needed (Standard speed)
Classical60
Medieval120
Renaissance240
Industrial420
Modern660
Atomic960
Information1,320

Great Prophets (famous prophets who found religions) run out early (none from the Industrial Era on, and fewer on smaller maps). In Gathering Storm (the second Civ VI expansion), points for a kind you can no longer earn turn into Faith (the yield spent on religion) one for one. You can pass on a Great Person you do not want and keep most of your points for the next one.

Patronage VI

You can also patronize a Great Person: pay to recruit them now. On Standard speed the price is 150 Faith plus 10 Faith per missing point, or 200 Gold plus 15 Gold per missing point. With 40 of the 60 points for a Classical Great Scientist (a famous scientist you recruit for a one-time boost), you are 20 short, so the price is 350 Faith or 500 Gold. The Oracle wonder cuts Faith patronage by 25%.

Source: Great People (Civ6).

Patronage is cheapest when you are nearly there, and paying the gap can beat losing a Great Person to a rival forever.

Decision science

The sunk-cost fallacy

Psychologists Hal Arkes and Catherine Blumer ran an experiment on the first 60 season-ticket buyers at the Ohio University Theater for its 1982–83 season. The seller handed out tickets in random order: full price, $15, or a "promotion" at $2 or $7 off. In the first half of the season, full-price buyers went to more plays. Everyone held the same tickets. Only what they had paid differed.

Sunk-cost fallacy: letting money or effort you cannot get back push you to keep going. A good decision weighs only future costs and benefits.

Open in the PDF, page 58

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Twenty turns into a wonder a rival is about to finishA project over budget and behind
Starting another wonder "to make the first one count"Throwing good money after bad

Where the map breaks

  • Not every Civ cost is fully sunk. Check what the game gives back first.
  • In multiplayer, quitting a war can signal weakness, so continuing can be rational.
  • The theater effect had faded by the second half of the season.

Read next

  • Accessible: Decision Science News on the paper.
  • Primary: Arkes and Blumer (1985), PDF.

Hal R. Arkes and Catherine Blumer, "The Psychology of Sunk Cost," Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes 35(1) (1985).

Concept lens: Every wonder is a Settler you chose not to build, an opportunity cost decision.

The hard question: is a great project worth the lives it costs?

In Civ a wonder costs only Production. The Panama Canal cost lives. A French company led by Ferdinand de Lesseps, builder of the Suez Canal, dug from 1881 until it went bankrupt in 1889, and an estimated 22,000 men died of disease and accidents. The United States took over in 1904. Its sanitation chief, William Gorgas, attacked the mosquitoes that spread yellow fever and malaria, and deaths fell. Even so, reported deaths in the American years run from 5,609 to 5,855. Most were West Indian laborers, paid on a separate "silver roll," with worse housing and mosquito control than white workers. The canal opened on 15 August 1914.

The case for building: the canal cut about 7,800 miles from a voyage between New York and San Francisco, and it has carried world trade ever since. Its workers were recruited with wages. The Americans learned from the French disaster and saved thousands of lives by fighting disease.

The case against: the risk fell hardest on the workers with the least say. Fewer deaths than the French is a low bar. Gorgas showed that disease could be fought, so protecting some workers less than others was a choice.

Your call: who should decide how much risk a great project may put on the people who build it?

Lab: The wonder or the city?

Predict first: which game will have the higher total Science, Culture and Production per turn on turn 100?

  1. Save before starting an ancient wonder in your capital. Note its Production cost and your capital's Production per turn.
  2. Build the wonder. Play to turn 100.
  3. Reload. Spend the same turns building Settlers instead. Play to turn 100.

Measure: total Science, Culture and Production per turn across your empire on turn 100 in each game.

What you should see: community consensus predicts the extra cities out-produce the wonder by turn 100. When the wonder wins, its effect matched your plan exactly. Learn to spot that case.

If your result differs: the wonder's effect may have matched your plan, as Stonehenge does for a religion. The new cities may sit on dry land with 2 Housing (a city's soft population cap) and grow slowly. The same choice pays differently on different land.

In the world: a small business can spend its savings on a smart new shopfront or on a second van that earns every day. Ask which it would rather own in five years. That is the wonder-or-city test.

Do

  • Chop (clearing Woods for a lump of Production) into a wonder you have committed to, so it finishes before rivals.

Avoid

  • Hoarding Great Generals and Admirals. Their bonuses only help units of their own era and the next.

Check yourself

  1. You have 50 of the 60 points for a Classical Great Scientist, 300 Faith and 300 Gold. A rival is close. Can you patronize, and with what?

    Show answer

    You are 10 short. Faith costs 150 + 10 × 10 = 250, which you have. Gold costs 200 + 15 × 10 = 350, which you lack. Pay 250 Faith.

  2. Twenty turns into the Pyramids, you see a rival will finish first. A friend says you have put in too much to stop. Name the error and the right test.

    Show answer

    The sunk-cost fallacy. Spending more cannot bring back what you already spent. Compare only what the next turns of Production would buy: a wonder you will probably lose, or a Settler.

  3. A town has spent half its budget on a stadium that now looks likely to sit empty. Council members want to finish it "so the money is not wasted." How should they decide?

    Show answer

    Ignore the half already spent; it is gone either way. Compare the cost of finishing with the benefit of a finished stadium, and with the best other use of the same money.

In the game: before each wonder, name what the same Production would build instead. When a race is lost, switch at once.

In the world: what you already spent is gone either way, so decide on what the next unit of effort buys.

Part 3

Living With Others

Rivals, city-states, barbarians and the weather all have plans for you. This part covers the systems that tie you to them, and the economics and international relations that explain how they behave.

Religion

A rival Missionary (a unit that spreads religion) walks in, and soon half your city prays to someone else's god. Faiths spread along friendships, and a steady trickle becomes a majority.

The rule VI

Turn 60. A rival founds the fourth religion, and a Standard map has room for five. You are behind in the Prophet race.

Pantheon (an early faith bonus). Your first 25 Faith (the yield spent on religion) (Standard speed) buys a pantheon, a small bonus usually tied to terrain. It stays all game, even if a foreign religion converts your cities.

Holy Sites (faith districts) and the Great Prophet (a famous prophet who founds a religion). The Holy Site, a district (a specialized city tile) unlocked by Astrology, earns Faith from adjacent Mountains, Woods, districts and natural wonders, and makes 1 Great Prophet point per turn, plus 1 per building in it (Shrine, then Temple). Enough points recruit a Great Prophet. With a pantheon, activate the Prophet on a Holy Site (or on Stonehenge, which grants a free one) to found a religion; that city becomes your Holy City.

The race. Religions are capped at half the map's default player count plus one: 5 on a Standard map, 4 on Small. Once the last Prophet is claimed, nobody else can found one. The Holy Site Prayers project adds Prophet points.

Beliefs. A religion holds four beliefs, one of each type. You pick two at founding: a Follower belief (helps every follower city, whoever owns it) plus a Founder (helps only you, scaling with spread), Worship (a tier-3 Holy Site building) or Enhancer (spread and defense) belief. An Apostle (a religious unit that spreads and defends a faith) can later add each of the other two. Each belief belongs to one religion only, so late founders get leftovers.

How cities convert. A city follows the religion held by more than half its citizens. Each city with a majority religion pushes pressure onto every city within 10 tiles: 1 point, doubled with a Holy Site, quadrupled in the Holy City (so 8 from a Holy City with a Holy Site). Trade routes carry a little too. Religious units speed this up; their Religious Strength is their strength in theological combat.

Religious unitNeedsReligious StrengthJob
MissionaryShrine1003 charges of Spread Religion; cuts rival pressure in the city by 10%
ApostleTemple1103 spreads (cuts rivals by 25%); starts theological combat; one promotion
GuruTemple90Heals itself and adjacent religious units
InquisitorTemple + an Apostle's "Launch Inquisition"lowestRemoves rival religions from your own cities (75% in Gathering Storm); +35 strength in your territory

Religious units are bought with Faith in a city with a Holy Site and a majority religion, at a price that rises with each purchase. A unit takes its city's current religion.

Theological combat. Apostles and Inquisitors (units that remove rival religions) can attack enemy religious units on adjacent tiles. Damage depends on the gap in Religious Strength. Terrain does not count; whose land you stand on does. A defender gets +5 in territory of a city following its religion and +15 in its Holy City's territory. Winning raises your religion's pressure nearby and lowers the loser's. Military units can destroy ("condemn") enemy religious units only at war, or when a World Congress (the game's recurring global vote) resolution allows it.

Religion and other systems. Your cities following the religion you founded get +3 Loyalty (how firmly a city stays yours) per turn; the rest get −3. A Religious Victory needs your religion to be the majority in more than half the cities of every civilization still in the game; city-states (minor powers that cannot win) don't count.

Source: Religion (Civ6), Great Prophet (Civ6), Belief (Civ6), Theological combat (Civ6), fandom

When to skip religion VI

Skipping religion costs little while nobody nears a Religious Victory. Skip it when rivals lead the Prophet race, your start lacks Mountains or Woods, or you plan an early war; your cities still get the Follower belief of whatever religion converts them. If one religion nears victory, defend: grow your cities (bigger ones take more charges to convert), buy Inquisitors (units that remove rival religions) if your religion has launched an Inquisition, or go to war so your military can condemn its missionaries. Take a pantheon regardless.

Sociology of religion

Network spread of religion

In the 1960s the sociologist John Lofland lived with a small group of American Unification Church members as they hunted for converts. Most efforts failed; most who joined came through personal ties, often family. His co-author Rodney Stark later did the math. Start from 1,000 Christians in the year 40, grow them 40% a decade, the Mormon rate over the past century, and by 300 you have 6,299,832. No mass conversions needed.

Network spread: religions grow mainly through existing ties of family, friends and trade, and a modest rate compounded over centuries turns a sect into a majority.

Open in the PDF, page 62

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Pressure from nearby cities and trade routesConversion through neighbors and trade partners
Missionaries and Apostles: fast, costly burstsMass preaching, which Stark thinks mattered less than ties

Where the map breaks

  • A Civ city follows one religion by majority. Real cities held several faiths side by side for centuries.
  • Stark credits women, fertility and care during epidemics for much of the growth. None of that has a mechanic.

Read next

  • Accessible: Stark, "Reconstructing the Rise of Christianity: The Role of Women"
  • Primary: Stark, The Rise of Christianity (1996)

Rodney Stark, The Rise of Christianity: A Sociologist Reconsiders History (1996)

The hard question: is a religion a gift when it arrives with an empire?

The game rewards converting every city you can reach. In British Africa until 1940, mission schools, mainly run by African converts, provided the bulk of education at extremely low cost. In Canada, for more than a century until 1997, churches ran government-funded residential schools. About 150,000 Indigenous children were placed in them. Many were forbidden to speak their own languages, and some were beaten for it. More than 4,000 student deaths have been documented, and in 2015 the country's Truth and Reconciliation Commission called the system cultural genocide.

The case for the missions: they gave British Africa most of its schooling, African converts ran most of the schools themselves, and a 2012 study found that the historic presence of Protestant missionaries explains about half the variation in democracy across Africa, Asia, Latin America and Oceania.

The case against: the same institutions decided which beliefs were allowed, and with colonial power behind them they could enforce it. A 2021 study re-ran the 2012 analysis with other measures of democracy and found no significant link. Schools can be given without taking a people's language.

Your call: can a gift be real when the receiver had no power to refuse it?

Lab: Can pressure beat a Missionary?

Predict first: which of the two cities will gain more followers from pressure alone in 15 turns, and will one Missionary charge beat all fifteen turns of pressure?

  1. Found a religion, then open the Religion lens.
  2. Pick two cities that don't follow your religion yet (a rival's or a city-state's): one about 6 tiles from your Holy City, one about 9 away. Note each one's followers.
  3. For 15 turns, send no religious units. Then spend one Missionary charge on the far city only.

Measure: followers gained per turn from pressure alone, against followers gained from one Missionary spread.

What you should see: pressure works slowly. Your Holy City pushes the same amount at 6 tiles as at 9, so any gap between the two cities comes from their size and the other cities pushing on them. A single spread usually moves the far city more in one turn than pressure did in fifteen.

If your result differs: a rival religion may be pushing on the same city, shrinking your share. A city that grows adds 50 pressure to its current majority religion, which may not be yours. Your other cities within 10 tiles push too, so the city nearer your core can pull ahead.

In the world: think of how you found your phone, your gym or a favorite show. Ask five friends whether they first heard of theirs from someone they know or from an ad. Stark's pattern predicts mostly people.

Check yourself

  1. Your Holy City has a Holy Site. Rival cities sit 7 and 12 tiles away. How much passive pressure does your Holy City push onto each per turn?

    Show answer

    8 onto the city 7 tiles away: 1, doubled for the Holy Site, quadrupled for the Holy City. None onto the one 12 tiles away, outside the 10-tile reach; only units or trade routes carry your faith there.

  2. A Missionary converts a city in a few turns; passive pressure takes far longer. Which is closer to how Stark says real religions grew, and why?

    Show answer

    Passive pressure. Stark argues faiths grew mainly through existing ties among neighbors, family and trading partners, at a steady compounding rate. That is pressure between nearby cities and along trade routes. Mass preaching, the Missionary's burst, mattered less.

  3. A new running club has ten members and a small budget. Should it pay for posters or reward members who bring a friend? Use network spread.

    Show answer

    Reward members who bring friends. Network spread says new members arrive mostly through existing ties, so each member's friends are the likeliest recruits. Posters are the broadcast, which Stark found mattered less than personal ties.

In the game: decide early whether to race for a Great Prophet. If a rival is ahead, keep your pantheon and build something else.

In the world: ideas travel along personal ties more than through broadcasts, so who knows whom often beats who says what.

Trade Routes

A Trader is the quietest unit in the game and binds you tightest to other players. The gain that makes a route pay makes cutting it hurt.

The rule VI

Turn 45. You have one Trader (the unit that runs a trade route) and one slot. Feed the capital, grow a new city or take a neighbor's Gold. The choice also decides who can hurt you later.

Traders and capacity. The Foreign Trade (a step on the culture tree) civic unlocks the Trader unit (40 Production (the yield that builds units and buildings) to start, rising with each one you build) and gives you a trading capacity (routes you can run at once) of 1. Each city with a Market or a Lighthouse adds 1; a city with both still adds only 1. Some wonders, Great Merchants (famous merchants you recruit for trade bonuses) and civ abilities add more.

Range and duration. A land route reaches 15 tiles; a sea route 30, once you have Celestial Navigation (a technology). The first time a route to a city finishes, you get a trading post in both the origin and the destination. A route through a city with your trading post gets its full range again from there, and posts along the way add Gold. A route runs about 20 turns on Standard speed; in Gathering Storm (the second Civ VI expansion) the Medieval, Industrial and Information Eras add 10, 20 and then 30.

Roads. A Trader builds a road on every land tile it crosses. Builders (units that build farms, mines and the like) can't make roads, so early on Traders are your road crews.

What a route pays. The origin city collects the yield, which depends on the districts (specialized city tiles that hold buildings) in the destination, so a route grows richer as that city builds.

District in destinationDomestic route (to your own city)International route (to a rival or city-state)
City Center1 Food, 1 Production3 Gold
Commercial Hub, Harbor1 Production3 Gold
Campus1 Food1 Science
Holy Site1 Food1 Faith
Theater Square1 Food1 Culture
Encampment, Industrial Zone1 Production1 Production

In Gathering Storm, crossing water, canals or railroads can raise a route's base Gold by up to 100%.

Side effects. An international route to another civ gives you one level of diplomatic visibility with them (you see more of their affairs), +25% Tourism toward them, and +2 to their opinion of you. War cancels routes with the enemy. Enemy units and barbarians can plunder a Trader: it dies and they take Gold.

Source: Trade route (Civ6), Trading post (Civ6), Roads (Civ6), fandom; yields from game data (see ledger)

Domestic or international VI

A route from your capital to a new city feeds the capital. To feed the young city, move the Trader there first ("Transfer to another city"), then send it to your most developed city. Use international routes for Gold and city-state (a minor power that cannot win) quests (community consensus on CivFanatics, though the best-known guides predate the expansions).

Economics

Comparative advantage

David Ricardo made his fortune financing government borrowing, then retired and wrote economics. In 1817 he set a puzzle. Portugal can make a year's cloth with 90 men and its wine with 80; England needs 100 and 120. Yet both gain if Portugal makes only wine and England only cloth, "notwithstanding that the commodity imported by Portugal could be produced there with less labour than in England".

Comparative advantage: two parties both gain from trade when each makes what it gives up least to make, even if one of them is better at everything.

Open in the PDF, page 65

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
An international route pays in the partner's specialty (Science from a Campus)Importing what your partner makes cheaply
Swapping a spare luxury for one you lack; both civs gain AmenitiesExchange where each side gives up little

Where the map breaks

  • Ricardo's gains come through prices. A Civ route has none; it just adds yields to the sending city.
  • Real trade has losers, such as workers in shrinking industries. The two-good model and the game both ignore them.

Read next

  • Accessible: CEPR VoxEU, "The principle of comparative advantage: 200 years"
  • Primary: Ricardo 1817, chapter VII

David Ricardo, On the Principles of Political Economy and Taxation (1817), chapter VII

International relations

Complex interdependence

In October 1973 Arab oil exporters embargoed the United States, the Netherlands, Britain and others that had backed Israel in that month's war. By March 1974, when it ended, oil had gone from $3 a barrel to nearly $12, and the shock helped push the world into recession. The exporters lost little, selling less oil at nearly four times the price. The importers had no quick substitute.

Complex interdependence: when states are tied by many channels and force is too costly, power comes from uneven dependence. Whoever would lose less from cutting the ties holds the leverage.

Open in the PDF, page 66

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
War cancels every route between you; the side with more routes loses moreVulnerability: the cost of cutting ties
Routes add opinion (+2), visibility and TourismMany channels beyond the state

Where the map breaks

  • Civ AIs will attack their biggest trade partner. Lost trade barely enters their war decision.
  • Keohane and Nye wrote about pairs like the US and Canada, where force was off the table. In Civ it never is.

Read next

  • Primary: Keohane and Nye 1977, chapters 1–2

Robert O. Keohane and Joseph S. Nye, Power and Interdependence: World Politics in Transition (1977)

The hard question: should you trade with a state you believe is in the wrong?

In the game a trade partner's politics never matter. In 1963 Egon Bahr, later the architect of Willy Brandt's opening to the East, proposed "Wandel durch Annäherung", change through rapprochement. Its offspring, "Wandel durch Handel", change through trade, bet that commerce would change autocracies. From 2011 Nord Stream 1 piped Russian gas under the Baltic to Germany. In February 2022, before Russia invaded Ukraine, 55% of Germany's gas imports came from Russia, and Chancellor Olaf Scholz suspended approval of Nord Stream 2. In April President Frank-Walter Steinmeier said, in Reuters' translation: "My adherence to Nord Stream 2 was clearly a mistake." By September Russia had shut Nord Stream 1.

The case for trading: Soviet gas had flowed west since 1973, through the Cold War. In 2007 Steinmeier wrote that Europe could find lasting peace only with Russia. After the invasion Scholz argued that Europe's energy for "heat generation, mobility, electricity supply and industry cannot be secured in any other way at the moment" (AFP's translation).

The case against: the dependence ran one way. Gas money funded the state that invaded, and when the tie was cut it was Germany that scrambled. Trade changed the buyer more than the seller.

Your call: how much would a trade have to pay before you accepted whom it strengthens?

Lab: Which job pays your Trader best?

Predict first: which route will pay more total yield per turn, the domestic or the international one, and which will help your new city grow faster?

  1. Move your first Trader to your newest city ("Transfer to another city"), then send it on a domestic route to your capital. The new city, as the origin, collects the yields. Note them in the route list.
  2. When it ends, send the same Trader on the best international route on offer. Note its yields.
  3. Track the new city's population and build times across both periods.

Measure: Food plus Production per turn from the domestic route against Gold per turn from the international one.

What you should see: the domestic route shortens the new city's build queue noticeably; the international route earns more total yield but leaves the young city where it was.

If your result differs: the destination's districts set the pay, so a capital with few districts pays little. Trading posts along the way add Gold, and in Gathering Storm crossing water or railroads can raise base Gold by up to 100%. A plundered Trader ends its route early.

In the world: watch two roommates split chores. The faster cook cooks and the other cleans, even if the cook is also faster at cleaning, and both finish sooner. That is Ricardo's rule in a shared flat.

Check yourself

  1. Your new city can send its Trader to your capital (City Center, Campus) or to a rival city (City Center, Commercial Hub). From the district table, what does each route pay?

    Show answer

    Domestic: 2 Food and 1 Production (City Center 1 and 1, Campus 1 Food). International: 6 Gold, 3 per district. Your new city collects either; the domestic route grows and builds it, the international one pays cash.

  2. Most of your routes run to one rival, and it threatens war. Which concept says who holds the leverage, and where does the game's version break?

    Show answer

    Complex interdependence: whoever loses more if the ties are cut is weaker. War cancels every route between you, so you lose more. The break: Civ AIs barely weigh lost trade, so your routes will not deter them.

  3. A baker makes better bread and slightly better coffee than the café next door. Why might she still buy her coffee from the café?

    Show answer

    Comparative advantage. Every hour she spends on coffee is an hour of bread lost, and bread is where her lead is biggest. If she bakes and the café brews, together they make more, and trade lets both gain.

In the game: an idle trade slot is lost yield. Keep capacity full, and base Traders in the city you want to grow.

In the world: trade pays because partners differ, and the same gain makes cutting the tie expensive for whoever needs it more.

City-States and Envoys

Twelve small neighbors can't win the game, but six well-placed envoys can tip it your way. Real small states choose between the giant next door and the one far away.

The rule VI

Turn 25. Your Scout (a fast unit for exploring) meets a Scientific city-state (a minor power that cannot win) first, and the free envoy pays +2 Science in your capital. Three would pay in every Library.

What a city-state is. A one-city AI minor power that never expands or competes to win. A Standard map has 12 by default, of six types.

Envoys (tokens of influence sent to city-states). Envoys are tokens of influence you place in the City-States screen. They never decay. You earn them from Influence (a diplomacy currency) Points (your government sets the rate: a tier-1 government like Classical Republic makes 3 per turn, and 100 points buy one envoy), from being the first civ to meet a city-state (+1 there), from its quests (+1 there), and from several civics. Declaring war on a city-state deletes all your envoys in it.

Thresholds. Bonuses depend only on how many envoys you have there.

Type1 envoy3 envoys6 envoys
Scientific+2 Science in capital+2 Science in every Library+2 more in every University
Cultural+2 Culture in capital+2 Culture in every Amphitheater+2 more in every Art or Archaeological Museum
Religious+2 Faith in capital+2 Faith in every Shrine+2 more in every Temple
Trade+4 Gold in capital+2 Gold in every Market and Lighthouse+2 more in every Bank and Shipyard
Industrial+2 Production in capital (wonders, buildings, districts)+2 in every city with a Workshop+2 more with a Factory
Militaristic+2 Production in capital (units)+2 in every city with a Barracks or Stable+2 more with an Armory

Since Rise & Fall the 3- and 6-envoy bonuses need those buildings. The paid Ethiopia Pack changes this table.

Suzerain (the player who controls a city-state). The civ with at least 3 envoys and strictly more than anyone else is the city-state's suzerain. A tie means nobody is. The suzerain gets the city-state's unique bonus, its luxury and strategic resources, vision around it, the right to build improvements in its land, and in Gathering Storm (the second Civ VI expansion) +1 Diplomatic Favor (a currency for World Congress votes, covered in Diplomacy) per turn. The city-state follows its suzerain into war and peace.

Levying. A suzerain can pay Gold to take control of the city-state's whole army for 30 turns (Standard speed). The price equals the units' total Production (the yield that builds units and buildings) cost, so 5 Warriors and a Swordsman cost 290 Gold. You pay their upkeep, and you lose them early if you lose suzerainty.

Source: City-state (Civ6), Envoy (Civ6), Suzerain (Civ6), fandom

Spread or stack VI

Early on, spread your envoys: one in each city-state (a minor power that cannot win) pays its capital bonus at once. Later, stack them where a suzerain (the player who controls a city-state) bonus fits your plan and the 3/6 thresholds match buildings you will have. A one-envoy lead can vanish in a turn.

International relations

Balancing vs bandwagoning

In June 1940 France fell to Germany. Hungary joined Germany's Tripartite Pact on 20 November 1940, Romania three days later, hoping for protection against the Soviet Union, and Slovakia the day after. Bulgaria, unable to resist by force, signed in March 1941. Britain, with the sea in between and American aid coming, fought on. Weak and alone, you join the danger. With options, you resist it.

Balancing vs bandwagoning: states usually ally against the power they fear most; weak, isolated states more often join it. Fear depends on power, nearness, offensive capability and intentions.

Open in the PDF, page 69

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Envoy bidding between you and a rival over one city-stateGreat powers competing for small clients
Suzerain's city-state joins its wars; an attacker hands the suzerain a Protectorate WarPatron and client: protection for alignment

Where the map breaks

  • City-states don't choose. Envoy (a token of influence sent to a city-state) counts decide, so threat and intentions play no part.
  • AIs balance against score, so a peaceful culture leader can draw the same hostility as a conqueror.
  • Walt's evidence comes mostly from Middle East alliances of 1955–79, which shifted slowly. Civ alliances form in one turn.

Read next

  • Accessible: Walt, "Alliance Formation and the Balance of World Power", International Security 9(4) (1985)
  • Primary: Walt 1987, chapter 2

Stephen M. Walt, The Origins of Alliances (1987)

The hard question: should a small state fight a giant it cannot beat?

In the game a city-state never resists; it follows its suzerain. On the evening of 2 August 1914, Germany demanded free passage through Belgium for its army, with twelve hours to reply. An 1839 treaty guaranteed Belgian neutrality. Belgium refused, saying that to accept would sacrifice the nation's honour and betray its duty to Europe. Germany invaded on 4 August. That evening its chancellor told the British ambassador, by the ambassador's account, that Britain was going to war "just for a scrap of paper", and that night it did. The Liège forts held for eleven days; the Germans had expected two. Germans shot 5,500 to 6,500 Belgian and French civilians by November, burned Leuven's university library and occupied more than 95% of the country for four years.

The case for fighting: a treaty is worth only what someone will defend. Belgium's refusal brought Britain in and cost Germany days it had not planned to lose.

The case against: Belgium could not win, and its people paid in executed civilians, a burned library and four years of occupation. Honor is cheap for the ministers who invoke it and dear for those who live along the road.

Your call: if refusing will cost lives and may not change the outcome, is it still right to refuse?

Lab: Can you win a bidding war for a city-state?

Predict first: who will hold the title after 30 turns, and how many envoys will each side have spent by then?

  1. Pick a contested city-state where you and one AI both hold envoys.
  2. Each time you or the AI adds an envoy there, write the turn and the new counts.
  3. Keep it up for 30 turns or until one side gives up.

Measure: envoys each side spent to hold the title, and the bonus you earned while holding it.

What you should see: a bidding war that can eat many envoys; often the same envoys would have bought more spread across uncontested city-states.
