If your result differs: the AI earns envoys from quests and civics as well as Influence, so it can jump ahead fast. A tie leaves nobody suzerain. From Rise & Fall on, a spy's Fabricate Scandal removes envoys of whoever leads there.

In the world: watch the last minutes of an online auction with two keen bidders. The price often ends well above what either first planned to pay. An envoy race overspends the same way.

Check yourself

  1. You and a rival each have 3 envoys in a Scientific city-state, and you have 2 Libraries. What do you get, and who is suzerain?

    Show answer

    +2 Science in your capital (1 envoy) plus +2 in each Library (3 envoys), so +6. Nobody is suzerain, because a tie means no suzerain. A fourth envoy would win you the title.

  2. One AI leads on score with a peaceful culture game; another, smaller, masses troops next door. Which does Walt say a small state should fear more, and whom do Civ AIs target?

    Show answer

    Walt: the neighbor. Threat depends on power, nearness, offensive capability and intentions, not size alone. Civ AIs balance against score instead, so the peaceful leader can draw as much hostility as a conqueror. That is where the map breaks.

  3. A small country sits between a large distant power and a smaller neighbor massing troops on its border. What does Walt predict it will do, and when might it bandwagon instead?

    Show answer

    Balance: line up with the distant power against the nearby threat, since nearness, offensive capability and intentions drive fear. If it is weak and isolated, with no help in reach, it is likelier to bandwagon with the neighbor.

In the game: one envoy everywhere first, then concentrate where a suzerain bonus changes your plan.

In the world: small states choose patrons by threat as much as by power, so the biggest player does not automatically win them.

Loyalty and Ages

A city you founded, fed and walled declares independence, though nothing attacked it. Its people felt closer to someone else. Nations form that way, and states break.

Loyalty VI

Every city has a Loyalty (how firmly a city stays yours) score from 0 to 100.

LoyaltyStateGrowthAll yields
76–100Loyalnormalnormal
51–75Wavering75%−25%
26–50Disloyal25%−50%
1–25Unrestnone−100%

Pressure from nearby citizens. Every citizen within 9 tiles pushes Loyalty toward its own civ, weaker with distance (10 minus the distance, per citizen). Your Age multiplies your citizens' push: 1.5 in a Golden or Heroic Age, 1 in a Normal Age, 0.5 in a Dark Age (a penalty era after low Era Score). Your push is set against the foreign push, capped at ±20 per turn.

Other sources. Any assigned Governor: +8. Happy or Ecstatic citizens (a city with spare Amenities): +3 or +6; Displeased or Unhappy: −3 or −6. Following the religion you founded: +3; not following it: −3. A Monument: +1. An occupied city with no garrison: −5. Several policy cards (swappable bonuses slotted into your government) add more.

Free cities. A city whose Loyalty hits 0 while still falling declares independence and becomes a Free City. It is hostile to everyone and trains units, but it still feels pressure. When some civ's pressure wins, the city asks to join it whole, so you can "flip" a rival's city without firing a shot.

Source: Loyalty (Civ6), fandom

Ages and Era Score VI

World eras. The world shares one era, which advances with the civs' overall progress after a 10-turn warning.

Era Score (points for historic moments that set your next era). During each era you earn Era Score from historic moments: meeting a civ, clearing a barbarian camp and more. When the world era changes, your score sets your next Age. (A Dedication, in the table, is an era-long goal; see below.)

AgeHow you get itEffect
DarkBelow the Dark Age thresholdCitizens exert 0.5 Loyalty pressure; special Dark Age policy cards; next threshold lowered
NormalBetween the two thresholdsPressure 1; your Dedication's basic effect
GoldenAt or above the Golden Age thresholdPressure 1.5; your Dedication's powerful Golden version; next thresholds raised
HeroicGolden threshold reached straight after a Dark AgeGolden Age pressure and three Golden Dedications

Thresholds. In Gathering Storm (the second Civ VI expansion) the base thresholds are 14 Era Score for a Normal Age and 28 for a Golden Age (a bonus era earned with high Era Score). Rise & Fall used 12 and 24. Both rise by 1 per city you own, by 5 for each earlier Golden Age, and fall by 5 for each earlier Dark Age. Score beyond the Golden threshold is wasted. The ring around the End Turn button shows your live numbers.

Dedications (bonuses picked when a new age or era begins). At each new era you pick a Dedication. In a Normal or Dark Age it earns Era Score for an activity (Monumentality: +1 per specialty district you build). In a Golden Age it becomes a bonus: Golden Monumentality lets you buy civilian units with Faith (the yield spent on religion), makes Settler (the unit that founds a city) and Builder (a unit that builds farms, mines and the like) purchases 30% cheaper and gives Builders +2 movement.

Source: Age (Civ6), Era (Civ6), fandom; thresholds from game data (see ledger)

A Dark Age (0.5) facing a Golden neighbor (1.5) means their citizens push three times as hard. Check both Ages before settling near a rival.

Political sociology

Imagined communities

On 28 October 1928, in Batavia, young delegates from clubs like Jong Java pledged one motherland, one nation and one language of unity, Indonesian. In 1945 it was the native language of about 5% of the people. Javanese, the mother tongue of 42 to 48%, was passed over so that no group would own the nation. A trade language native to few taught millions of strangers to say "we".

Imagined community: a nation whose members will never meet most of the others yet feel they are one people, a feeling Anderson says mass-printed books and newspapers spread ("print capitalism").

Open in the PDF, page 72

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Loyalty pressure from nearby citizens of your civIdentification with a wider "us"
Golden and Dark Ages scale that pressureNational pride and national doubt

Where the map breaks

  • Anderson's community lives in people's heads. Civ's Loyalty (how firmly a city stays yours) is a distance-weighted head count.
  • Print capitalism has no mechanic. In Civ, Printing is just a tech.

Read next

  • Accessible: Verso, "The Origins of National Consciousness" (excerpt)
  • Primary: Anderson 1983, chapters 1–3

Benedict Anderson, Imagined Communities: Reflections on the Origin and Spread of Nationalism (1983)

Political economy

Exit, voice and loyalty

On 19 August 1989 more than 600 East Germans ran through a gate in Hungary's border fence, and in September Hungary let them all cross. In Albert Hirschman's 1970 book, leaving and protesting were alternatives. In 1989 both surged at once: on 9 October 70,000 marched in Leipzig chanting "Wir sind das Volk", we are the people, and on 9 November the Berlin Wall opened.

Exit, voice and loyalty: in a declining firm, party or state, members can leave (exit) or complain and organize (voice). Loyalty delays exit and gives voice time; easy exit can drain voice.

Open in the PDF, page 73

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
A city at 0 Loyalty becomes a Free City and may join a rivalExit
Governors, Amenities and Monuments raise LoyaltyRepairs that keep members in

Where the map breaks

  • Civ citizens have no voice. Disloyalty is a number you manage, and nobody marches.
  • Exit starving voice, as when the most demanding parents leave public schools, has no game version.

Read next

  • Primary: Hirschman 1970, chapters 1–3

Albert O. Hirschman, Exit, Voice, and Loyalty: Responses to Decline in Firms, Organizations, and States (1970)

Lab: Can a Governor hold a border city?

Predict first: write the city's Loyalty per turn you expect once the Governor (a named official assigned to a city) arrives, and guess whether the next Age change will move it more or less than the Governor did.

  1. Pick your city closest to a rival. Hover its Loyalty bar and copy every line of the breakdown.
  2. Assign a Governor there and note the change next turn.
  3. After the next era change, note both civs' new Ages and the new breakdown.

Measure: Loyalty per turn before the Governor, after the Governor, and after the Age changes.

What you should see: the Governor adds a flat +8; the Age change moves the citizen-pressure line, sometimes by more than the Governor did.

If your result differs: citizen pressure is capped at ±20 per turn, so a big Age swing can hit the cap. Amenities (city contentment from luxuries and entertainment) sliding from Happy to Displeased moves Loyalty by 6. A city growing, or a new one settled, within 9 tiles changes the pressure line too.

In the world: people in a border town often shop, watch TV and work across the line. Ask someone from a border region which side they feel part of, and what pulls them that way.

Check yourself

  1. Your border city shows 60 Loyalty. What is that costing it, and which single flat bonus in this lesson adds the most Loyalty per turn?

    Show answer

    60 is Wavering: growth at 75% and all yields −25%. A Governor adds +8, the biggest flat source listed; Ecstatic citizens add +6. Growing your nearby cities also raises the pressure line.

  2. A Disloyal city declares independence and later joins your rival. In Hirschman's terms, what did its people use, and which response did the game never offer them?

    Show answer

    Exit: the city left as a Free City and joined another civ. Voice, complaining or bargaining before leaving, has no mechanic. Disloyalty is a number you manage, not a protest you can negotiate with.

  3. A gym raises prices and cuts classes. Some members cancel at once; others complain to the manager. Which group is likelier to get the classes back, and what keeps them there?

    Show answer

    The complainers. Voice tells the manager what is wrong and gives time to fix it. Loyalty, such as friendships at the gym, delays exit and gives voice time to work. If leaving is too easy, that pressure drains away.

In the game: Loyalty is a population map. Keep more of your citizens near a city than your rival does, and it stays yours.

In the world: a state holds people through shared identity and a fair hearing. Take both away and they leave.

Diplomacy and the World Congress

An AI leader smiles, signs a friendship and votes against you at the next Congress. Every opinion is a number you can read, and the oldest rules of cooperation apply.

Relationships and agendas VI

Agendas. Every AI leader has a public leader agenda based on the historical figure and a random hidden agenda; Gathering Storm (the second Civ VI expansion) adds a second hidden one later. Pleasing an agenda raises the leader's opinion of you. Diplomatic visibility reveals hidden ones.

Visibility, delegations and embassies. You raise diplomatic visibility, how much you see of a rival's affairs, with a delegation (25 Gold, +3 opinion), later replaced by an embassy once you have the Diplomatic Service (a step on the culture tree) civic (50 Gold, +5 opinion), with an international trade route, and with a spy's Listening Post. A leader who dislikes you refuses both.

Opinion. The diplomacy screen lists every modifier as a number. Plus: shared government, trade routes, open borders, gifts, kept promises. Minus: settling near them, troops on their border, broken or refused promises, caught spies, and being close to winning.

Source: Diplomacy (Civ6), Agenda (Civ6), fandom

Friendship, alliances and grievances VI

Declared friendship is possible with a leader whose attitude is Friendly. It lasts 30 turns, bans war and denunciations between you, and is the gate to an alliance. Denouncing a leader is the opposite: a public hostile statement that lasts 30 turns, expels delegations, and five turns later lets you declare a Formal War (a war with advance warning, which angers other civs less than a Surprise War).

Alliances need the Civil Service (a step on the culture tree) civic and declared friendship. Each includes a defensive pact (an attack on one brings in the other) and grows through three levels as it earns alliance points over time, faster with trade routes between you. In Gathering Storm each alliance also earns +1 Diplomatic Favor (a currency spent in the World Congress) per turn per level.

AllianceLevel 1 exampleLevel 3
Research+2 Science on your routes to your allyFaster research on techs one of you already has
Military+5 combat strength against your shared enemiesNew units start with a free promotion
Economic+4 Gold on your routes to your allyShare each other's suzerain bonuses
CulturalNo Loyalty pressure between your citiesGain 20% of your ally's city Tourism and 10% of its city Culture
ReligiousNo religious pressure on each other's cities; +2 Faith on routes to your ally+1 Faith per citizen of yours following your ally's religion

Grievances (a running record of who wronged whom) (Gathering Storm): each pair of civs keeps a score of who has wronged whom. A Surprise War adds 150 grievances against you, a Formal War 100, a denunciation 25, and occupying, keeping or razing cities adds more. A casus belli (a recognized reason for war) lowers the cost; see Why wars start. Your victim's allies gain half its grievances against you. Grievances fade each turn you are at peace, by 10 per turn in the Ancient Era down to 2 in the Future Era, so a late war is remembered far longer. Liberating cities cuts them at once.

Source: Alliance (Civ6), Grievances (Civ6), Casus belli (Civ6), fandom; decay rates from game data (see ledger)

Diplomatic Favor and the World Congress VI

Diplomatic Favor (Gathering Storm) is a currency for votes. You earn it per turn from your government (1 per government tier, so Monarchy gives 2 and Democracy 3), from each city-state (a minor power that cannot win) you are suzerain (the player who controls a city-state) of (+1), from alliances, and in lump sums from liberating cities. You lose it per turn for large grievances held against you (up to −10), for emitting well above the world's average CO2 (−1 per 3 points over, up to −20) and for each original capital you hold that isn't yours (−5).

Regular sessions. The World Congress (the game's recurring global vote) first meets after the world enters the Medieval Era, then every 30 turns. Each session puts two resolutions to the vote. Each has two opposite outcomes and a target (a district type, a luxury, a civ), and lasts until the next session. Every civ gets one free vote per resolution; extra votes cost 10, then 20, then 30 Favor and so on. The outcome with the most votes wins, then the target with the most votes among its backers. Voters who backed the winning outcome and target earn 1 Diplomatic Victory point. From the Modern Era a third resolution awards or removes 2 Diplomatic Victory points from one leader. You win a Diplomatic Victory at 20 points.

Special sessions and emergencies. When a civ commits a serious act, such as conquering a city-state, converting another religion's Holy City or using a nuclear weapon, an affected civ can spend 30 Favor to call a special session, at least 15 turns after the last one. If the emergency passes, everyone who voted for it shares visibility and open borders and goes to war with the target without grievances (a religious emergency is fought with religious units instead). Most last 30 turns, and success pays the members in Favor.

Source: Diplomatic Favor (Civ6), World Congress (Civ6), Emergency (Civ6), fandom

International relations

Realpolitik

In 1833 about fifty students stormed Frankfurt's main guardhouse to start a German revolution, and failed. One, Ludwig von Rochau, was sentenced to life and escaped into ten years of exile. In 1849 an elected German parliament chose the King of Prussia as emperor; he refused, and that June soldiers occupied what was left of the parliament. In 1853 Rochau wrote, in a German Historical Institute translation: "To rule is to exercise power, and only those who have power can exercise it." Prussian power then united Germany in 1866–71.

Realpolitik: politics follows real power the way nature follows physics; ideals win only when force backs them. Rochau, a liberal, meant it as advice about means and kept his goals.

Open in the PDF, page 76

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
A strong civ can make demands of an Unfriendly one and often gets themPower sets terms
Favor buys votes, and the richest civ steers the CongressInstitutions reflect the power behind them

Where the map breaks

  • Rochau counted public opinion and ideas as forces in their own right. Civ has no public opinion.
  • The popular meaning, "the ends justify the means", reverses much of what Rochau wrote.

Read next

  • Accessible: John Bew, Realpolitik: A History (2016), chapter 1
  • Primary: Rochau 1853, English extracts at German History in Documents

Ludwig August von Rochau, Grundsätze der Realpolitik (1853)

Game theory

Repeated games and the evolution of cooperation

Robert Axelrod, a political scientist at Michigan, invited experts in game theory, economics and mathematics to a tournament. Each program played the prisoner's dilemma against every other, 200 rounds a pair, though in any single round cheating pays best. The winner was a very simple entry from Anatol Rapoport: cooperate first, then copy the other side's last move. In a second tournament Tit for Tat won again.

Repeated games: when the same players meet in a prisoner's dilemma again and again with no known last round, cooperation can last if each reciprocates: start nice, punish betrayal, forgive.

Open in the PDF, page 76

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
A broken promise costs 100 grievances and hands the victim a casus belliPunishing defection
Grievances decay each turn of peaceForgiveness

Where the map breaks

  • Many Civ games have a known last turn, and theory says cooperation unravels as it approaches.
  • Axelrod's players were equals. A Civ superpower can defect and shrug off the punishment.

Read next

  • Primary: Axelrod and W. D. Hamilton, "The Evolution of Cooperation", Science 211 (1981)

Robert Axelrod, The Evolution of Cooperation (1984)

Social choice

Arrow's impossibility theorem

In 1785 the Marquis de Condorcet, a French mathematician, found a flaw in majority voting. With three options, majorities can prefer A to B, B to C and C to A, leaving no coherent first choice. Ramon Llull, a Catalan philosopher, had found it in the thirteenth century, but his work was lost until the twenty-first. In 1950 Kenneth Arrow proved the flaw was general: no ranked voting rule meets a few mild fairness conditions at once.

Arrow's theorem: with three or more options, no ranked voting rule can always turn individual rankings into a group ranking while allowing any rankings, respecting unanimity, ignoring irrelevant options and having no dictator.

Open in the PDF, page 77

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Votes split across targets of the same outcomeVote splitting between similar options
A Favor-rich civ outvotes everyoneArrow's "dictator", a voter whose ranking always wins, here bought with Favor

Where the map breaks

  • Arrow's theorem is about full rankings. The Congress only picks one winner, a narrower problem.
  • Buying votes with Favor (a currency spent in the World Congress) measures how much you care, which Arrow's ranked framework leaves out.

Read next

  • Accessible: Amartya Sen, "The Possibility of Social Choice", Nobel lecture (1998)

Kenneth J. Arrow, "A Difficulty in the Concept of Social Welfare," Journal of Political Economy 58(4) (1950); expanded as Social Choice and Individual Values (1951)

The hard question: should you ally with a monster to stop a worse one?

In the game nobody checks an ally's record. The evening before Germany invaded the Soviet Union in June 1941, Winston Churchill told his private secretary, John Colville, that if Hitler invaded Hell he would make at least a favourable reference to the devil in the House of Commons. The next day he broadcast that no one had opposed Communism more consistently, but "all this fades away before the spectacle which is now unfolding." In April 1943 Germany announced mass graves at Katyn. They held some of the nearly 22,000 Poles shot in 1940 by the Soviet secret police on an order Stalin signed. Britain pressed the Poles to drop their call for a Red Cross inquiry. Moscow admitted the killings only in 1990.

The case for the alliance: about 8.7 million Soviet soldiers died in the war, and without them it might have been lost or lasted years longer. A leader's first duty is to the people he can still save.

The case against: the ally was also a mass murderer. Silence about Katyn was paid for first by Poles, and victory left Poland and its neighbors in the Soviet bloc until 1989.

Your call: which of your values would you set aside for a partner you needed, and who would pay?

Lab: Can you buy the World Congress?

Predict first: will your 5 votes (1 free, 4 bought) carry the resolution, and how many votes will the winning option get?

  1. Save up at least 100 Favor before a regular session.
  2. Pick one resolution where you care about the outcome. Put 4 extra votes on it (10 + 20 + 30 + 40 = 100 Favor). On the other, use only your free vote.
  3. Note the totals the session reports for each option.

Measure: the votes the winning option needed, and your Favor refunded.

What you should see: rising vote prices make concentration expensive; a small group of AIs voting alike often outweighs one big spender.

If your result differs: votes for one outcome add up across all its targets, so AIs split over targets can still beat you. Ties go to whoever committed more Favor. The refund depends on the result: none if your outcome and target won, half if only your outcome won, all if your outcome lost.

In the world: at any club or council vote, count the winners' backers. A small group voting together usually beats one keen member, which is why people agree a joint line before the meeting.

Check yourself

  1. You hold 50 Favor at a World Congress session. How many votes can you put on one resolution, and what do you earn if your outcome and target both win?

    Show answer

    3: the free vote, then extras at 10 and 20 Favor (30 spent). The next costs 30, more than the 20 left. A full win earns 1 Diplomatic Victory point and no Favor refund.

  2. You promised an AI not to settle near it, and a great city site appears there. What does Axelrod say decides whether breaking the promise pays, and what does Civ charge?

    Show answer

    Whether the future outweighs the one-time gain: cooperation lasts when play repeats with no known end. Civ charges 100 grievances and hands the victim a casus belli, and grievances fade only during peace.

  3. A club of 9 picks a trip by plurality: 4 vote beach, 3 mountains, 2 city. All 5 others rank beach last. What went wrong, and what would a runoff pick?

    Show answer

    Vote splitting: plurality chose the trip most members ranked last. A runoff between the top two picks the mountains, 5 to 4. Arrow proved no ranked rule meets every fair condition at once, so the rule shapes the winner.

In the game: AI opinion is a ledger you can read. Work the numbers up before you need a friend.

In the world: cooperation lasts when the parties expect to meet again, and every voting rule shapes which choice wins.

Espionage

Your rival's Campus (the science district) is about to hand you a free Eureka (a 40% research discount for a related task), if your spy gets out alive. Markets, deals and wars all turn on who knows what.

The rule VI

Spies. The Diplomatic Service (a step on the culture tree) civic (Renaissance Era) lets you train your first Spy (225 Production (the yield that builds units and buildings) to start; can't be bought with Gold). Later civics raise your spy capacity, and the Intelligence Agency building in the Government Plaza district (a specialized city tile that holds buildings) adds one more, for a usual maximum of 6. Spies don't walk the map; you pick a city and they travel there.

Offensive missions. What you can do depends on the districts in the target city.

MissionTargetEffect
Listening PostCity CenterRaises diplomatic visibility while it runs; can't fail
Gain SourcesCity CenterYour spies in that city act 2 levels higher for 24 turns; can't fail
Siphon FundsCommercial HubSteals Gold
Steal Tech BoostCampusGives you the Eureka (tech boost) for a tech they know and you don't
Great Work HeistTheater SquareSteals a Great Work
Sabotage ProductionIndustrial ZonePillages its buildings
Disrupt RocketrySpaceportPillages it, pausing their space projects
Foment UnrestCity CenterCuts the city's Loyalty (Rise & Fall on)
Neutralize GovernorCity CenterTakes their Governor out for several turns (Rise & Fall on)
Fabricate ScandalCity-stateRemoves envoys of the leading civ there (Rise & Fall on)
Breach DamDamCauses a flood (Gathering Storm)

Defense. A spy left in one of your own cities on Counterspy guards one district and every district next to it, and makes enemy spies there much more likely to be caught.

What decides success. The mission briefing shows the odds before you commit. The odds rise with your spy's level (Recruit, Agent, Secret Agent, Master Spy; each success can earn a level and a promotion chosen from three), with matching promotions (Technologist counts as 2 levels higher when stealing boosts), with Gain Sources and with the Intelligence Agency. They fall when an enemy counterspy is in or next to the target district.

Getting caught. A discovered spy must escape; faster exits are riskier. The victim gains a casus belli (a justification for war) against you, asks you to stop, and in Gathering Storm (the second Civ VI expansion) accrues grievances.

Source: Espionage (Civ6), Spy (Civ6), fandom

Economics

Information asymmetry

In 1967 George Akerlof, a Berkeley economist, sent off a paper about used cars. If buyers can't tell good cars from bad, they offer an average price, owners of good cars won't sell, and the market fills with "lemons". Two journals rejected it as trivial. A third explained why he was wrong; the killer, Akerlof recalled, was that "if this paper was correct, economics would be different." He later shared the 2001 Nobel Prize.

Information asymmetry: when one side knows more about quality, the uninformed side pays an average price, good sellers withdraw and quality falls. Inspection, warranties and signals close the gap.

Open in the PDF, page 80

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Hidden agendas and diplomatic visibility levelsNot knowing the other side's real preferences
Listening Posts, delegations, trade routesPaying to inspect

Where the map breaks

  • Akerlof's result needs a market with many sellers. Civ diplomacy is mostly one-on-one bargaining.
  • The game's spies never bring back false information. Real intelligence is often wrong.

Read next

  • Accessible: Akerlof, "Writing 'The Market for Lemons': A Personal and Interpretive Essay"

George A. Akerlof, "The Market for 'Lemons': Quality Uncertainty and the Market Mechanism", Quarterly Journal of Economics (1970)

The hard question: may you deceive a friend to protect a secret source?

In the game you can spy on allies, and nobody minds. In January 1917 Germany's foreign secretary, Arthur Zimmermann, cabled his ambassador in Mexico: if the United States entered the war, Mexico should join Germany and "reconquer her lost territory of New Mexico, Texas and Arizona" (Britain's translation). Britain's codebreakers in Room 40 read it, and their chief, William Hall, waited three weeks. Releasing it would tell Germany its codes were broken, and America that Britain read its cables. So a British agent bribed a telegraph clerk in Mexico for another copy, and the Americans were told, a half-truth, that it was "bought in Mexico". Wilson published it on 1 March, and Zimmermann then admitted it: "I cannot deny it. It is true." Congress declared war on 6 April.

The case for the cover story: the codebreakers kept their secret, the ally got the truth that mattered, and the lie harmed no one. Intelligence that reveals itself dies.

The case against: Britain was reading the cables of the ally it courted. Americans were told falsely that the text was stolen in Mexico, and many took it for a British forgery. Trust built on a managed truth belongs to the manager.

Your call: is a friend who lies to you for your own good still a friend?

Lab: Steal a Eureka, guard a Campus

Predict first: by how many percentage points will Gain Sources raise your Steal Tech Boost odds, and how many rival spies will your counterspy catch in 30 turns?

  1. Send one spy to a rival city with a Campus. Note the success odds for Steal Tech Boost.
  2. Instead of starting it, run Gain Sources first, then check the odds again.
  3. Put your second spy on Counterspy in your own Campus and note how often rival spies are caught over 30 turns.

Measure: success odds before and after Gain Sources; enemy missions caught against missions that hurt you.

What you should see: preparation shifts the odds by a large margin; a counterspy turns your Campus from an easy target into a costly one.

If your result differs: an enemy counterspy in or next to the target Campus lowers your odds. If the rival knows no tech you lack, Steal Tech Boost is not possible. Your counterspy guards only its district and the ones next to it, so missions against other districts slip past.

In the world: before buying a used phone, check the seller's ratings or ask to test it. That time is the price of closing an information gap, the same job a delegation or Listening Post does in the game.

Check yourself

  1. You plan to steal a tech boost from a rival's Campus. How long does Gain Sources last, what does it do, and why run it first?

    Show answer

    24 turns. Your spies in that city act 2 levels higher, which raises the odds of the risky steal, and Gain Sources itself can't fail. A caught spy hands the rival a casus belli, so better odds matter.

  2. An AI offers a deal, and you can't see its hidden agenda. Which concept describes your position, and how can you close the gap in the game?

    Show answer

    Information asymmetry: it knows its real preferences and you don't. Pay to inspect: a delegation or embassy, an international trade route or a spy's Listening Post raises diplomatic visibility, which reveals hidden agendas.

  3. You are buying a used bike from a stranger and can't judge its condition. Using Akerlof, what happens to a market full of buyers like you, and what can you do?

    Show answer

    Buyers offer an average price, so owners of good bikes keep them and the listings fill with lemons. Close the gap: inspect it, take a test ride, or buy from a seller with ratings or a warranty.

In the game: spies earn most against the leader in your victory race. Aim them at the district that powers that lead.

In the world: whoever knows more can set the terms, which is why both sides pay to close the gap.

Climate and Disasters

Your Coal plant warms the whole planet, and the flood lands on someone else's coast. That is the classic market failure, and the bill is hard to split.

The rule VI (Gathering Storm)

Turn 260. Phase II: the sea is up 1.0 m, and a 1 m tile by your port is pillaged. Your share of the CO2 is small. The water does not check.

CO2. Climate is tracked for the whole world. Most late buildings, from the Factory on, need Power to work at full strength, and Power Plants burn fuel to make it. Coal Power Plants emit the most CO2 per unit of Power, Oil less, Nuclear far less (but nuclear plants risk accidents). Units that burn fuel and new Railroads also emit. Clearing Woods, Rainforest and Marsh across the world raises the effective CO2 level.

Seven phases. On a Standard map, each 1,000 on the climate screen's CO2 counter (which hides the last three digits) adds 0.5°C and one Climate Change point. The phases can't be reversed.

PhaseSea levelCoastal lowlandsStorm and flood fertility
I+0.5 mnoneyes
II+1.0 m1 m tiles floodedyes
III+1.5 m2 m tiles floodedyes
IV+2.0 m1 m tiles submergedno
V+2.5 m3 m tiles floodedno; storms and droughts start stripping yields
VI+3.0 m2 m tiles submergedno
VII+3.5 m3 m tiles submergedno

Sea level. Coastal lowland tiles carry a rating of 1, 2 or 3 meters. A flooded tile is pillaged, along with any district (a specialized city tile that holds buildings) on it, until its city builds a Flood Barrier. A submerged tile turns into Coast for good. City Centers (the tiles cities were founded on) never go under.

Disasters. Floods hit river Floodplains, droughts hit open Plains and Grassland, storms roam the map, and volcanoes erupt over nearby tiles. Their base rate is a setup option (Disaster Intensity 0 to 4); warming makes them more frequent and severe. They pillage, damage and can kill citizens. Most also fertilize land: a flood or storm can leave affected tiles with lasting extra Food or Production (the yield that builds units and buildings), and an eruption can leave Volcanic Soil. A Dam on the river stops flood damage but roughly halves the fertility gains. Droughts give nothing back.

The Congress response. The World Congress (the game's recurring global vote) can pass a Global Energy Treaty (from the Modern Era) that discounts or bans a type of power building, and a Deforestation Treaty (Atomic and Information Eras). From the Atomic Era it can hold Climate Accords, a competition that scores each civ for emitting less than the worst polluter; first place earns 2 Diplomatic Victory points. The Carbon Recapture project (Industrial Zone, Global Warming Mitigation tech) removes 50 of your lifetime CO2 and earns 30 Favor (a currency spent in the World Congress) each time.

Source: Climate change (Civ6), Disasters (Civ6), Flood (Civ6), Climate Accords (Civ6), Carbon Recapture (Civ6), fandom

Settle by rivers and volcanoes for the fertile soil, but keep districts off Floodplains and the tiles around a volcano, where repeat damage lands.

Economics

Externalities

For decades sulfur dioxide from American coal plants fell as acid rain far downwind. In 1990 Congress voted to cap power plants' sulfur dioxide at 8.95 million tons a year, with tradable permits. In 1991 the Energy Department valued a permit for one ton at $665 to $736. At auction in March 1993, they sold for $122 to $450. Once the harm had a price, cutting it was far cheaper than forecast.

Externality: a cost or benefit of an action that falls on people who had no part in it and is not priced. Pigou's fix is to tax the harm and subsidize the benefit.

Open in the PDF, page 83

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Your Coal plant's Production is yours; its sea-level rise is everyone'sPrivate gain, shared cost
Losing Favor for emitting above the world averageA Pigouvian tax

Where the map breaks

  • A Pigouvian tax is set by the damage. The Favor (a currency spent in the World Congress) penalty is a fixed formula unrelated to the floods.
  • Coase argued in 1960 that clear property rights let parties bargain an externality away without a tax. Civ has no way to bargain over emissions.

Read next

  • Primary: Pigou 1920, Part II, chapter IX

Arthur C. Pigou, The Economics of Welfare (1920)

Economics and politics

Tragedy of the commons, and governing the commons

Every Thursday, in a public square in Valencia, nine people judge their neighbors: the Tribunal de les Aigües, one representative from each of nine irrigation communities sharing the river Turia's canals. A farmer accused of taking water out of turn is heard in Valencian, and nothing is written down. The court's roots go back to the tenth century. The river's users wrote the rules and police each other.

The commons: each user of an open resource keeps the gain of taking more but shares the cost, so it is overused (Hardin). Communities often prevent this with boundaries, local rules, monitoring and graduated sanctions (Ostrom).

Open in the PDF, page 84

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
One world CO2 level; a clean civ floods along with the dirty onesOpen access, where restraint alone does not pay
Climate Accords and energy treaties voted in the CongressRules a community writes for itself

Where the map breaks

  • Hardin conceded in 1998 that his argument covers unmanaged commons. The Congress can manage, though rarely well.
  • Ostrom's cases are small communities built on talk and trust. Civ AIs can't be talked into a quota.

Read next

  • Accessible: Ostrom, "Beyond Markets and States", Nobel lecture (2009)
  • Primary: Hardin 1968; Ostrom 1990, chapter 3

Garrett Hardin, "The Tragedy of the Commons", Science (1968); Elinor Ostrom, Governing the Commons (1990)

The hard question: who should pay for a shared sky?

In the game a fixed formula docks Favor from heavy emitters. Real nations argue about who emitted first. Since 1751 the world has put more than 1.5 trillion tonnes of CO2 into the air. The United States is responsible for 25% of it and the 28 countries of the European Union for 22%, and until 1882 more than half the total had come from Britain alone. Most African countries account for less than 0.01% each. Today China emits more than a quarter of each year's total. In many of the poorest countries of sub-Saharan Africa the average person emits about 0.1 tonnes a year, around 150 times less than an American. The 1992 climate treaty says developed countries "should take the lead", in line with their "common but differentiated responsibilities and respective capabilities".

The case for the rich paying: they built their wealth on the emissions that cause the damage, they can afford the bill, and the poor did not create the problem.

The case against: nobody alive chose the emissions of 1882. A bill for past generations asks the living to pay for the dead, and cuts that spare today's biggest emitters cool little.

Your call: should responsibility follow what was emitted, what is emitted now, or who can pay?

Lab: Does one Coal plant matter?

Predict first: write your share of world CO2 now and your guess for 20 turns later, and say whether your Favor per turn will drop.

  1. In the Industrial Era, note the World Climate screen's global CO2 and your share.
  2. Build one Coal Power Plant and play 20 turns.
  3. Note your contribution, the world total and your Favor per turn again.

Measure: your share of the world total, and your Favor.

What you should see: your own change moves the global number very little, while the Favor penalty lands on you directly. That gap is the whole lesson.

If your result differs: Favor drops only once you emit well above the world average, at −1 per 3 points over. Other civs' plants, units and Railroads, and clearing of Woods and Rainforest anywhere, raise the world total too, so your share can shrink even as you emit more.

In the world: look at a shared office fridge or a student kitchen. Each person's mess barely shows, so nobody cleans until someone posts rules. That sign on the door is a climate treaty in miniature.

Check yourself

  1. Your coastal city has 1 m and 3 m lowland tiles. In which climate phases does each kind flood, and when is it lost for good?

    Show answer

    1 m tiles flood in Phase II and are submerged in Phase IV. 3 m tiles flood in Phase V and are submerged in Phase VII. A Flood Barrier stops flood damage; the City Center never goes under.

  2. You stop building Coal plants, but the world keeps warming and your coast floods anyway. Which concept explains why your restraint alone did not save you?

    Show answer

    The tragedy of the commons. One world CO2 level means each civ keeps the full gain of its own plants but shares the flood cost. One user's restraint barely moves the total; only shared rules like Climate Accords can.

  3. A factory upstream dumps waste into a river, and a fishing village downstream loses its catch. What is this, and what did Pigou propose?

    Show answer

    A negative externality: a cost falling on people who had no part in the decision, with no price attached. Pigou proposed taxing the harm so the factory's private cost matches the cost to everyone.

In the game: early disasters usually pay in yields; late warming you can't stop alone, so build where the water won't reach.

In the world: when a cost lands on everyone, each actor's own cut barely shows, so fixes need rules, prices or shared agreements.

Barbarians

Raiders, warlords and states are closer relatives than they look. The difference is mostly how long each plans to stay.

The rule VI

Turn 12. A barbarian Scout (a fast unit for exploring) steps out of the fog, sees your capital and turns for home. If it gets back, 3 to 6 raiders will follow. You have a few turns.

Outposts. Barbarians (hostile raiders from camps) live in outposts (camps). New outposts can appear on any land tile in unclaimed territory that has no revealed resource and that no unit can currently see. Each outpost starts with a fortified anti-cavalry unit (a Spearman early, later Pikemen) as its guard and a Scout. Outposts near Horses send mounted units; coastal outposts send ships.

Scouts and raids. The barbarian Scout wanders until it spots a city. If it gets back to its outpost, the outpost starts building a raiding party of 3 to 6 units, depending on difficulty. Raiders pillage, plunder trade routes and capture civilians; from Emperor up they attack cities too. Barbarians raze any city they take, but they can't capture an original capital. Kill the Scout before it gets home and the raid never starts.

Clearing an outpost. Defeat the guard, then move any land unit onto the tile. You get a Gold reward that scales with difficulty and game speed (it is smaller on harder levels), and in some eras a historic moment worth Era Score (points for historic moments that set your next era). Founding a city that claims the tile also removes it. Barbarians learn any tech half the civs know, so camps grow stronger.

Source: Barbarian (Civ6), fandom

Optional rule: Barbarian Clans mode VI

Barbarian Clans, a free game mode since the February 2021 update (tick it in Advanced Setup), makes each outpost a named clan. Its banner lets you Bribe it (Gold for 20 turns without attacks on your cities), Hire a unit, Incite it against a neighbor, or Ransom a captured civilian. When you defeat a clan you choose to Disperse it (the camp goes, but in this mode you get XP instead of Gold) or Raid it for Gold and leave it standing. Clans earn progress toward civilization every turn, faster when you bribe or hire them and slower when you incite or raid them. A clan that gets there becomes a new city-state (a minor power that cannot win), unless it sits within 3 tiles of another city.

Source: Barbarian Clans (Civ6), fandom

Political economy

Stationary vs roving bandit

In the 1920s much of China was ruled by warlords who taxed their people heavily and pocketed much of the money. Mancur Olson, an economist, noticed something odd about one, Feng Yu-hsiang, who "was noted for the exceptional extent to which he used his army for suppressing bandits". Feng's subjects were still robbed, by a thief who stayed. Yet "most people in Feng's domain found him much preferable to the roving bandits".

Stationary bandit: a roving bandit takes everything and moves on, so nobody invests. A bandit who settles gains from his subjects' prosperity, so he taxes instead of looting and keeps other thieves out.

Open in the PDF, page 86

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Barbarian raiders pillage, plunder and capture civiliansThe roving bandit
Barbarian Clans: a bribed clan can become a city-stateRaiders settling into rulers

Where the map breaks

  • Olson's stationary bandit still rules others. In Civ you are ruler and ruled at once, so their conflict vanishes.
  • In standard games barbarians never settle; only the Clans mode shows anything like Olson's transition.

Read next

  • Accessible: Olson, Power and Prosperity (2000), chapters 1–2
  • Primary: Olson 1993

Mancur Olson, "Dictatorship, Democracy, and Development", American Political Science Review (1993)

Political sociology

Monopoly of legitimate violence

On 28 January 1919, with Bavaria in revolution, Max Weber told students in Munich that a state is the human community that successfully claims the monopoly of the legitimate use of physical force within a territory. On 21 February Kurt Eisner, who had proclaimed the Bavarian republic, was shot dead on his way to resign. The elected government fled Munich, and a short-lived Soviet republic took its place.

Monopoly of legitimate violence: Weber defines the state as the community that successfully claims the sole right to use physical force in a territory, a right resting on tradition, charisma or legal rules.

Open in the PDF, page 87

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
A new city whose land covers a camp removes itTerritory where only the state may use force
Free Cities: armed, hostile, belonging to no oneA broken monopoly

Where the map breaks

  • Weber's monopoly is a claim others must accept. In Civ it is a hard rule of movement.
  • Legitimacy has no mechanic. Loyalty (how firmly a city stays yours) counts nearby citizens, not their consent.

Read next

  • Accessible: "World Wide Weber", History Today
  • Primary: Weber, "Politics as a Vocation" (any translation)

Max Weber, "Politik als Beruf" ("Politics as a Vocation"), lecture of 1919

The hard question: when is force legitimate?

In the game you clear a camp and nobody asks by what right. In 67 BC pirates ran the Mediterranean: Plutarch counts more than a thousand pirate ships and 400 captured cities, and Rome feared for its food. The tribune Aulus Gabinius proposed giving Pompey command of the whole sea and the land 50 miles inland for three years, with, Plutarch says, 200 ships and the treasury. The Senate balked, since Sulla had made himself dictator fifteen years before, but the people passed the law. Pompey cleared the western seas in forty days and all piracy in under three months. He spared more than 20,000 who surrendered and settled them on farmland in half-empty towns such as Dyme in Greece, reasoning, in Bernadotte Perrin's translation, that a man "may be softened by new customs and a change of place and life".

The case for the law: the pirates were starving Rome, and only one commander with sweeping power could clear a whole sea. The result was humane as well as fast: raiders became farmers.

The case against: a republic that hands one man an army and a treasury has already changed what it is. The law later served as a precedent for the powers of Augustus, Rome's first emperor.

Your call: would you accept an emergency power you know will outlive the emergency?

Lab: Bribe the raiders or rob them?

Predict first: which clan becomes a city-state first, and will bribing or raiding leave you with more Gold after 40 turns?

  1. Start a game with Barbarian Clans on. Find two clans at similar distances from your cities.
  2. Bribe the first one whenever its bribe runs out. Raid the second one whenever you can.
  3. Record for 40 turns: Gold spent and earned, improvements pillaged, and each clan's progress toward civilization.

Measure: net Gold and damage per clan, and which one civilizes first.

What you should see: the bribed clan edges toward becoming a city-state; the raided clan pays now and keeps raiding.

If your result differs: a bribe only stops attacks on your cities, so the bribed clan can still hit your units. You can raid a clan only once every 10 turns. Other civs act too: their bribes and hires speed a clan, their incites and raids slow it. A clan within 3 tiles of a city never civilizes.

In the world: ask a few renters whether their landlord plans to keep the building for years or sell soon. Olson's logic predicts long-term owners fix the roof while quick sellers patch and go.

Check yourself

  1. A barbarian Scout spots your second city and turns back toward its outpost, 4 tiles away. What happens if it gets home, and what should you do this turn?

    Show answer

    The outpost starts building a raiding party of 3 to 6 units, depending on difficulty. Kill the Scout before it gets home and the raid never starts, so attack it with any unit in reach.

  2. In Clans mode you keep bribing a clan until it becomes a city-state (a minor power that cannot win). Which concept describes that change, and where does the game's version break?

    Show answer

    Olson's roving bandit turning stationary: raiders who settle gain a stake in the land and stop looting it. The break: only Clans mode shows this; in standard games barbarians never settle.

  3. Residents hire private guards who start fining people for littering. Even if the streets get cleaner, why would the city government object, in Weber's terms?

    Show answer

    Weber defines the state by its claim to a monopoly on the legitimate use of force in its territory. Guards who punish people on their own authority challenge that claim; their force lacks the legitimacy the state claims.

In the game: barbarians are a timer that starts when their Scout sees you. Kill the Scout and you control the clock.

In the world: order begins when one group can promise protection over a territory for a long time, and that promise usually rests on force.

Part 4

War and Power

War in Civ VI is a list of prices: Grievances to declare, hit points to fight, Gold to upgrade. Learn to read them here, and you will see them in real wars.

Why Wars Start (and How to Avoid One)

A surprise war costs 150 Grievances (a running record of who wronged whom), and the AI still declares them. States that want only safety fall into wars too, through the trap that armed Europe before 1914.

The rule: every war has a stated reason VI

Turn 45. Your neighbor's Warriors have loitered at your border. Then the notice arrives: Surprise War. You now hold 150 Grievances against him, and none of them fade while the war lasts.

A casus belli (a justification for war) (Latin for "case for war") is the official reason you give when you declare war, and it sets how angry the world gets. The anger is counted in Grievances, a score that each pair of civs keeps against each other (Gathering Storm). They make other leaders like you less, usually after the war ends. At peace they fade by 10 per turn in the Ancient Era, one less each later era, down to 2 in the Future Era.

Casus belliWhen you can use itGrievances for declaring
Surprise WarAny time, no conditions150
Formal WarYou denounced them (or they denounced you) at least 5 turns ago100
War of Territorial ExpansionMobilization civic; two of your cities within 10 tiles of two of theirs75
Holy WarThey converted one of your cities to their religion50
War of RetributionThey broke a promise to you in the last 30 turns50
Colonial WarNationalism civic; they are two or more eras behind you50
Ideological WarYou both run Tier 3 governments, and they differ50
Golden Age WarYou chose the To Arms! dedication in a Golden Age25
Liberation, Reconquest, Protectorate WarThey took a friend's city, took your city, or attacked a city-state you lead0

Most casus belli also need a particular civic.

Capturing a city adds up to 50 Grievances, keeping it in the peace deal adds the same again, razing it costs three times the capture cost, and taking a civ's last city costs 150 with every civ still in the game. Allies of your victim pick up 50% of the victim's Grievances against you, and declared friends pick up 25%. The one action that lowers Grievances during a war is liberating a city: handing a captured city back to the civ that founded it.

Long wars also cause war weariness (unhappiness that grows the longer you fight): battles add points (double outside your borders), and every 400 points cost 1 Amenity, the contentment that keeps cities growing.

Sources: Casus Belli (Civ6), Grievances (Civ6), War weariness (Civ6), civilization.fandom.com.

Why the AI attacks weak neighbors VI

Community consensus on CivFanatics is that a Deity (the hardest difficulty) AI often rushes its nearest neighbor between turns 20 and 35, sends a second wave of Horsemen or Knights later, and refuses friendship once it has decided to attack. Agendas, the likes and dislikes that drive each AI leader, point the same way: Cleopatra "dislikes civilizations with weak militaries". The AI also gets +1 to +4 combat strength (how hard a unit fights) from King to Deity (see How the AI Thinks and Cheats), so it often wins even fights.

Sources: CivFanatics, "Getting Rolled on Deity" (2018); List of agendas in Civ6.

Defending without fighting VI

  • Look expensive. Build Ancient Walls (Masonry). They give your city a ranged attack and a 100 HP outer wall that melee units can barely scratch (see Combat).
  • Keep units at home. A melee unit fortified on a hill gets +3 for the hill and up to +6 for fortifying (staying put to dig in). Community consensus for Deity is 4 to 5 Archers once a rush is coming.
  • Declare friendship. A declared friend cannot declare war on you while the friendship lasts (30 turns on Standard speed). Send a delegation early: it adds +3 to the relationship.
  • Keep your promises. If an AI asks you to move troops away from its border and you agree, then break the promise, that alone is 100 Grievances.

International relations

Anarchy and the security dilemma

In 1898 Alfred von Tirpitz, who ran Germany's navy office, got the Reichstag to fund eleven battleships. He never expected to beat the Royal Navy, only to make Britain offer concessions. London read a threat. In 1906 Britain's First Sea Lord, Jacky Fisher, called Germany the "only probable enemy", while Berlin grew nervous of a surprise British strike on its fleet. In 1908 Wilhelm II insisted the ships were aimed at Japan. By 1914 Britain had 20 dreadnoughts to Germany's 15. Each side's insurance was the other's reason to arm.

The security dilemma: with no authority above states, each arms to feel safe. Neighbors cannot tell defense from attack, so they arm too, and all end up less safe.

Open in the PDF, page 91

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
No one enforces peace between civs; only Grievances and agendas push back.Anarchy: no authority above states.
Units parked near a border earn a "move your troops" request.Troops near a border look like an attack being prepared.

Where the map breaks

  • The AI's fear is a few if-then rules, and some agendas admire strength. Real fear runs on history and pride.
  • Civ shows every rival's units. Britain had to guess what Germany's fleet was for, and that guessing is the dilemma.

Read next

  • Accessible: Joseph S. Nye Jr. and David A. Welch, Understanding Global Conflict and Cooperation.
  • Primary: Jervis (1978), below.

John H. Herz, "Idealist Internationalism and the Security Dilemma," World Politics 2(2) (1950); Robert Jervis, "Cooperation under the Security Dilemma," World Politics 30(2) (1978).

International relations / strategy

Deterrence and compellence

On 16 October 1962 John F. Kennedy was shown photographs of Soviet missile sites in Cuba. His Joint Chiefs wanted an attack and an invasion. He chose a naval "quarantine", since a blockade is an act of war. The missiles were already there, so this was compellence, the harder job: making a rival undo something. On 28 October Khrushchev said the missiles would go, for a public American promise not to invade Cuba and a secret one to pull American missiles out of Turkey.

Deterrence and compellence: deterrence is a threat that stops someone starting something; compellence makes them stop or act. Both work only if the other side believes you.

Open in the PDF, page 92

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Walls, a hill garrison and Archers make your city a bad trade.Deterrence by denial: make the attack fail.
Siege units beside a city you demand in a peace deal.Compellence: the AI cedes cities it expects to lose.

Where the map breaks

  • Schelling wrote about threats under the shadow of shared catastrophe, and Civ wars are recoverable.
  • An AI that backs down pays nothing at home. Khrushchev fell two years after Cuba, partly over his concessions.

Read next

  • Accessible: Thomas C. Schelling, Arms and Influence (1966), chapter 2, "The Art of Commitment."
  • Primary: Schelling, The Strategy of Conflict (1960).

Thomas C. Schelling, The Strategy of Conflict (1960) and Arms and Influence (1966).

The hard question: fight now or buy time?

In the game you postpone a war you cannot win yet, and build walls. In September 1938 Germany demanded the Sudetenland, the mountainous Czechoslovak border region that held its fortifications. On 30 September, in Munich, Neville Chamberlain, Édouard Daladier, Mussolini and Hitler agreed to hand it over. Czechoslovakia was not invited. Chamberlain told the crowd in Downing Street, "I believe it is peace for our time". In March 1939 Germany took the rest of the country.

The case for buying time: Britain's Chiefs of Staff reported that it could not stop Germany conquering Czechoslovakia, and the Dominions signalled their help was not assured. The first Spitfires reached a squadron only that August, and Britain's radar chain was not fully working until 3 September 1939. After Munich, Chamberlain told his Cabinet it "would be madness for the country to stop rearming".

The case against: Churchill told the Commons on 5 October, "we have sustained a total and unmitigated defeat". After inspecting the Czech fortifications, Hitler privately told Joseph Goebbels, his propaganda minister, that "we would have shed a lot of blood" (in translation). By Hitler's count, the 1939 occupation gave Germany 469 tanks and 43,000 machine guns. The year Britain bought, Germany bought too.

Your call: when you cannot win today, how do you tell buying time from feeding the appetite you fear?

Lab: Does a wall buy peace?

Predict first: how many turns later will the first war declaration come in the walled game than in the open one? Write a number, or "never".

  1. Start a King game and note the Map Seed and Game Seed in Advanced Setup. Start a second King game with the same leader and both seeds.
  2. Game A: Ancient Walls and three units in your border city. Game B: no walls, one unit. Play both to turn 60 with the same other choices.

Measure: the turn of the first war declaration against you, and how many cities you hold at turn 60.

What you should see: the walled game is attacked later or not at all. If war does come, the walled city survives it.

If your result differs: a leader whose agenda favors surprise wars, such as Cyrus (Opportunist), may attack whatever you build. A broken promise to move troops costs 100 Grievances and opens a War of Retribution. And if war came in both games, compare cities held: walls that fail to deter can still deny.

In the world: a burglar picks the easier door. Walk down any street and note which houses show alarms, lights or high gates. Each one is deterrence by denial: it makes an attack look likely to fail, so it is less likely to be tried.

Do

  • Denounce 5 turns before a war you plan, to unlock Formal War.

Avoid

  • Surprise wars after the early game: 150 Grievances before a shot is fired.
  • Razing cities you could keep or return.

Check yourself

  1. You denounced a neighbor 6 turns ago and have Mobilization, but only one city within 10 tiles of theirs. You meet no other condition. Which casus belli is cheapest, and at what cost?

    Show answer

    Formal War, 100 Grievances, because the denunciation is more than 5 turns old. Territorial Expansion (75) needs two of your cities within 10 tiles of two of theirs. Surprise War would cost 150.

  2. Your neighbor builds walls and three Archers. You read it as preparation for war and build too. Name the concept, and say what Civ shows you that real states lack.

    Show answer

    The security dilemma: each side's defense looks like a threat to the other. Civ shows rival units and scores on screen. Real states have to guess, and the guessing is what drives the dilemma.

  3. Two neighboring countries share a tense border. One wants to feel safer without alarming the other. Using this lesson, what kind of build-up should it favor?

    Show answer

    Defenses that are hard to use for attack, such as fixed border fortifications. Your defense is someone else's threat, so the cheapest security is the kind that cannot be turned outward.

In the game: before you declare, find the cheapest casus belli you qualify for; before you settle a border city, budget for walls.

In the world: your defense is someone else's threat, so the cheapest security is the kind that cannot be used to attack.

Combat

Your Warrior attacks a Warrior dug in on a hill, deals 21 damage and takes 43. One formula decides every Civ VI fight, and strategy is the art of tilting it before you strike.

The damage formula VI

Every military unit has 100 hit points (HP), its health, and a combat strength (CS), one number for how hard it fights. Ranged units also have a ranged strength for attacking from a distance, and siege units have a bombard strength. In an attack the game subtracts the defender's strength from the attacker's. Call that difference Δ (delta). The damage is:

damage = 30 × e0.04 × Δ, then multiplied by a random factor between 80% and 120%.

(e ≈ 2.718.) An even fight deals 30 damage, and each point of advantage multiplies your damage by about 1.04. Only the difference matters: 30 against 20 plays out exactly like 110 against 100. Δ +10 deals 45, Δ +17 deals 59, and Δ +30 deals about 100, which kills a healthy unit in one hit. A melee attacker takes damage back, worked out the same way; a ranged attacker takes none.

Damaged units fight worse. A unit loses 1 strength for every 10 HP it is missing, up to −10, so a unit at 50 HP fights at −5. In a focused attack, the first hit makes every later hit stronger.

Source: Combat (Civ6), civilization.fandom.com (formula from player research cited there).

Modifiers that change Δ VI

SituationStrength changeApplies to
Hills+3Unit standing there
Woods or Rainforest+3Unit standing there (Hills plus Woods = +6)
Marsh or Floodplains−2Unit standing there
River between attacker and defender+5Defender, against melee attacks only
Attacking from the water (amphibious)−10Attacker
Fortified 1 turn / 2 or more turns+3 / +6Defending land unit that has not moved
Flanking: each other friendly unit next to the target+2 eachMelee attacker
Support: each friendly unit next to the defender+2 eachDefender, against melee attacks only
Anti-cavalry unit fighting cavalry+10Spearman line
Ranged attack on a city, district or ship−17Land ranged attacker
Bombard attack on a land unit−17Siege attacker
Each 10 HP missing−1Either side, up to −10

Flanking and support switch on with the Military Tradition (a step on the culture tree) civic. Neither helps ranged attacks, and you get no flanking bonus when you attack a City Center (the tile the city was founded on) or Encampment (the military district).

An older beginners' guide got the river and fortify bonuses wrong; this table is right. Its advice to "bring 3× its force" means nothing in Civ VI, because combat compares two strength numbers, never army sizes.

Sources: Combat (Civ6); Flanking and Support (Civ6).

Moving, shooting, healing and promotions VI

Zone of control (the tiles next to a unit, where enemies must stop) (ZOC): land melee units, City Centers, Encampments and most ships block the tiles next to them, so an enemy that steps into one must stop for the turn. Cavalry and naval raiders ignore ZOC, and it does not cross rivers.

Melee units (Warrior, Swordsman, and also Spearmen and cavalry) walk into the target's tile, take damage back, and are the only land units that can capture a city. Ranged units (Slinger, Archer) shoot without taking damage back but are weak when attacked: an Archer has 25 ranged strength and only 15 combat strength. Woods and Hills block a ranged shot unless the shooter stands higher.

Healing: a unit that does nothing for a turn heals 5 HP in enemy territory, 10 in neutral land, 15 in your own territory and 20 on a district (a specialized city tile that holds buildings) or improvement in your own territory. Ships heal only in friendly territory. Promotions: a unit levels up after 15, then 30, then 45 more experience points (XP), and so on. A fight between two units gives at most 8 XP. Taking a promotion heals 50 HP and ends the unit's turn.

Sources: Zone of control (Civ6); Promotion (Civ6); Combat (Civ6); Archer (Civ6).

Cities, walls and siege VI

A city has 200 HP. Its defending strength is the strength of the best unit you have built, minus 10 (or the garrison's own strength, if higher), plus 2 for each district, +3 on a hill, +3 for the capital and +3 for each level of walls. Without walls a city cannot shoot. Walls give it a ranged attack with a range of 2 tiles and add an outer layer of HP: 100 for Ancient Walls, plus 100 each for Medieval and Renaissance Walls, and 400 once Steel (a technology) brings Urban Defenses (Gathering Storm values). While the walls stand, melee units deal 85% less damage to them and ranged units 50% less.

  • Battering Ram (Masonry): next to the city, your melee and anti-cavalry attacks do full damage to Ancient Walls. Useless against Medieval Walls and up.
  • Siege Tower: next to the city, your melee and anti-cavalry attacks skip the walls and hit the city itself. Works on Ancient and Medieval Walls.
  • Siege units (Catapult, Bombard, Artillery) attack with bombard strength and do full damage to walls. They cannot move and shoot in one turn without the Expert Crew promotion, and take −17 against units, so guard them.

A city heals 20 HP each turn. If your units' zones of control cover every passable tile around it, the city is besieged and stops healing. Damaged walls never heal on their own; the owner must run a repair project.

Sources: City combat (Civ6); Ancient Walls (Civ6); Battering Ram (Civ6); Siege Tower (Civ6).

Worked example. Your Warrior (20) attacks an enemy Warrior fortified for two turns on a hill (20 + 3 + 6 = 29). Δ is −9: you deal about 21 and take about 43 back. A Swordsman (35) has Δ +6 and deals about 38. Shooting first with Archers helps more: every 10 HP they remove is another point in your favor.

Military strategy

Fog of war, friction and the culminating point

In 1812 Carl von Clausewitz, a Prussian officer, quit his army rather than fight for Napoleon, and joined the Russians. That June more than 450,000 invaders crossed into Russia. In the first two weeks of July, sickness and desertion alone cost them 100,000 men. About 100,000 reached Moscow on 14 September and found it deserted and burning. Five weeks later they turned for home. Napoleon lost about 380,000 men, half of them to disease. In On War, Clausewitz named the point where an attack has spent its strength.

Fog, friction and the culminating point: most information in war is uncertain; small problems make simple plans hard; and every attack weakens as it advances, until pressing on invites defeat.

Open in the PDF, page 97

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Unseen tiles hide enemy units.Fog: partial information.
The 80–120% damage roll; a unit stuck in a zone of control.Friction: small obstacles add up.
Units heal 5 HP a turn in enemy land; fighting abroad doubles war weariness.Culminating point: each step costs more.

Where the map breaks

  • Civ's fog is clear or blank, and what you see is true. Real fog includes false reports.
  • A healed Civ unit forgets its culminating point. Most of Napoleon's army never reached Moscow.

Read next

  • Accessible: Michael Howard, Clausewitz: A Very Short Introduction.
  • Primary: On War, trans. Michael Howard and Peter Paret (Princeton, 1976).

Carl von Clausewitz, On War (Vom Kriege, 1832–34).

Military strategy

Sun Tzu, The Art of War

In spring 1944 the Allies gave the Germans an army to fear. The First United States Army Group existed on paper, with many divisions that did not. Its commander was George Patton, the Allied general the Germans rated highest, and it had dummy landing craft, fake radio traffic and a double agent, Juan Pujol García, reporting from 27 invented agents. The real invasion hit Normandy on 6 June, and most German reserves waited for an attack on the Pas-de-Calais that never came. As Sun Tzu wrote, "All warfare is based on deception."

Sun Tzu's teaching: the best victories come with the least fighting. Know yourself and the enemy, shape the situation before battle, strike weakness, and use terrain and deception.

Open in the PDF, page 98

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Hills and Woods give +3 each; a river gives +5 against melee attacks.Ground decides battles before they start.
Hitting units the AI has left outside its walls.Attack where the enemy is weak.

Where the map breaks

  • The maxims fit almost any plan after the fact, so they predict nothing.
  • Civ's Domination victory means taking capitals. Sun Tzu called besieging walled cities "the worst policy of all".

Read next

  • Accessible: Sun Tzu, The Art of War, trans. Lionel Giles (1910), free online.
  • Primary: The Art of War, trans. Samuel B. Griffith (1963), with notes.

Sunzi bingfa (The Art of War), attributed to Sun Wu (China, Warring States period; date and authorship debated).

Military operations research

Lanchester's laws

On 21 October 1805 Horatio Nelson met a Franco-Spanish fleet of 33 ships of the line with 27 of his own. Orthodoxy said to form a single line and trade broadsides in parallel. Nelson sailed at the enemy in two columns, split their line in three and surrounded the middle, so that part of his fleet fought part of theirs. Eighteen enemy ships were captured or destroyed, and Britain lost none. In 1916 Frederick Lanchester, an engineer, used the battle to illustrate his square law.

Lanchester's laws: equations for how fast two forces wear each other down. When all can fire at once, strength grows with the square of numbers.

Open in the PDF, page 98

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Damaged units lose strength, so hitting first and together keeps you hitting harder.Losses cut the enemy's fire, which cuts your losses.
A one-tile pass lets only a few units fight.The linear law: when few can engage, numbers matter less.

Where the map breaks

  • Lucas and Türkeş (2004) fitted the laws to daily data from Kursk and the Ardennes, and none fitted well.
  • Civ's damage formula rewards quality (the strength difference) more than numbers.

Read next

  • Accessible: The Dupuy Institute, "Lanchester equations have been weighed" (2016).
  • Primary: Lanchester (1916), chapters V and VI.

Frederick W. Lanchester, Aircraft in Warfare: The Dawn of the Fourth Arm (1916), collecting his 1914 articles in Engineering.

Lab: Concentrate or trickle?

Predict first: which run destroys the enemy group in fewer turns, and how much more total HP will the winner keep? Write the run and the HP gap.

  1. Save the game when your 4 Archers and 2 Warriors face 4 similar enemy units.
  2. Run A: focus every attack on one enemy unit until it dies, then the next. Run B (reload): spread one hit onto each enemy unit per turn.

Measure: turns until the enemy group is gone, and your total HP left.

What you should see: Run A wins faster with more HP left. Dead units stop hitting back, and wounded units hit weaker. That is the square law in hexes.

If your result differs: the 80–120% damage roll can flip a close fight, so run each version twice. An enemy that fortifies or stands in Woods or on Hills gains +3 to +6, so hit the same targets in both runs. A wounded unit that pulls back heals 5 to 20 HP a turn, which wastes spread-out hits.

In the world: watch a game of dodgeball. A team that targets one player at a time removes throwers fast, and every player out means fewer balls coming back. Count the throws each side makes as players go out.

Do

  • Read the combat preview before every attack.
  • Soften with ranged units, finish with melee.
  • Bring a Ram or Tower, or siege units, to any walled city.

Avoid

  • Leaving Archers or Catapults in front of your melee line.

Check yourself

  1. Your Swordsman (35) attacks an Archer that has just moved into Woods. What is Δ, and about how much damage do you deal before the random roll?

    Show answer

    The Archer defends with its combat strength of 15, plus 3 for Woods, so Δ = 35 − 18 = +17. That deals about 59 damage. Its 25 ranged strength only counts when it shoots.

  2. Army A has 10 equal units and Army B has 7, and every unit can fire at once. Under Lanchester's square law, about how many does A have left when B is gone?

    Show answer

    About 7.1. Fighting strength goes with the square of numbers: 10² − 7² = 51, and √51 ≈ 7.1. A head start of three units costs A only about three losses.

  3. A café has two staff, a long queue and a jammed coffee machine, and waiting customers leave. Using this lesson, should each fix one problem or both work on one first?

    Show answer

    Both clear the problem that feeds the losses first, likely the machine, then the queue. When losses compound, as walkouts do, concentrated effort beats spreading it thin.

In the game: win the strength difference. Stack terrain, fortify, flanking and focused fire until Δ is in your favor, then strike.

In the world: when losses compound, concentrating effort early beats spreading it thin.

Timing Pushes

Iron Working lands next turn. Your four Warriors could be Swordsmen before your neighbor builds walls. Every edge in war is a window, and the skill is leaving before it shuts.

Upgrades, corps and armies VI

When you research a new military tech, older units can be upgraded to the new type for Gold. The unit must be in your own territory with movement left, and you also need the strategic resource the new unit uses (Iron for Swordsmen, for example). Upgraded units keep their promotions but do not heal. The Professional Army policy card (a swappable bonus slotted into your government) (Mercenaries civic) halves the Gold cost.

Build cheap units early, bank Gold, then upgrade them all in a turn or two when the new tech lands. A Warrior (20 strength) upgraded to a Swordsman (35) gains 15 points, so against the same target it deals about 1.8 times as much damage.

FormationMade fromUnlocked byStrength bonusUpkeep
Corps (Fleet at sea)2 identical unitsNationalism civic+103/4 of two units
Army (Armada at sea)3 identical unitsMobilization civic+172/3 of three units

Upgrading a Corps (two or three units merged into one) costs twice as much as a single unit, and an Army three times.

Sources: Unit (Civ6); Professional Army (Civ6); Warrior and Swordsman (Civ6).

Windows players use VI

These are community consensus from CivFanatics and shift with patches.

  • Early Archer push. Several Archers with a melee screen can take an unwalled neighbor. Players say the window closes when "the first couple xbows" (Crossbowmen, 40 ranged strength) appear.
  • Renaissance push. One player describes a push around turns 120 to 140 (Standard speed) with about 3 Musketmen, 3 Crossbowmen, 2 Bombards and 3 to 5 cavalry, mostly upgraded older units, with about 1,000 Gold saved for half-price upgrades. It works, they write, "as long as the AI doesn't have renaissance walls or field cannons."

A window is open only if scouting shows the enemy still lacks the counter.

Source: CivFanatics, "Power Windows".

Ending a war VI

Peace is negotiated like a trade: Gold, resources and cities at full HP can all go on the table. A city you captured stays occupied, with weak output and falling Loyalty (how firmly a city stays yours) unless garrisoned, until the old owner cedes it in a deal. Keeping it costs the same Grievances (a running record of who wronged whom) as capturing it. Returning it removes those Grievances.

Sources: City combat (Civ6); Grievances (Civ6); Diplomacy (Civ6).

Concept lens: every push has a culminating point, so pick your cities and your stop turn before you march. Clausewitz

Military strategy

The OODA loop

John Boyd flew F-86 Sabres in Korea in 1953: 22 missions, no kills. The MiG-15 out-climbed and out-accelerated the early Sabres, yet American pilots were credited with ten MiGs for each Sabre lost. Boyd credited two details. The bubble canopy let the pilot see more, and hydraulic controls let him change maneuvers faster, so he could observe, orient, decide and act first. Later research cut that ratio, some of it to about two to one.

The OODA loop: observe, orient, decide, act, repeat. Whoever cycles faster and orients better acts inside the rival's loop, so the rival keeps reacting to a situation that has changed.

Open in the PDF, page 102

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Scouting their walls and units, and judging if your window is open.Observe and orient, Boyd's key steps.
Upgrading the whole army in one turn and attacking the next.Decide and act faster than they can respond.

Where the map breaks

  • Civ is turn-based, so both sides get the same tempo, and speed comes from preparation.
  • Boyd never published a tested theory, and Osinga argues the "faster loop wins" reading skips his stress on orientation.

Read next

  • Accessible: Robert Coram, Boyd: The Fighter Pilot Who Changed the Art of War (2002).
  • Primary: Boyd, "Patterns of Conflict" (1986); Frans P. B. Osinga, Science, Strategy and War (2007).

John R. Boyd, "Destruction and Creation" (essay, 1976) and "Patterns of Conflict" (briefing, final version 1986).

Military strategy / technology

Revolution in military affairs

On 10 May 1940 Germany attacked France with 2,445 tanks. France alone had 3,383 to 4,071, better armored and armed, though slower. France signed an armistice six weeks later. The French spread most of their tanks along the front in tiny formations. The Germans massed theirs in Panzer divisions, fitted radio receivers so command tanks could direct them, and struck at one point: on 13 May they forced three crossings of the Meuse near Sedan. The same machines, organized differently, made a different weapon.

Revolution in military affairs (RMA): a jump in how war is fought, when new technology combines with new organization and doctrine so that older forces become obsolete almost at once.

Open in the PDF, page 102

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Steel's Urban Defenses jump wall HP to 400.A technology that makes a class of weapon obsolete.
Nationalism and Mobilization add +10 and +17 to the same units.Organization multiplying old technology.

Where the map breaks

  • Civ's tech tree is fixed and known. Real armies adopt new methods unevenly, and in 1940 the side with better tanks lost.
  • In Civ, doctrine comes free with the tech. No amount of Production (the yield that builds units and buildings) buys an idea.

Read next

  • Accessible: Max Boot, War Made New (2006).
  • Primary: Krepinevich (1994), below.

Andrew F. Krepinevich, "Cavalry to Computer: The Pattern of Military Revolutions," The National Interest, no. 37 (Fall 1994).

Lab: Bank, upgrade, strike

Predict first: in each run, how many turns from first attack to first captured city, and how many units lost? Write two numbers per run.
