  1. In a King game, have 4 Warriors and 2 Slingers (early ranged units) by turn 40 and research Iron Working and Archery. You need Iron to upgrade Warriors to Swordsmen.
  2. Run A: upgrade one unit at a time as Gold allows. Run B (same save): bank Gold, upgrade everything within 2 turns, attack the turn after.

Measure: turns from first attack to first captured city, and units lost.

What you should see: Run B takes its city faster with fewer losses, because the AI has less time to build a counter.

If your result differs: without Iron, Warriors cannot upgrade, so Run B has no Swordsmen. If the target has Ancient Walls, melee units deal 85% less to them until you bring a Battering Ram. And upgraded units do not heal, so upgrading a damaged army hands the AI easy kills.

In the world: a rival can answer one change at a time more easily than many at once. When a shop or app you use relaunches, note whether it changed one thing or many on the same day, and how long its competitors took to respond.

Do

  • Name the target city and the stop turn before you build.
  • Slot Professional Army for the turns you upgrade, then swap it out.

Avoid

  • Starting a war you cannot finish before their counters arrive.

Check yourself

  1. You have 4 Warriors, Iron and Iron Working. What does each Warrior-to-Swordsman upgrade add, and why upgrade all four in one turn?

    Show answer

    +15 strength each (20 to 35), about 1.8 times the damage against the same target. Upgrading together and attacking next turn gives the AI less time to build a counter before your window shuts.

  2. You attack the turn after a mass upgrade, while the AI is still reacting to your old army. Which idea is this, and where does Civ's version break?

    Show answer

    Boyd's OODA loop: you act inside the enemy's decision cycle. Civ is turn-based, so both sides get the same tempo of decisions, and your speed comes from preparation.

  3. Two rival bakeries buy the same new oven. One keeps its old routine; the other reorganizes its shifts around the oven. Using the RMA idea, which gains more, and for how long?

    Show answer

    The one that reorganizes. A revolution in military affairs needs new organization as well as new technology, and the advantage lasts only until the rival copies both.

In the game: a push is a window. Bank Gold, upgrade all at once, strike, and make peace before the window shuts.

In the world: new tools change outcomes only once people reorganize around them, and the edge lasts until rivals catch up.

Navies, Air and the Map

Your Archers chip at a walled port for turns; two ships offshore would do it faster. Geography sets what an army is worth, and two theorists mapped world power on that idea.

Ships and harbors VI

A city founded on the coast can build ships. An inland city can too, once it builds a Harbor district (a specialized city tile that holds buildings) (Celestial Navigation tech), which must sit on a coast or lake tile next to land. Early ships stay on coast tiles until you research Cartography.

ClassLineJob
Naval meleeGalley, Caravel, Ironclad, DestroyerFight ships; can capture coastal cities
Naval rangedQuadrireme, Frigate, Battleship, Missile CruiserBombard cities and units from the sea
Naval raiderPrivateer, Submarine, Nuclear SubmarineInvisible; ignore zone of control; raid coasts
CarrierAircraft CarrierFloating base for aircraft

Naval ranged and raider ships do not take the −17 penalty to combat strength that land archers take against cities, which makes them the best tool for a coastal siege. They still deal 50% less to walls. A naval raider can make a coastal raid, pillaging an adjacent land tile from the water. Land units embark to cross water; embarked, they cannot attack at sea, and attacking land from water costs −10.

Sources: Harbor (Civ6); Combat (Civ6); City combat (Civ6); List of units in Civ6 by class.

Air units, the basics VI

Planes need the Flight tech and an Aerodrome (the air force district) district, and each needs a base: a City Center (the tile the city was founded on) holds 1, an Aerodrome 2 (4 with its buildings), an Aircraft Carrier 2. Fighters attack units and planes and can patrol to intercept. Bombers use bombard strength, so they hit cities and ships hard. Air attacks take no damage back unless an anti-air ship or an Anti-Air Gun (covering its tile and the tiles next to it) is there.

Sources: Air combat (Civ6); Anti-Air Gun (Civ6).

Chokepoints and canals VI

A chokepoint is a narrow place every army or fleet must pass: a land bridge, a mountain pass, a strait. One fortified unit on a hill in a pass faces only a few attackers at once, and its zone of control (the tiles next to a unit, where enemies must stop), which makes any enemy stepping next to it stop, blocks most units (cavalry ignore it).

In Gathering Storm (the second Civ VI expansion) you can build your own sea route with a Canal district (Steam Power tech). It goes on flat land with water on one side and a City Center or more water on the other, and ships and maritime trade routes can pass through. A pillaged canal blocks ships until it is repaired, so guard it.

Sources: Canal (Civ6); Zone of control (Civ6).

Geopolitics

Sea power

In 1890 Alfred Thayer Mahan, a US Navy captain who had run the Naval War College, published a history arguing that Britain grew great by commanding the sea. Only a battle fleet that could destroy the enemy's could win command; raiding merchant ships never would. Britain's rivals read it closest. Kaiser Wilhelm II ordered his officers to read Mahan. In 1898 Tirpitz had the book translated and 8,000 copies given away to win votes for a navy bill. Japan's navy used it as a textbook.

Sea power: greatness rests on a navy, merchant shipping, bases and sea lanes, and a navy wins command of the sea by beating the enemy's battle fleet.

Open in the PDF, page 105

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Harbors add Gold and can add a trade route.Commerce and navies feed each other.
Ranged ships besiege any coastal city without the −17 penalty.Command of the sea lets you strike where you choose.

Where the map breaks

  • Civ VI has no blockade, the core of real command of the sea.
  • In 1914–18 Germany's Mahan-style battle fleet fought one major battle, at Jutland, while its submarines, the commerce raiders Mahan dismissed, threatened Britain's imports all war.

Read next

  • Accessible: Paul Kennedy, The Rise and Fall of British Naval Mastery (1976).
  • Primary: Mahan (1890), chapter 1.

Alfred Thayer Mahan, The Influence of Sea Power upon History, 1660–1783 (1890).

Geopolitics

Heartland and rimland

In 1904 Halford Mackinder, a British geographer, argued that railways were shifting power from sea to land: trains could now move armies across inner Eurasia, beyond the reach of any fleet. He meant it as a warning that Britain's reliance on sea power would become a weakness. In 1919, writing for the peacemakers in Paris, he boiled it down: "Who rules East Europe commands the Heartland; Who rules the Heartland commands the World Island; Who rules the World Island commands the World."

Heartland and rimland: Mackinder argued that whoever holds inner Eurasia, safe from sea power, can rule the world. Spykman replied that the prize is the coastal belt around it, with most people and industry.

Open in the PDF, page 106

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
An inland empire on Pangaea can be reached only by land, through chokepoints.The Heartland, out of reach of fleets.
Coastal cities get Harbors and trade, and are open to naval sieges.The Rimland: rich, connected and contested.

Where the map breaks

  • Civ maps are random, and Mackinder's theory is about one real continent.
  • Neither theory was tested. Cold War planners drew on Spykman, which shows influence and proves nothing.

Read next

  • Accessible: Robert D. Kaplan, The Revenge of Geography (2012).
  • Primary: H. J. Mackinder, "The Geographical Pivot of History" (1904, short).

Halford J. Mackinder, "The Geographical Pivot of History" (1904) and Democratic Ideals and Reality (1919); Nicholas J. Spykman, America's Strategy in World Politics (1942) and The Geography of the Peace (1944).

The hard question: is it right to kill many to end a war sooner?

In the game a nuke can set off a Nuclear Emergency against you. Only two have been used in war. On 6 and 9 August 1945 the United States dropped them on Hiroshima and Nagasaki. By the end of 1945 they had killed an estimated 90,000 to 166,000 in Hiroshima and 60,000 to 80,000 in Nagasaki. About 90% of Hiroshima's dead were civilians. The Soviet Union declared war on 8 August; Japan announced its surrender on 15 August.

The case for the bombs: Japan had ignored the Allies' ultimatum. One study expected an invasion to cost 132,500 to 220,000 American casualties; another put Japanese deaths at 5 to 10 million. Truman said the bomb was used "to shorten the agony of war". The historian Richard Frank says intercepts show Japan's forces "were determined to fight a final Armageddon battle in the homeland".

The case against: Admiral William Leahy, Truman's chief of staff, wrote that the bombs were "of no material assistance in our war against Japan" and that "wars cannot be won by destroying women and children". A 1946 US survey judged Japan would have surrendered before 1946 anyway; historians dispute it. The historian Tsuyoshi Hasegawa argues the Soviet entry mattered more.

Your call: if killing civilians might save more lives, how sure must you be?

Lab: Land or sea siege?

Predict first: how many turns will each run take to capture the city, and which run will lose more units?

  1. Save next to a walled AI coastal city, with a melee unit ready to take it.
  2. Run A: land units only. Run B (reload): add 2 or 3 Quadriremes (early ranged warships) or Frigates firing from the sea.

Measure: turns to capture and units lost.

What you should see: Run B is faster and cheaper, because naval ranged units skip the −17 city penalty and the city must split its fire.

If your result differs: ships deal 50% less to walls, as Archers do, so high walls (100 HP per level) slow both runs. Before Cartography, ships cannot cross open ocean to reach a distant city. And ranged ships cannot capture: without a melee unit beside the city, neither run finishes.

In the world: a defender watching two directions spreads thin. In a two-on-one drill in football or basketball, watch the lone defender commit to one attacker and leave the other free. A city facing land and sea has the same problem.

Do

  • Escort embarked units with warships.
  • Add an Anti-Air Gun once enemies have planes.

Avoid

  • Attacking from boats: each attack is at −10.

Check yourself

  1. You besiege a walled coastal city. Should your ranged damage come from Archers on land or Quadriremes at sea, and what finishes the job?

    Show answer

    Quadriremes. Land ranged units take −17 against cities and ships do not, though both deal 50% less to walls. Ranged units cannot capture, so a melee unit, on land or a naval melee ship, takes the city.

  2. A rival raids your coast with Privateers (naval raiding units) but never takes a city. Which theorist warned that commerce raiding alone does not win, and what core tool of sea power does Civ VI lack?

    Show answer

    Mahan. Civ VI has no blockade: a ship stops trade only by plundering a Trader, while blockade was the core of real command of the sea.

  3. A landlocked country and its coastal neighbor both export grain. Using heartland and rimland, which has more trade options, and which is easier to attack from the sea?

    Show answer

    The coastal country on both counts. Like Spykman's rimland it is rich and connected but open to fleets. The landlocked one is safe from the sea but must trade through chokepoints it does not control.

In the game: look at the map before the army. Coastal targets fall to ships; inland ones fall at chokepoints.

In the world: geography sets the price of every option a state has.

Part 5

Grand Strategy: How to Win

Civ VI has five ways to win, plus a score win at the turn limit. Each needs a different empire, so the first decision is which to chase.

Choosing Your Victory

Five finish lines, one empire, and every Campus (the science district) you build is a Holy Site (the faith district) you did not. Winning starts with a goal that fits your means, which strategists call grand strategy.

The six ways a game ends VI

The first player to meet any victory condition (the finish line for one kind of win) wins. The World Rankings panel at the top right shows everyone's progress toward each.

VictoryYou win when
ScienceYour exoplanet ship arrives after four space projects (Gathering Storm)
CultureYour visiting tourists outnumber every other civ's domestic tourists
DominationYou hold the original capital of every other civ
ReligiousYour religion is the majority in every civ still in the game
DiplomaticYou reach 20 Diplomatic Victory Points (Gathering Storm only)
ScoreNobody has won when the turn limit hits, and you have the highest score

The turn limit is 2050 AD, which is 500 turns at Standard speed and 250 at Online. Under Rise & Fall and Gathering Storm (the second Civ VI expansion) rules, score counts 15 points per Wonder (a one-of-a-kind building only one civ can own), 10 for founding a religion, 5 per city and per Great Person (a famous figure you recruit for one big effect), 3 per civic, 2 per tech and per district (a specialized city tile that holds buildings), 1 per building and per citizen, plus your Era Score. You can switch Score Victory off in Advanced Setup.

Sources: Victory (Civ6); Speed (Civ6)

Read three things before turn 10

  1. Your civ. Most leader and civ abilities point at one victory. The wiki's beginner list suggests Korea for Science, Greece for Culture, Russia for Religious, Mongolia for Domination and America for Diplomatic.
  2. Your map. Mountains and reefs feed Campuses, so they favor Science (Districts). Unspoiled land around mountains or a natural wonder favors Culture through National Parks. Few neighbors favor any peaceful victory.
  3. Your neighbors. A close, weak neighbor is an early Domination chance. A close, strong one means your victory must survive a war. An early Holy Site next door means a religion race.

Commit, but keep a backup

Pick a primary victory and a secondary one that uses the same buildings and yields. Science pairs with Diplomatic, because the Future Era tech Seasteads and civic Global Warming Mitigation each give 1 Diplomatic Victory Point, and science players reach them first. Culture pairs with Religious, because Faith (the yield spent on religion) buys Rock Bands (units that perform for Tourism). Domination pairs with Religious, because removing a civ's unconverted cities raises your religion's share there.

Pivot rules for any victory. Switch when (1) a rival's World Rankings lead in your victory grows at every check, (2) a hard gate shuts, such as every religion being founded, or (3) a war will cost more than the victory it protects. Switch to the backup that reuses most of what you built.

Strategy

Grand strategy (ends, ways, means)

Britain fought Napoleon with a great navy, deep pockets and a small army, and it picked ways to fit those means: a naval blockade, a modest force in Spain, and cash for allies who had the soldiers. In March 1814 at Chaumont, in France, the foreign secretary Lord Castlereagh wrote most of a treaty in which the four allies each promised 150,000 soldiers, and Britain paid to keep the other armies in the field. By the end of the month the allies were in Paris.

Grand strategy: a state's plan for using all its resources, military and civilian, to reach its biggest goals. Ends are the goals, means the resources, and ways how one becomes the other.

Open in the PDF, page 110

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Your chosen victoryEnds: the national goal
Districts, units, Gold, Faith, FavorMeans: the economy, the armed forces, allies
Build order, wars, treaties, policy cardsWays: the concrete plan

Where the map breaks

  • Civ hands you five fixed goals. Real states argue about what their ends even are, and the argument never stops.
  • You are one mind. Real grand strategy runs through ministries, parliaments and generals, each pulling its own way.

Read next

  • Accessible: Lykke, "Defining Military Strategy" (1989), a short article.
  • Primary: Liddell Hart, Strategy (1967), chapter XXII.

B. H. Liddell Hart, Strategy (2nd rev. ed., New York: Praeger, 1967; first ed. 1954); ends, ways and means from Arthur F. Lykke Jr., "Defining Military Strategy," Military Review 69(5) (1989).

Check yourself

  1. You play Korea, with mountains near your capital. Which primary and secondary victory does this lesson point to, and why does that pair work?

    Show answer

    Science primary: Korea is on the wiki's Science list, and mountains feed Campuses. Diplomatic secondary: Seasteads and Global Warming Mitigation give 1 Diplomatic Victory Point each, and science players reach them first.

  2. Your goal is a Culture victory, your resources are Faith and Wonders, and your plan is to buy Rock Bands. Name the concept and match each part.

    Show answer

    Grand strategy, in Lykke's terms. The end is the Culture victory, the means are Faith and Wonders, and the way is buying Rock Bands with Faith. The way has to fit the means.

  3. A small football club with little money wants promotion. Using ends, ways and means, what should it decide first, and what kind of plan should it avoid?

    Show answer

    Fix the end (promotion), then list the means (budget, young players, home ground) and pick ways that fit them, such as developing young players. Avoid a plan its means cannot pay for, such as buying stars.

In the game: name your primary and secondary victory before turn 10, and write them down.

In the world: a strategy is a choice of goal plus a plan that fits your resources.

Science Victory

Your scientists can lead all game and still lose the space race. The four launch projects cost 6,300 Production (the yield that builds units and buildings), so the finish goes to the factories.

The win condition VI

Every step happens in a Spaceport (the district that builds space projects), a district (a specialized city tile that holds buildings) you unlock with the Rocketry tech. It costs 1,800 Production and cannot go on Hills. Each project must finish before the next one starts. Costs below are for Standard speed.

StepTech neededProductionEffect
1. Launch Earth SatelliteRocketry900Reveals the whole map
2. Launch Moon LandingSatellites1,500One-time Culture equal to 10 times your Science per turn
3. Launch Mars ColonyNanotechnology1,800Unlocks step 4
4. Exoplanet ExpeditionSmart Materials2,100Launches a ship that must fly 50 light-years

The ship flies 1 light-year per turn, so 50 turns on its own. After the Offworld Mission (a technology) tech, two repeatable projects each add 1 light-year per turn: the Lagrange Laser Station (600 Production plus 30 Aluminum) and the Terrestrial Laser Station (600 Production, city must stay powered).

In the base game and Rise & Fall, step 3 is three separate Mars projects (Reactor, Hydroponics, Habitation), and finishing all three wins. Enemy spies can shut a Spaceport down with the Disrupt Rocketry mission (Espionage).

Sources: Spaceport (Civ6); Exoplanet Expedition (Civ6); Lagrange Laser Station (Civ6); Terrestrial Laser Station (Civ6)

The plan

  1. Ancient–Classical Expand. More cities means more Campuses (science districts). Put each Campus next to Mountains, Reefs or other districts for adjacency (extra yield from what sits next door) (+1 per Mountain, +2 per Reef, +0.5 per district).
  2. Medieval–Renaissance Libraries, then Universities. Take Great Scientists (famous scientists you recruit for one-time boosts) when you can. Rough target: a Campus in every city that has room for a district.
  3. Industrial–Modern Industrial Zones (production districts) in your best Production cities. The endgame needs Production more than Science.
  4. Atomic Rocketry. Spaceports in your two or three highest-Production cities, each guarded by a spy.
  5. Information–Future Run the four projects back to back, then laser stations in every Spaceport city until the ship lands.

Concept lens: in Romer's endogenous growth, ideas never run out, so a knowledge lead feeds itself. Clustered Campuses (science districts) are agglomeration on a map.

Pivot rule: if a rival finishes Launch Earth Satellite before you have Rocketry, you will not win the race by building faster. Send spies to Disrupt Rocketry in their Spaceport cities, and switch to your Diplomatic secondary.

The hard question: should you hire your enemy's scientists to win the race?

In the game a Great Scientist (a famous scientist you recruit for a one-time boost) arrives with no past. From 1945 to 1959 the United States brought over 1,600 German scientists and engineers, several of them former Nazi Party or SS members. Wernher von Braun, technical director of the army's rocket center at Peenemünde, had joined the Party in 1937 and the SS in 1940. His V-2 killed an estimated 9,000 people, and some 12,000 forced laborers and camp prisoners died building it. In America he became chief architect of the Saturn V Moon rocket.

The case for hiring them: the aim was to keep German expertise from the Soviets, who in one night in October 1946 shipped over 2,000 specialists east. Truman later said that, because of the Soviets, "this had to be done and was done". Only one recruit was ever tried for a crime, and he was acquitted.

The case against: von Braun admitted 12 to 15 visits to the underground factory where prisoners built his rockets, and called its conditions "repulsive". Hiring such men told the world that skill could buy forgiveness. In 1984 Arthur Rudolph, who had managed Saturn V development, gave up his US citizenship and left after a war-crimes investigation.

Your call: if a crime is already done, does using what its makers know make you part of it?

Check yourself

  1. Your Exoplanet Expedition has launched, and two cities each finish one laser station. How fast does the ship now fly, and about how many turns will 50 light-years take?

    Show answer

    1 light-year per turn on its own, plus 1 per station, makes 3 per turn: about 17 turns instead of 50. Each extra station adds another light-year per turn.

  2. You lead in Science, and each new tech seems to come faster than the last. Which idea explains why the lead feeds itself, and what does this lesson say limits you at the end?

    Show answer

    Romer's endogenous growth: ideas never run out, so knowledge compounds. The space projects cost 900 to 2,100 Production each, so factories decide the finish.

  3. Two neighboring towns each fund small research labs. One puts its labs side by side near the university; the other scatters them. Using this lesson's lens, which gains more?

    Show answer

    The clustered town. Agglomeration means nearby labs share people and ideas, the way a Campus gains +0.5 adjacency for each neighboring district.

In the game: Science wins on Production at the end, so build your Industrial Zones before you need them.

In the world: a lead in knowledge compounds, but turning knowledge into hardware still takes factories.

Culture Victory

Culture victory means making foreigners want what you have, with no army and no bribe. Real governments chase that power and find they cannot control it.

The win condition VI

Every civ has domestic tourists, its defense. That number is all the Culture it has made all game, divided by 100. Visiting tourists are your offense. Under Rise & Fall and Gathering Storm (the second Civ VI expansion) rules, you gain 1 from a civ for every (200 × number of civs) Tourism (cultural pull that wins a Culture victory) you send it over the game. In an 8-civ game that is 1,600 Tourism per tourist. The base game uses 150 instead of 200.

You are culturally dominant over a civ when your total visiting tourists exceed its domestic tourists. Dominate every civ and you win. Fewer civs in the game makes this easier.

Where Tourism comes from

  • Great Works from Great Writers (famous writers whose works earn Tourism), Artists and Musicians, plus Relics and Artifacts. Each needs a slot in a building.
  • Wonders (one-of-a-kind buildings only one civ can own).
  • National Parks. A Naturalist (a unit that creates National Parks) (Conservation civic) makes one on four tiles whose Appeal (how attractive a tile is), a tile's attractiveness score from Disgusting up to Breathtaking, is Charming or better. It gives Tourism equal to their total Appeal.
  • After Flight, improvements that make Culture also make Tourism. Seaside and Ski Resorts add more.
  • Rock Bands (units that perform for Tourism) (Gathering Storm), bought with Faith (the yield spent on religion) after the Cold War (a step on the culture tree) civic, from 600 Faith and rising. Each concert drops a burst of Tourism on the civ whose land it plays in.

Trade routes to a civ and sharing its government raise the Tourism you send it.

Sources: Victory (Civ6); National Park (Civ6); Rock Band (Civ6)

The plan

  1. Ancient–Classical Build early Wonders you can finish. Keep districts (specialized city tiles that hold buildings) off future National Park tiles.
  2. Medieval–Renaissance A Theater Square (the culture district) in most cities, with Amphitheaters and Art Museums for Great Work slots. Recruit Great Writers and Artists.
  3. Industrial–Modern Naturalists, National Parks, Archaeologists (units that dig up artifacts).
  4. Atomic onward Rock Bands and trade routes aimed at the civ you are furthest behind. Resorts on high-Appeal tiles.

International relations

Soft power

In 1956 the State Department sent Dizzy Gillespie and his integrated band on an eight-week tour abroad. Soviet propaganda pointed to the injustice Black Americans faced, and Black bandleaders and mixed bands were the answer. In 1957, after the Little Rock school crisis, Louis Armstrong said he would cancel a planned State Department tour of the Soviet Union: "The way they're treating my people in the South, the government can go to hell." A government can send its music abroad. It cannot own its musicians.

Soft power: getting others to want what you want through the appeal of your culture, values and policies, instead of through force or payment.

Open in the PDF, page 113

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Tourism from Great Works and WondersThe pull of a country's art, film and heritage
Rock Bands touring foreign citiesCultural diplomacy
Bonus Tourism to civs with the same governmentShared values make influence easier

Where the map breaks

  • In Civ, Tourism (cultural pull that wins a Culture victory) is a number you push. Nye stresses that a government does not control its own culture.
  • In the game attraction never backfires. A war or a scandal can wipe out decades of goodwill.
  • Civ's Culture victory only counts tourists; no one changes their mind.

Read next

  • Accessible: Nye, Soft Power: The Means to Success in World Politics (2004), chapter 1.
  • Primary: Nye (1990), below.

Joseph S. Nye Jr., Bound to Lead: The Changing Nature of American Power (1990); "Soft Power," Foreign Policy, no. 80 (Autumn 1990), pp. 153–171.

Pivot rule: if one civ's domestic tourists keep pulling away as the turn limit nears, switch to Score. A Culture empire is full of Wonders (15 points each) and Great People (a famous figure you recruit for one big effect) (5 each), so leave Score Victory on.

Check yourself

  1. In an 8-civ Gathering Storm game, how much Tourism must you send a civ over the game to gain one visiting tourist from it, and why is a smaller game easier?

    Show answer

    200 × 8 = 1,600 Tourism per tourist. The cost of each tourist grows with the number of civs, so fewer civs make every tourist cheaper.

  2. A rival switches to your government, and the Tourism you send it rises. What real concept does that bonus model, and name one way the game's version breaks.

    Show answer

    Soft power: shared political values make influence easier. The game's version breaks because attraction never backfires, and because Civ only counts tourists while no one changes their mind.

  3. A small city has a famous festival and wants more visitors. Using soft power, what builds its pull, and what could undo years of work quickly?

    Show answer

    Attraction it does not fully control: the festival, local food, a welcoming name. A scandal or a violent incident can wipe out goodwill fast, because attraction is slow to build and fast to lose.

In the game: find the civ with the most domestic tourists and aim everything at it.

In the world: attraction is real power, but it is slow to build and fast to lose.

Domination Victory

To win by conquest you must hold every original capital at once. Each one makes you stronger and more resented, and real empires have broken on that sum.

The win condition VI

You must control the original capital of every other civ in the game, including civs already knocked out. An original capital is the first city a civ founded. You do not have to take any other city.

What it costs

  • Grievances (a running record of who wronged whom) (Gathering Storm): a running score of how much each civ resents you, raised by war declarations, captures and razing. A casus belli, a recognized reason for war, reduces it.
  • Diplomatic Favor (a currency spent in the World Congress): you lose 5 Favor per turn for each original capital you occupy, and more for high grievances.
  • Military Emergency (a temporary pact against a rule-breaker): conquering another civ's city can unite rivals in an emergency to liberate it.
  • War weariness (unhappiness that grows the longer you fight): a growing Amenity loss while you fight.

Sources: Victory (Civ6); Grievances (Civ6); Diplomatic Favor (Civ6); Emergency (Civ6)

The plan

  1. Ancient–Classical Find the nearest capital. An early rush works best against a neighbor with few units and no walls. Bring siege support (Combat).
  2. Medieval–Renaissance Take one capital per campaign, then rest, heal and let grievances decay.
  3. Industrial onward Time pushes to your unit upgrades (Timing pushes). Bring ships for coastal capitals (Navies).
  4. Throughout Keep captured cities loyal (Loyalty).

Historical sociology

"War made the state"

In 1690 the French navy beat the Royal Navy off Beachy Head, and William III's government wanted £1.5 million to rebuild the fleet. Its credit was so poor it could not borrow the money. In 1694 Parliament let a company of lenders raise £1.2 million for the government at 8% a year, "towards the carrying on the Warr against France". Subscribers put up the sum in 12 days. The company was the Bank of England. The war ended in 1697, and the bank is still there.

"War made the state": modern states were built to fight wars. Rulers who wanted armies had to tax, borrow and count their people, and the offices that did it became the state.

Open in the PDF, page 115

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Encampments, Barracks, Gold spent on armiesStanding armies paid from taxes
Captured cities adding to your yieldsConquest funding the next war
Governments with military policy slotsInstitutions shaped by the needs of war

Where the map breaks

  • In Civ your state exists on turn 1, and you never bargain with taxpayers or bankers. In Tilly's account that bargaining built the state.
  • Tilly wrote about early modern Europe. Jeffrey Herbst argues it fits poorly in Africa, where land was plentiful and people scarce.

Read next

  • Accessible: the review essay "Testing Tilly: Does War Really Make States?"
  • Primary: Tilly (1990), chapters 1 and 3.

Charles Tilly, "Reflections on the History of European State-Making," in The Formation of National States in Western Europe (1975); Coercion, Capital, and European States, AD 990–1990 (1990).

History / IR

Imperial overstretch

Philip II of Spain ruled an empire fed by treasure from the Americas, and he fought the Ottomans, the English and the Dutch rebels, often at once. His treasury defaulted on its loans in 1557, 1560, 1575 and 1596. After the 1575 default his bankers stalled, and his army in the Netherlands went unpaid. On 4 November 1576 the unpaid soldiers mutinied and sacked Antwerp, the region's financial center. At least 7,000 people died. Four days later the provinces united to drive Spanish troops out. Spain defaulted six more times in the next 65 years.

Imperial overstretch: when a great power's military and territorial commitments grow faster than the economy that pays for them, the power declines.

Open in the PDF, page 116

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Unit maintenance eating your GoldMilitary spending crowding out investment
Conquered cities losing LoyaltyHolding distant, hostile territory
Grievances and Emergencies gathering against youRivals balancing against the leading power

Where the map breaks

  • Kennedy predicted relative decline for the United States in 1987. Critics point out that it stayed the leading power for decades after.
  • In Civ, captured cities usually turn a profit. Many real conquests never paid for their garrisons.

Read next

  • Accessible: the New York Review of Books retrospective "What Makes the World Go Round?" (2001).
  • Primary: Kennedy (1987), introduction and chapter 8.

Paul Kennedy, The Rise and Fall of the Great Powers: Economic Change and Military Conflict from 1500 to 2000 (1987).

Concept lens: every attack has a culminating point past which you get weaker as you advance. Stop there, not at the next city.

Pivot rule: if your army stalls two campaigns in a row, make peace and keep what you hold. Each city is worth 5 Score. Switch your secondary to Score or Religious.

The hard question: can conquest be justified by what it builds?

In the game a conquered city simply adds to your yields. From 58 to 50 BC Julius Caesar conquered Gaul, today's France, Belgium and Switzerland. He claimed to have killed over a million Gauls, a figure historians call implausible. In 55 BC he attacked the camp of two migrating peoples, the Usipetes and Tencteri, and slaughtered the men, women and children. His enemies in the Senate wanted to try him for war crimes.

The case for judging by results: Caesar presented the war as preemptive and defensive, and the historian Kurt Raaflaub argues it was not exceptionally brutal for its day. Gaul became Roman. Its towns grew into Lyon, Paris and Bordeaux, and its Latin became French. In AD 48 the emperor Claudius, born in Lyon, asked the Senate to admit wealthy Gallic citizens. In 212 every free person in the empire became a citizen.

The case against: historians agree Caesar fought mainly to boost his career and pay his debts. The historian Ben Kiernan calls the slaughter of the Usipetes and Tencteri a genocide. The benefits went to the survivors' descendants, centuries later, and the dead were never asked.

Your call: can benefits that arrive centuries later ever pay for a conquest's victims?

Check yourself

  1. You occupy the original capitals of two rivals. How much Diplomatic Favor does that cost you each turn, before any loss from grievances?

    Show answer

    10 Favor per turn: 5 for each occupied original capital. Grievance losses come on top, which is one reason the plan says take one capital per campaign, then rest.

  2. Your empire doubles, but unit upkeep eats your Gold, conquered cities lose Loyalty (how firmly a city stays yours) and Emergencies form against you. Name the concept and its central claim.

    Show answer

    Imperial overstretch (Paul Kennedy): when military and territorial commitments grow faster than the economy that pays for them, the power declines. Rivals balancing against the leader are part of the pattern.

  3. A café chain opens five branches in one year, and each needs a manager and stock before it earns money. Using this lesson, what warning sign should the owner watch?

    Show answer

    Costs of the new branches outrunning income from the old ones. Like an empire, the expansion is paid for by the existing base, and it fails when the bill outruns the income.

In the game: one capital per campaign, then rest; the bill for war arrives in Favor, grievances and Amenities (city contentment from luxuries and entertainment).

In the world: conquest is paid for by an economy, and empires fail when the bill outruns the income.

Religious Victory

Your religion must win a majority in every civ, including yours, and the window to found one shuts early. Faith (the yield spent on religion) spreads like real ideas, along roads and friendships.

The win condition VI

Your religion must be predominant in every civ still in the game, including your own. Predominant means it is the majority religion in more than half of that civ's cities.

Getting a religion

  • First adopt a pantheon, a small faith bonus, for 25 Faith.
  • Then earn a Great Prophet (a famous prophet who founds a religion) with Great Prophet points. Each Holy Site (the faith district) gives 1 point per turn, plus 1 per building in it. Stonehenge gives a free Prophet.
  • Only a limited number of religions can exist: 4 on a Small map, 5 on Standard, 6 on Large. When they are gone, nobody else can found one.

Spreading it

  • Cities spread their religions a little to cities within 10 tiles.
  • Missionaries (units that spread religion) convert cities with no religion yet. Apostles (religious units that spread and defend a faith) convert better and fight rival religious units in theological combat. Inquisitors (units that remove rival religions) clean your own cities.

You cannot win while a Religious Emergency (a temporary pact against a rule-breaker) is active. One starts when you convert another religion's Holy City. Once you convert a civ, it may declare a Holy War on you.

Sources: Victory (Civ6); Religion (Civ6); Great Prophet (Civ6)

The plan

  1. Ancient Holy Site early, Shrine next, then a pantheon.
  2. Classical–Medieval Found the religion. Pick beliefs that help spread it (Religion). Convert your own cities and nearest neighbor first.
  3. Medieval–Renaissance Apostles in groups against rival religions, Missionaries into cities with no religion. Convert rivals' Holy Site cities; religious units can only be bought there.
  4. Later Escort religious units if you are at war; enemy soldiers can kill them outright with Condemn Heretic. Keep Inquisitors at home.

Concept lens: Rodney Stark argued that early Christianity grew mainly through ties among family, friends and trade partners. That is network diffusion, and in Civ trade routes carry religious pressure too.

Pivot rule: if every religion is founded before you get a Great Prophet, drop Religious that turn. Your Holy Sites still make Faith, and Faith buys Rock Bands (units that perform for Tourism), so pivot to Culture.

Check yourself

  1. On a Standard map, four religions are founded and you still have no Great Prophet. What is at stake, and what does the pivot rule say if a rival founds the fifth first?

    Show answer

    A Standard map allows 5 religions, so one slot is left. If a rival takes it, nobody else can found one: drop Religious that turn and pivot to Culture, since Faith buys Rock Bands.

  2. Your religion keeps reaching cities tied to yours by nearness and trade routes, with no Missionary (a unit that spreads religion) sent. Which concept explains this, and whose study of early Christianity does the lesson cite?

    Show answer

    Network diffusion: ideas travel along ties between people. Rodney Stark argued that early Christianity grew mainly through ties among family, friends and trade partners.

  3. A school club wants more members and can afford either posters or a bring-a-friend event. Using network diffusion, which should it try first, and why?

    Show answer

    The bring-a-friend event. Ideas spread along existing relationships, so recruiting through members' friends uses the network. Posters reach strangers with no tie to the club.

In the game: the religion race is decided early; an early Holy Site with a Shrine is the ticket in.

In the world: ideas spread along relationships, so map who talks to whom before you spend on persuasion.

Diplomatic Victory

You need 20 points to win, and the AI gangs up to take two away as you near them. Helping others can help you win, here and in real diplomacy.

The win condition VI

This victory exists only in Gathering Storm (the second Civ VI expansion). The first player to reach 20 Diplomatic Victory Points (DVP) on standard settings wins. Points can also be lost.

SourceDVP
Voting for the winning outcome and target of a World Congress resolution+1
Building the Statue of Liberty+4
Building the Mahabodhi Temple+2
Building the Potala Palace+1
Researching Seasteads (Future Era tech)+1
Completing Global Warming Mitigation (Future Era civic)+1
Top score in an Aid Request or Military Aid Request+2
First place in another scored competition (not an Emergency)+1
Diplomatic Victory resolution, from the Modern Era+2 or −2

Wonder (a one-of-a-kind building only one civ can own) points go to the builder; capturing the Wonder later gives none.

How the World Congress works

  • It first meets once the world reaches the Medieval Era, then every 30 turns at Standard speed.
  • Votes cost Diplomatic Favor (a currency spent in the World Congress), a currency you earn each turn. Your first vote on each item is free. Each extra vote costs 10 Favor more than the last: 10, then 20, then 30.
  • Favor comes from your government (1 per turn per tier, so Democracy gives 3), each city-state (a minor power that cannot win) you are suzerain (the player who controls a city-state) of (+1), alliances, competitions and kept promises. You lose it for grievances, carbon emissions and occupied original capitals.

Sources: Victory (Civ6); World Congress (Civ6); Diplomatic Favor (Civ6); Statue of Liberty (Civ6)

The plan

  1. Ancient–Classical Meet everyone. Become suzerain of city-states (City-States). Try for Mahabodhi.
  2. Medieval–Renaissance Stay at peace, make alliances, and vote with the likely winner for a steady +1 per resolution.
  3. Industrial–Modern Build the Statue of Liberty and Potala Palace. Keep Gold for Aid Requests, which pay +2.
  4. Modern onward Spend Favor on the Diplomatic Victory vote. Pick up Seasteads and Global Warming Mitigation.

When you are 1 or 2 points from winning, AI leaders spend their Favor to take 2 points away. Vote prices rise steeply, so several civs pooling votes beat one rich civ. The wiki suggests a counter: when you will lose that vote anyway, vote "take away 2" on yourself. You lose 2 but gain 1 for backing the winner.

Concept lens: the Congress votes by plurality on an agenda you do not set, and Arrow showed that no ranked voting rule with three or more options passes every fairness test (Arrow's impossibility). Your steady +1 a session comes from cooperating again and again: repeated games at work.

Pivot rule: if a war breaks out on your border and costs you Favor every turn, your diplomatic lead will shrink. Defend, liberate cities if you can (it earns Favor and lowers grievances), and lean on Science as the backup.

The hard question: does a good deed count if it also serves you?

In the game, aid is a contest you enter for Diplomatic Victory Points. On 5 June 1947, at Harvard, Secretary of State George Marshall offered to help rebuild Europe: "Our policy is directed not against any country or doctrine but against hunger, poverty, desperation and chaos." From April 1948 the United States sent $13.3 billion to 17 European countries over four years. By 1952 every participant's economy had passed its pre-war level.

The case for counting it: the same offer went to the Soviet Union and its allies, and it was Moscow that refused. The recipients planned the aid together in a new body, the OEEC, which lives on as the OECD. A deed that serves both giver and receiver may be the kind that lasts.

The case against: the motives were mixed. Washington feared the strong communist parties in France and Italy and wanted to contain Soviet influence, and 83% of the money was spent in the United States. Some economists also find the direct boost modest: the aid came to about 3% of the recipients' national income.

Your call: does a good deed count for less when it also serves the giver?

Check yourself

  1. Your first vote on a resolution is free. What do your second, third and fourth votes cost in total, and why do several AIs pooling votes beat one rich civ?

    Show answer

    10 + 20 + 30 = 60 Favor. Each extra vote costs 10 more than the last, so several civs buying a few cheap votes each outvote one civ buying many expensive ones.

  2. The World Congress (the game's recurring global vote) decides by plurality, on an agenda you do not set. Which theorem says no ranked voting rule with three or more options passes every basic fairness test?

    Show answer

    Arrow's impossibility theorem. Because no counting rule is perfectly fair, the agenda and the rule shape the result as much as the votes do.

  3. A seven-member city council votes every month. A new member wants influence over the coming years. What does the idea of repeated games suggest she do?

    Show answer

    Cooperate reliably, for example by backing workable proposals, so others expect cooperation back. Repeated play rewards a steady reputation, like the +1 Diplomatic Victory Point for voting with the winner each session.

In the game: bank Wonder points early, then vote with the winning side every session.

In the world: in any body that votes, the agenda and the counting rule matter as much as the votes.

Write Your Grand Strategy Memo

Before turn 1, write down how you will win and what would make you switch. One university course in world politics asks students for exactly that.

Where this comes from IV

Jeffrey Berejikian's University of Georgia course INTL 4265, "Global Simulation," runs Civilization IV as a multiplayer model of world politics. Before play, each team writes a national security doctrine: the values its country will defend, its main challenges, its central objectives, and the policies to reach them. Teams weight their goals with 500 points (150 domestic, 350 foreign), log each decision, and afterward analyze why they succeeded or failed. The memo below is a one-page solo version.

Source: Berejikian, INTL 4265 syllabus (UGA, Fall 2018)

The template

HeadingWhat to write (one or two lines each)
EndsPrimary victory, secondary victory, and why each fits this civ and map.
WeightsSplit 100 points across your goals, for example growth, security, the victory engine.
MeansWhat you have: civ abilities, terrain, luxuries, nearby city-states.
WaysThree concrete moves, one per phase: early, middle, late.
ThreatsThe neighbor or rival most likely to stop you, and how.
Trigger to pivotOne observable event that makes you switch to the secondary.
Check-insThe eras when you will reread this memo.

A filled example

  • Ends: Science primary, Diplomatic secondary. Korea's Seowon (Korea's own Campus) replaces the Campus (the science district), and the start has a mountain range to the east.
  • Weights: Science engine 50, expansion 25, defense 15, city-state (a minor power that cannot win) relations 10.
  • Means: two Reefs, a Scientific city-state nearby.
  • Ways: early, Seowons by the mountains; middle, Universities and two Industrial Zones (production districts); late, two guarded Spaceports (districts that build space projects).
  • Threats: the warlike neighbor near my second city. Walls there by the Medieval era.
  • Trigger to pivot: a rival launches its Earth Satellite before I have Rocketry. Then Diplomatic becomes primary.
  • Check-ins: Medieval, Industrial and Atomic eras.

Lab: Does your plan survive the game?

Predict first: how many of your check-ins will say "on track", and will your pivot trigger fire? Write both guesses on the memo before turn 1.

  1. Write the memo before turn 1 of your next game.
  2. At each check-in, write one line: on track, behind, or pivot.
  3. After the game, write three sentences on why the plan worked or failed. Write as a policy analyst explaining causes, without defending your choices.

Measure: how many check-ins said "on track," and whether your trigger fired.

What you should see: each check-in forces a decision while switching is still cheap.

If your result differs: an early rush can wreck any plan, and Deity (the hardest difficulty) players report them between turns 20 and 35. Every religion may be founded before your Great Prophet (a famous prophet who founds a religion) arrives, shutting a hard gate. A rival's Earth Satellite before your Rocketry (a technology) fires the Science trigger. Each shows why a trigger must be an event you can see.

In the world: before your next month of spending, write what you expect to spend and one sign that means "cut back now". Check halfway through. A written trigger makes changing course easier, because you decided before you were committed.

In the game: write your ends, means, ways, threats and pivot trigger on one page before you press Start.

In the world: a plan with a written trigger to change course is easier to abandon when it fails.

Part 6

Climbing the Difficulty Ladder

What the eight Civ VI difficulty levels really change, how AI leaders pick friends and targets, and how to practice from Prince to Deity.

How the AI Thinks and Cheats

On Deity (the hardest difficulty) the computer is no smarter than on Prince (the default difficulty). It is richer, and like many real states it fears whoever is strong and preys on whoever is weak.

The rule: difficulty means AI bonuses VI

Turn 1 on Deity. Each AI rival starts with 3 Settlers (the units that found cities), 5 Warriors and 2 Builders (units that build farms, mines and the like), against your one Settler and one Warrior. It plays from the same long list of if-then rules as on Prince; the game makes it harder by handing it extra yields, units and combat strength. Below Prince, you get the help instead.

LevelAI Science, Culture, FaithAI Production, GoldAI starting SettlersAI combat strengthYour combat strengthAI free boosts
Settler+0%+0%1−1+30
Chieftain+0%+0%1−1+20
Warlord+0%+0%1−1+10
Prince+8%+20%1001
King+16%+40%1+102
Emperor+24%+60%2+203
Immortal+32%+80%2+304
Deity+40%+100%3+405

Forum data-miners disagree on whether Prince already gets the first +8%/+20% step, so treat Prince as roughly even. "Free boosts" are free Eurekas (40% research discounts for related tasks) and Inspirations (40% civic discounts for related tasks), which each cut 40% off a tech's or civic's cost (since Rise & Fall). The AI also starts with more Warriors (1 on Prince, 5 on Deity), extra Builders from King up (2 on Immortal (the second-hardest difficulty) and Deity), and earns 10% to 50% more combat experience from Prince to Deity. Below Prince you earn 15% to 45% more. In Gathering Storm (the second Civ VI expansion), city-states (minor powers that cannot win) start with Ancient Walls on Immortal and Deity.

An older beginners' guide gave a single "+80%" Deity bonus. That is wrong for Civ VI, where +80% is only the Immortal bonus to Production (the yield that builds units and buildings) and Gold. (VII Civ VII's Deity does give the AI +80% Gold, Science, Culture and Happiness 1.5.0; see How Civ VII Is Different.)

The yield bonuses grow steadily, but the big jumps are in the opening: 2 Settlers on Emperor and Immortal, 3 on Deity. Most of what makes Deity hard is that head start compounding. Combat strength is the other edge. On the damage curve, +4 multiplies the AI's damage by about 1.17 in every fight.

Sources: Difficulty level (Civ6), civilization.fandom.com; CivFanatics, "Which file governs AI difficulty bonuses".

Agendas: the AI's likes and dislikes VI

Each AI leader has a leader agenda, fixed and shown when you meet them, and in Gathering Storm two hidden agendas, chosen at random, one for the early game and one for the late game. You uncover hidden agendas by raising your diplomatic visibility, how much you know about a civ, through delegations, embassies, gossip and spies. An AI likes you more when you match its agendas and less when you break them.

  • Trajan (Optimus Princeps) likes civs with lots of territory.
  • Cyrus (Opportunist) often declares surprise wars and dislikes civs that never do.
  • Gorgo dislikes civs that have never gone to war, and never gives up items in a peace deal.
  • Montezuma wants every luxury and dislikes civs that hold one he lacks.

Sources: Agenda (Civ6); List of agendas in Civ6; Diplomacy (Civ6).

Handling the AI in practice VI

Community consensus (CivFanatics, Steam forums):

  • Sell spare luxuries. Each luxury type gives +1 Amenity (the contentment that keeps cities growing) to up to 4 of your cities, and a second copy adds nothing, so sell spares to the AI for Gold. Use "What would you give me?" to see its best offer.
  • Expect an early rush and prepare cheaply. Deity players commonly report AI rushes between turns 20 and 35; common advice is walls in frontline cities and 4 to 5 Archers. See Why Wars Start.
  • Make the AI fight your walls. The AI is widely reported to be weak at taking walled cities; one CivFanatics poster writes that it never has "enough siege to take Renaissance walls or Urban Defenses down."

Sources: Amenities (Civ6); Steam, "Trading Luxury Resources?" (2023); CivFanatics, "Getting Rolled on Deity" (2018) and "My Yearly 'Is the AI Any Good Yet' Thread" (2020).

International relations

Structural realism and the balance of power

On 2 March 1969 Chinese troops ambushed Soviet border guards on Zhenbao Island, in the Ussuri River. By October the Soviets were weighing a massive nuclear strike on China, and Beijing's government and military evacuated the city. To balance the Soviet threat, China reached out to the United States. In February 1972 Richard Nixon, who had made his name as a strong anti-communist, flew to Beijing and met Mao Zedong. Shared ideology had not held the two communist giants together. Fear of the Soviet Union brought the anti-communist and the communist to the same table.

Structural realism: with no world government above them, states respond mainly to how power is spread among them, more than to ideology or leaders. To survive, they balance against whoever grows too strong.

Open in the PDF, page 125

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Difficulty changes who has what: a Deity AI starts with 3 Settlers and +4 combat strength.The distribution of capabilities, which Waltz says explains most behavior.
Paranoid dislikes strong militaries; Cleopatra seeks allies among the strong.Balancing versus bandwagoning (Walt).

Where the map breaks

  • Civ AIs follow written personality agendas, the very thing Waltz says matters little.
  • Waltz explains broad patterns among great powers. One AI declaring war on you tests nothing.

Read next

  • Accessible: Stephen M. Walt, "International Relations: One World, Many Theories," Foreign Policy (1998).
  • Primary: Kenneth N. Waltz, Theory of International Politics (1979), chapters 5 and 6.

Kenneth N. Waltz, Theory of International Politics (Addison-Wesley, 1979)

International relations

Offensive realism

In April 1802 Thomas Jefferson wrote to Robert Livingston, his envoy in Paris, about Spain's cession of Louisiana to France. France had been, he wrote, "our natural friend". Yet "there is on the globe one single spot, the possessor of which is our natural and habitual enemy. It is New Orleans." If France took it, "we must marry ourselves to the British fleet and nation." Livingston had been sent to buy New Orleans. In 1803 Napoleon sold all of Louisiana instead: 828,000 square miles for $15 million. Jefferson, a strict reader of the Constitution who thought of amending it to justify the deal, nearly doubled the country on paper anyway.

Offensive realism: because no state can be sure what others intend, great powers try to gain as much power as they can, ideally becoming the only great power in their region. Only the strongest are safe.

Open in the PDF, page 126

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Deity AIs expand fast and rush the nearest weak neighbor.Grab power while the chance exists.
Your best Deity defense is to look too costly to attack.Weak states invite predation.

Where the map breaks

  • Civ has one winner, so every game is zero-sum. Real states can all survive and prosper together.
  • Mearsheimer says states seek regional dominance because crossing oceans is hard. Civ VI armies cross oceans easily late in the game.
  • Defensive realists such as Waltz answer that grabbing too much power provokes balancing and backfires.

Read next

  • Accessible: John J. Mearsheimer, "Anarchy and the Struggle for Power," chapter 2 of The Tragedy of Great Power Politics.
  • Primary: John J. Mearsheimer, The Tragedy of Great Power Politics (W. W. Norton, 2001).

John J. Mearsheimer, The Tragedy of Great Power Politics (W. W. Norton, 2001)

Concept lens: you can shape how the AI sees you, because its fear and its appetite both respond to your strength. Security dilemma and deterrence.

The hard question: is it right to crush the weak so the strong look strong?

On Deity the AI rushes its weakest neighbor, and the game pays it in cities. In 416 BC, during a truce with Sparta, Athens sent 38 ships and over 3,000 troops to Melos, a Spartan colony, hostile since Athenian raids. Thucydides, an exiled Athenian historian, wrote the talks as a debate. His Athenians say right counts only between equals in power, "while the strong do what they can and the weak suffer what they must" (Richard Crawley's translation). Melos refused to submit. After a siege Athens killed all the grown men it took, sold the women and children as slaves and settled 500 colonists.

The case for Athens: an empire held together by fear cannot let one island defy it. The Athenians said Melos's friendship would look like weakness to their other subjects, and its hostility like strength. Mercy could invite revolts, and more deaths.

The case against: the Melians warned that fair dealing protects the strong too, since Athens' fall "would be a signal for the heaviest vengeance". When Athens later lost its fleet, Xenophon wrote, no one slept, picturing the fate "they themselves had inflicted upon the men of Melos" (H. G. Dakyns's translation).

Your call: when you are strong, which serves you better: a name for ruthlessness, or for restraint?

Lab: How much richer is the AI on Deity?

Predict first: how many cities will the nearest AI have at turn 50 on Prince, on Emperor and on Deity? Write three numbers.

  1. Start a game on Prince with a fixed leader, and note the Map Seed and Game Seed in Advanced Setup. Play to turn 50 and save.
  2. Start games with the same leader and seeds on Emperor and on Deity. Make the same first 50 turns of choices as closely as you can.

Measure: at turn 50, the number of AI cities, the AI's score, and whether anyone declared war on you.

What you should see: the most visible gap is the AI's city count, from the extra Settlers. On Deity the nearest AI is the most likely to attack.

If your result differs: your guess probably underrated the Settler gap (1 on Prince, 2 on Emperor, 3 on Deity) or the AI's bonus Production (+60% on Emperor, +100% on Deity), which speeds everything its cities build. And a leader whose agenda favors surprise wars, such as Cyrus (Opportunist), may attack you on any level.

In the world: a head start compounds. Two friends save the same amount each month, but one starts with a gift of savings. After a year, compare the interest each earned. The gap comes from the starting position alone.

Do

  • Check each AI's agendas when you meet them.
  • Build walls before you think you need them on Emperor and up.

Avoid

  • Believing the AI is smarter on Deity.

Check yourself

  1. On Deity, what bonus does the AI get to Production and to Science, and how many Settlers does it start with? Where does the older guide's "+80%" fit?

    Show answer

    +100% Production and Gold, +40% Science, Culture and Faith, and 3 Settlers. In Civ VI, +80% is only Immortal's Production and Gold bonus; it is Civ VII's Deity that gives +80%.

  2. Across many games, rivals keep lining up against whichever civ grows strongest, whatever its leader's personality. Which theory predicts this, and who wrote it?

    Show answer

    Structural realism, from Kenneth Waltz (1979). The structure of the system, more than leaders or ideas, drives states to balance against a power that grows too strong.

  3. A small country borders a much stronger, expanding neighbor. Using offensive realism, what does the strong neighbor want, and what is the small country's best protection?

    Show answer

    Offensive realism says the strong state seeks as much power as it can, ideally dominance of its region, and that weakness invites predation. The small country's best protection is to look too costly to attack.

In the game: the AI's edge is a head start in cities and units, so survive the first 50 turns and the gap starts to close.

In the world: to predict a rival, look at its position before its personality.

Climbing the Ladder

Game after game is a slow way to get better. Practice aimed at one weakness, checked against a number, is faster, and this four-stage ladder is built on it.

The four stages (this book's framework, not the game's)

StageLevelsWhat you are learningMove on when
InitiateSettler to PrinceThe rules: growth, districts, combat, one victory typeYou win on Prince without the AI declaring a war you cannot handle
StrategistKing and EmperorPlanning: expansion pace, a victory plan from turn 1, defense on a budgetYou win on Emperor with two different victory types
ChampionImmortal and DeityEfficiency: fast expansion, surviving rushes, turning AI weaknesses into winsYou win on Deity most of the time
MultiplayerHuman opponentsReading people: bluffs, alliances, speedSee Multiplayer

Rough targets VI

Rough targets from CivFanatics players, for Standard speed; map size and civ change them a lot. City quality (population and districts) matters as much as count.

BenchmarkRough targetWho says so
Cities at turn 100, KingAbout 6; 10 is a strong startVictoria, "Milestones and Yardsticks"
Cities at turn 100, DeityAbout 10 ("a common goal")Several players, same thread; BTSeven7, "Benchmarks"

The older guide's "6+ cities by T100" is a fair King target but too low to judge Deity (the hardest difficulty) play. One Deity player puts it this way: "If I only have 5 I will struggle, if I have 10 I have it made."

Sources: CivFanatics, "Milestones and Yardsticks" and "Benchmarks".

Learning science

Deliberate practice

For a 1993 study, K. Anders Ericsson and two colleagues asked professors at the Music Academy of West Berlin to pick violin students. Ten had the potential for careers as international soloists, ten were good, and ten were future music teachers. Each estimated their weekly hours of practice alone, year by year. By age 18 the best averaged 7,410 hours, the good 5,301 and the future teachers 3,420. Skill lined up exactly with solitary practice. A double-blind replication in 2019 found a smaller effect, and the clean step from group to group did not reappear.

Deliberate practice: effortful practice aimed at one specific weakness, just beyond your current ability, with fast feedback and usually a teacher's design.

Open in the PDF, page 129

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Replaying turns 1 to 60 from one save, changing one choice.Repeat one task with one variable changed.
Benchmarks at turn 100 (cities, Science).A clear measure of performance.

Where the map breaks

  • A 2014 meta-analysis (Macnamara, Hambrick and Oswald) found practice explained about 26% of the differences in performance in games, 21% in music and 18% in sports. It matters, but far less than claimed.
  • The popular "10,000 hours" rule is not a finding of the paper; it comes from Malcolm Gladwell's retelling.
  • Ericsson required a teacher who designs the practice. Most Civ players coach themselves.

Read next

  • Accessible: K. Anders Ericsson and Robert Pool, Peak: Secrets from the New Science of Expertise (2016).
  • Primary: Ericsson, Krampe and Tesch-Römer, Psychological Review 100(3): 363–406, 1993; then Macnamara, Hambrick and Oswald, Psychological Science 25(8), 2014.

K. Anders Ericsson, Ralf T. Krampe and Clemens Tesch-Römer, "The Role of Deliberate Practice in the Acquisition of Expert Performance," Psychological Review 100(3) (1993)

A practice plan

  1. Week 1 Pick one skill from your last loss, such as early defense, expansion pace or district (a specialized city tile that holds buildings) timing.
  2. Week 1 Play the same map seed to turn 60 three times, changing only that one skill. Note cities, Science and Culture per turn at turn 60.
  3. Week 2 Play a full game at your current level. At turns 50, 100 and 150, write the numbers from the rough targets table.
  4. Week 3 Compare with the targets. Pick the biggest gap as your next skill. Move up one level only after two wins in a row.
  5. Always After a loss, reload 20 turns earlier and replay the moment it went wrong.

Lab: Do more cities mean more Science?

Predict first: with 2 more cities at turn 100, will your Science per turn rise, fall or stay level? Guess the change as a number.

  1. Play a King game, Standard speed, and save at turn 100.
  2. Count your cities, their total population and how many have two or more districts.
  3. Replay from turn 1 aiming for 2 more cities by turn 100, keeping everything else the same.

Measure: Science and Culture per turn at turn 100 in each run.

What you should see: more cities usually means more Science and Culture by turn 100, unless the extra cities are too small to build districts.

If your result differs: cities squeezed onto poor land stay too small for districts, so they add little. Each luxury gives an Amenity to only 4 cities, so more cities can mean unhappy ones that grow slowly. And Settlers (the units that found cities) built instead of defenders can invite an AI attack that costs more than the cities add.

In the world: more branches do not always mean more output. Compare a chain with many small shops and one large store in your town: count staff and opening hours as well as doors. Size and quality both matter, as with Civ cities.

Do

  • Practice one skill at a time, from a save.
  • Track the same three numbers every game.

Avoid

  • Jumping from Prince (the default difficulty) straight to Deity.
  • Judging a game by city count alone.

Check yourself

  1. Your Deity game has 6 cities at turn 100. Using the rough targets, are you on track, and what does the practice plan say to do next?

    Show answer

    Behind: about 10 is the Deity rough target, and one player says 5 means struggling. Make expansion pace your one skill, and replay the same seed to turn 60 changing only that.

  2. You replay turns 1 to 60 three times from one seed, changing only your expansion pace and noting the numbers. Name the concept and one limit the lesson gives.

    Show answer

    Deliberate practice (Ericsson and colleagues, 1993). One limit: a 2014 meta-analysis found practice explained only about 26% of performance differences in games. Another: Ericsson required a teacher, and most Civ players coach themselves.

  3. A student plays her favorite piano pieces for an hour a day and has stopped improving. What change does deliberate practice suggest?

    Show answer

    Pick one weakness just beyond her ability, such as a hard passage, and drill just that passage with fast feedback, ideally from a teacher. Playing what is already comfortable adds hours without much gain.

In the game: pick one weakness, replay it from a save, and measure it at turn 100.

In the world: skill grows fastest from focused, measured practice on your weakest point.

Part 7

Civilization VII

Civ VII keeps the hex map and turn-by-turn empire building but changes the shape of a game. This part teaches the rules as of patch 1.5.0 and its 30 September 2026 hotfix, and the two ideas the game leans on hardest, collapse and creative destruction.

What's Different From Civ VI

Your Civ VI reflexes will cost you here. There are no Builders (units that build farms, mines and the like) or Eurekas (40% research discounts for related tasks), settling everywhere has a price, and every age the game pulls the leader back toward the pack.

The big changes VI VII

As in Civ VI, settlements on a hex map turn tiles into yields such as Food, Production (the yield that builds units and buildings), Gold, Science and Culture. Civ VII adds Influence, which pays for diplomacy, and Happiness, which keeps settlements working.

FeatureCiv VI VICiv VII VII
Shape of a gameOne long game through eras.Three ages: Antiquity, Exploration, Modern. Tech and civic trees start fresh in each.
Your civilizationOne civ, one leader, all game.Your leader stays. Each age you pick a new civ or keep yours 1.4.0.
Improving landBuilders, a unit with 3 build charges.No Builders. Each time a settlement grows, you choose a tile to improve.
New settlementsEvery settlement is a city.Only the capital starts as a city. Others are towns, which turn Production into Gold until you pay to upgrade them.
How many settlementsNo cap.A limit of 3, 8 and 16 by age. Each settlement over it costs 5 Happiness in every settlement you own, uncapped 1.4.0.
Building spaceSpecialty districts, one per tile, with adjacency bonuses.Buildings sit on urban tiles, two per tile. Two buildings from the current age make a Quarter.
ArmiesUnits move one at a time.Commanders carry up to 4 units, earn experience and keep it from age to age.
Diplomacy currencySeparate systems: Diplomatic Favor, Envoys, spies, trade route limits.One yield, Influence, pays for nearly all of it.
Research shortcutsEurekas and Inspirations cut 40% of a tech or civic when you do a task.No Eurekas. Masteries: an optional second research of a tech or civic, at about 80% of its cost, for extra bonuses.
Goals inside an ageEra Score from Historic Moments (Rise & Fall onward).Triumphs: about 30 goals per age in six themes. Major ones unlock Dedications for the next age 1.4.0.
WinningFive victory types with fixed finish lines, such as 20 Diplomatic Victory Points.Lead second place by a set multiple for 5 turns (Military, Cultural, Economic), or race to 100 points (Science). Otherwise top score wins 1.4.0.

Sources: Patch 1.4.0 notes; Settlement (Civ7); Influence (Civ7); Builder (Civ6); Boost (Civ6).

The ages matter most. A bigger empire makes more and spends it on growing, so a lead feeds itself. The Civ VII wiki calls age transitions a way back in for players who fall behind.

Concept lens: path dependence says early choices lock in later ones. Civ VII's age resets try to loosen that lock. Full lesson

The hard question: should the rules stop a winner from winning too much?

Civ VII pulls the leader back every age. In 1911 America did it to a company. John D. Rockefeller's Standard Oil controlled 91% of American oil refining by 1904, helped by secret railroad rebates. On 15 May 1911 the Supreme Court ruled it an unreasonable monopoly and split it into more than thirty companies. Each shareholder got a slice of each one. Within ten years their combined value rose fivefold, and Rockefeller's fortune reached about $900 million, 2.3% of American output in 1913.

The case for the breakup: a firm that refines nine tenths of a necessity can set its price and choose which rivals survive. Ida Tarbell's 1904 history laid out its methods. The government charged that where competition was active, Standard cut prices "to a point which leaves even the Standard little or no profit", and charged "altogether excessive prices" where it met none.

The case against: customers did well. Kerosene fell in price by nearly 80% over the company's life, and defenders said Standard was simply the better competitor. By 1911 at least 147 refiners competed with it, Gulf, Texaco and Shell among them. And the breakup left the same owners holding the oil.

Your call: at what size does winning the game become a reason to change its rules?

Lab: Feel the brake at an age change

Predict first: write down whether your Science per turn will rise, hold or fall on the first turn of the new age, and by roughly how much.

  1. Play an Antiquity (Civ VII's first age) game until the end-of-age countdown starts (10 turns before the end by default, since 1.2.3), or load a save from that point.
  2. On the last turn of Antiquity, write down your Science and Culture per turn from the top of the screen.
  3. Make your transition choices. On the first turn of Exploration, before you build anything, write the same two numbers.
  4. Play 10 more turns, overbuild one outdated Quarter (see Quarters and Overbuilding), and write them again.

Measure: Science and Culture per turn at the three points.

What you should see: a dip on the first turn of Exploration. Every Antiquity building that is not ageless turns outdated and loses its adjacency (extra yield from what sits next door) bonuses and special effects, though it keeps its base yields. Overbuilding (replacing an old building with a newer one) starts to win the loss back.

If your result differs: no dip means most of your yields came from base yields, which outdated buildings keep, or from wonders and other ageless buildings, or your new civ and Dedications (bonuses picked when a new age or era begins) added more than you lost. A big dip means you had stacked adjacency, which is exactly what goes out of date.

In the world: in the US National Football League, barring trades, the team with the worst record picks first in every round of the next draft. Compare this year's draft order with last season's standings to see a brake on a runaway leader.

Check yourself

  1. In Antiquity you own 4 settlements and nothing has raised your settlement limit (how many settlements you can hold before penalties). How much Happiness does each settlement lose, and which Civ VI habit led you here?

    Show answer

    The Antiquity limit is 3, so you are 1 over: −5 Happiness in every settlement. Civ VI has no settlement cap, so "settle everywhere" is a Civ VI habit that now has a price.

  2. A friend says Civ VII's ages make early choices meaningless. Using path dependence, say what the age resets loosen and what still carries over.

    Show answer

    They loosen lock-in: tech and civic trees start fresh and you can pick a new civ. Your leader stays and Commanders keep their experience, so early choices still shape later ones.

  3. A sports league gives its weakest team the first pick of new players each year. What does that rule share with Civ VII's age transitions?

    Show answer

    Both are brakes on snowballing. A strong team or empire turns its lead into more strength, so the designers give trailing players a way back in.

In the game: drop three Civ VI reflexes on day one: building Builders, settling cities everywhere, and waiting for Eurekas.

In the world: when the rules of a system change, ask which of your habits were answers to the old rules.

Ages and Crises

At 70% of Antiquity or Exploration the game turns on you. Plague, revolt or invasion arrives, with policies that mostly hurt. Rome met its crises with more officials, soldiers and taxes. Its fall may have left many better off.

Age progress and the transition VII

Every age has an Age Progress meter, shown as a percentage. Each turn adds a little. Researching Future Techs or Future Civics adds more, and so does eliminating a civilization (except in Modern). At 100% the age ends for every player at once, in the age transition.

At the transition you choose your civ for the next age, may swap your Mementos (small leader bonuses you unlock by playing), and pick 3 Dedications (bonuses picked when a new age or era begins). New tech and civic trees appear, new resources fill the map, and the playable map grows. Your leader, relationships, Traditions (policies from a civ's own civic tree), wonders, Unique Quarters (Civ VII tiles holding two buildings), ageless buildings and Commanders (Civ VII units that lead armies)' experience carry over. So do the yield bonuses of settlements you captured, while conquest penalties end with the age 1.4.0. Older buildings keep weaker yields until you build over them (see Quarters and Overbuilding).

Continuity or Regroup 1.2.3

A setup option decides how much survives a transition. Continuity, the default, keeps most of your empire, units and alliances. Regroup, the launch rules, leaves you fewer units and less Gold and Influence (a diplomacy currency), and ends alliances.

Choosing your next civ, or keeping it 1.4.0

Your options come from a historical or geographic link (Egypt to the Abbasids), from your leader (Himiko always unlocks Meiji Japan), or from what you did in the game, and you can also keep your civ. A civ is at full strength in its Apex age, with its unique units, buildings and civic tree. In other ages it is Time-Tested, with a smaller civic tree, though its unique ability works in every age.

Crises VII

Antiquity (Civ VII's first age), 70% Age Progress. A Crisis (a Civ VII late-age challenge) begins, and the game makes you slot a Crisis Policy. Most of the choices hurt. Exploration ends the same way; the Modern Age (Civ VII's third age) has no Crisis. Each game picks one theme at random: Invasion, Plague or Revolts in Antiquity, Plague or Religious Revolts in Exploration (Revolutions was removed in 1.4.1). Bad events hit settlements, and more slots open as the meter climbs.

Age ProgressCrisis policies you must slotEvents
70%1Light
80%2More frequent
90%3–4Severe
100%0Crisis ends; the next age begins

Crisis policies grow harsher as your empire grows.

Sources: Age (Civ7); Patch 1.1.1 notes; Crisis (Civ7); Starting a new game (Civ7); Patch 1.4.0 notes; Patch 1.4.1 notes.

History / Systems

Collapse of complex societies

Diocletian became Roman emperor in 284, after fifty years of near-collapse. His answer was more of everything. He shared rule among four emperors, doubled the provinces from fifty to almost a hundred, and enlarged the army and the civil service. Taxes rose to pay for it. Lactantius, a hostile writer who had taught rhetoric at his court, wrote that "there began to be fewer men who paid taxes than there were who received wages" (in William Fletcher's translation). Joseph Tainter, an anthropologist, argues that each layer bought less than the last, and that when the west broke into kingdoms, many ordinary people were better off.

Collapse: a society solves its problems by adding complexity, each new layer costing more and returning less, until it suddenly sheds much of that complexity at once.

Open in the PDF, page 135

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Crisis policies get harsher as your empire growsA bigger, more complex state carries higher fixed costs when a shock hits
Each settlement over the limit costs 5 Happiness in every settlement, with no capEach added unit of complexity pays back less than the one before
Regroup strips units, Gold and alliances at the transitionCollapse as simplification: smaller units, less central control
