Where the map breaks

  • The game schedules the crisis at 70% on a meter. Tainter's collapses had no calendar. They came from inside, when complexity stopped paying.
  • Every player faces the crisis at once and survives it. Real collapses hit some societies and spared their neighbors.
  • Tainter's thesis is contested. Patricia McAnany and Norman Yoffee argue that many "collapses" were long transformations in which people adapted and endured.

Read next

  • Accessible: Patricia A. McAnany and Norman Yoffee (eds.), Questioning Collapse (Cambridge University Press, 2010), the critics' case.
  • Primary: Tainter (1988).

Joseph A. Tainter, The Collapse of Complex Societies (1988).

Lab: Can a bigger empire ride out the crisis?

Predict first: write down what share of your yields per turn you will lose between 65% and 95% Age Progress, and whether a bigger empire loses a bigger share.

  1. At 65% Age Progress in Antiquity, save and note your yields per turn from the top of the screen. Note them again at 75%, 85% and 95%.
  2. Replay from the save. This time found one more settlement (build or buy a Settler) before 70%, staying within your settlement limit (how many settlements you can hold before penalties), and note the same numbers.

Measure: the drop in yields from 65% to 95% in each run.

What you should see: yields stall as crisis policies stack up. Compare how much each run lost. Crisis policies scale with empire size, so the run with the extra settlement should feel harsher policies.

If your result differs: crisis events are random, so one destroyed building or a settlement in Unrest can swing a run. If no crisis came at all, Crises may be switched off in Advanced Setup, where since 1.2.2 you can pick which ones run. Growth finished before 70% also cushions the fall.

In the world: find a business news story about a firm closing a division in a downturn. Ask which layers it added in good years and what they cost to keep once revenue fell. That is Tainter's question in miniature.

Check yourself

  1. Exploration's Age Progress reaches 80%. How many Crisis Policies must you slot now, and what changes at 90%?

    Show answer

    Two at 80%, with more frequent bad events. At 90% you must slot 3 or 4 and events turn severe. The crisis ends at 100%, when the transition begins.

  2. Crisis policies get harsher as your empire grows. Which part of Tainter's theory of collapse does that match, and where does the game's version break?

    Show answer

    It matches the rising cost of complexity: a bigger state carries more upkeep when a shock hits. It breaks because the game schedules the crisis at 70%, while Tainter's collapses come from inside, when complexity stops paying.

  3. A town council has added a new department every year. Then a recession cuts its revenue. Using Tainter's idea, what should the council ask about each department?

    Show answer

    What it costs to keep compared with what it now returns. Each added layer tends to return less than the one before, so low-return layers are where cuts hurt least when times get bad.

In the game: treat 70% Age Progress as a deadline. Finish your growth push before it, then play for stability.

In the world: every new layer of an organization needs upkeep, so price it for the bad years as well as the good.

Towns, Cities and the Settlement Limit

Antiquity allows 3 settlements without penalty. Your fourth brings good land and costs 5 Happiness in every settlement you own, itself included. A fifth makes it 10 in each, with no ceiling.

Towns and cities VII

Your capital is founded as a city. Every other settlement you found starts as a town.

  • A city has a production queue. It builds units, buildings and wonders, and it can hold Specialists.
  • A town has no production queue. All its Production (the yield that builds units and buildings) turns into Gold. It can buy some buildings with Gold, and it cannot hold Specialists (citizens working buildings instead of tiles).

A new town starts on the Growing Town focus, which gives +50% growth. At 7 population you can pick a different focus. A focused town sends its Food to connected cities and gets the focus bonus, such as Farming Town, Mining Town, Trade Outpost or Fort Town. The choice is locked for the age, except that you can switch back to Growing Town. Since 1.4.0 focus bonuses no longer grow by age, and focused towns can buy more kinds of buildings.

Converting a town to a city

A town can pay Gold to become a city. The price rises with every city you already own, including your capital, and falls by 50 Gold for each point of the town's population.

Cost partAntiquityExplorationModern
Base cost400700800
Added per city you own+300+700+1,000
Off per point of town population−50−50−50
Minimum200200200
Maximum1,0003,0005,000

Example: in Antiquity (Civ VII's first age) you own only your capital, and a town has 5 population. The price is 400 + 300 − 250 = 450 Gold. A town you captured costs double, up to the cap.

The settlement limit VII

The settlement limit (how many settlements you can hold before penalties) is how many settlements you can own without a penalty. It starts each age at 3 (Antiquity), 8 (Exploration) and 16 (Modern), or higher if you ended the last age above that. Every settlement over the limit costs −5 Happiness in each of your settlements, so two over the limit means −10 in every one. Since 1.4.0 there is no cap on this penalty. The techs Irrigation, Feudalism, Urbanization and Mass Production raise it, as do some civics, leader attribute nodes, city-state (a minor power that cannot win) bonuses and civ or leader abilities. The Memento Corona Civica adds +1 per age but makes converting towns to cities 50% dearer.

Sources: Town (Civ7); Settlement (Civ7); Patch 1.4.0 notes.

Each conversion costs more than the last, so convert your best-placed town first.

Concept lens: the uncapped over-limit penalty is imperial overstretch in miniature: each extra holding adds a cost every other holding pays, until the costs outrun the gains. Full lesson

Lab: Cross the settlement limit on purpose

Predict first: write down how much Happiness each of your settlements, the new one included, will lose when you found one settlement over your limit.

  1. In Antiquity, own exactly as many settlements as your limit: 3, unless a tech such as Irrigation or another bonus has raised it. Save the game.
  2. Open each settlement and write down its Happiness per turn.
  3. Build or buy a Settler (the unit that founds a city) and found one more settlement. Next turn, write each settlement's Happiness again.
  4. Reload the save afterward if you want the settlement back off your books.

Measure: each settlement's Happiness per turn before and after.

What you should see: every settlement, the new one too, loses 5 Happiness to the over-limit penalty. A second settlement over the limit would make it 10 in each.

If your result differs: no change means your limit was higher than you thought, from Irrigation, a leader attribute node or a city-state's Hegemon bonus. A bigger change means something else moved Happiness that turn, such as a new building's upkeep, a Specialist or war weariness (unhappiness that grows the longer you fight).

In the world: a shop owner who opens a branch also adds head-office work that every branch shares. Ask anyone who opened a second location what happened to the first one.

Check yourself

  1. In Antiquity you own only your capital and a town with 6 population. What will converting the town to a city cost?

    Show answer

    400 base, plus 300 for the capital, which counts as a city, minus 6 × 50 = 300: 400 Gold. That sits between the 200 minimum and the 1,000 cap.

  2. Your Exploration empire sits at its limit of 8. A ninth settlement would add good tiles. What does imperial overstretch tell you to count before founding it?

    Show answer

    The cost it puts on all the others. All 9 settlements would lose 5 Happiness each. Overstretch says to weigh the new holding's gain against what every other holding pays for it.

  3. A café owner runs 3 shops well. Each new branch takes some of the owner's time, which every branch shares. Why might shop 5 add less than shop 2 did?

    Show answer

    Each branch adds a cost every other branch pays: less of the owner's attention. Past some point those shared costs outrun the new shop's sales, like the penalty that hits every settlement.

In the game: treat the settlement limit as a budget. Going over is allowed, but each step costs 5 Happiness in every settlement.

In the world: growth that adds a fixed upkeep each time needs a different test than growth that pays for itself.

Quarters and Overbuilding

Kodak built the first digital camera in 1975 and filed for bankruptcy in 2012 as film faded. Every Civ VII age change does the same to your buildings, and you still pay their upkeep.

Districts and Quarters VII

When a city builds its first building on a tile, that tile becomes an urban district (a specialized city tile that holds buildings). Each urban tile has room for 2 buildings (walls do not count). When the second building goes up, the tile becomes a Quarter (a Civ VII tile holding two buildings). Some Modern buildings, such as the Rail Station, fill a whole tile and make a Quarter alone. Many bonuses apply only to Quarters, so fill tiles in pairs. A Unique Quarter, two of a civ's unique buildings on one tile, is ageless and can last the whole game.

Adjacency (extra yield from what sits next door) is the bonus a building gets from its neighbors, +1 for each. The wiki's rule of thumb:

  • Culture and Happiness buildings like Mountains and Natural Wonders.
  • Gold and Food buildings like Coast and Navigable Rivers.
  • Science and Production (the yield that builds units and buildings) buildings like resources.
  • Every building likes Wonders (one-of-a-kind buildings only one civ can own).

Overbuilding VII

The first turn of Exploration. Your capital's best Quarter has lost every adjacency bonus, yet nothing was destroyed. Its buildings are outdated. At an age change, every building from the old age that is not ageless goes outdated. It loses its adjacency bonuses and special effects, stops counting toward a Quarter, and keeps its full maintenance. The tile drops back to a district. The fix is to overbuild. A new building on the tile replaces the old one with the lowest Production cost. Wonders, unique buildings and Warehouse buildings such as the Granary are ageless and never go out of date.

Sources: Quarter (Civ7); Building (Civ7); Patch 1.4.0 notes.

Plan your best tiles twice, putting this age's buildings where next age's will also want to be.

Economics

Creative destruction

In 1975 Steven Sasson, an electronics engineer at Kodak, built a camera that used no film. It weighed 3.6 kilograms, took pictures of 100 by 100 pixels, and needed 23 seconds to record each one to a cassette tape. Kodak executives watched it work and said they saw no market for it. Sasson was granted a patent in 1978, and the camera never went into production. Film stayed Kodak's core business. As digital photography replaced film, that business shrank, and in January 2012 Kodak filed for bankruptcy protection. It had invented its own replacement.

Creative destruction: capitalism grows by replacing old products, methods and firms with new ones, so the new makes the old worthless and growth and destruction are one process.

Open in the PDF, page 139

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Age change makes old buildings outdatedA new technology makes old capital obsolete
Outdated buildings keep full maintenance and lose their bonusesOld plant still costs money to run after it stops earning

Where the map breaks

  • In the game a calendar triggers the destruction. In Schumpeter, rivals trigger it by innovating, and the new usually comes from outsiders.
  • You own both the old building and its replacement. Kodak owned both too, and kept betting on the old one.
  • Your new building always beats the old one. Real innovations often fail, and the losses fall hardest on workers tied to the old trade.

Read next

  • Accessible: Philippe Aghion, Céline Antonin and Simon Bunel, The Power of Creative Destruction (Harvard University Press, 2021).
  • Primary: Schumpeter (1942), chapter 7, "The Process of Creative Destruction."

Joseph A. Schumpeter, Capitalism, Socialism and Democracy (1942).

The hard question: who pays for progress?

When you overbuild, nobody loses a job. In March 1811 framework knitters in Nottinghamshire, soon called Luddites, began smashing wide knitting frames that produced cheap, inferior work with fewer hands. In March 1812 Parliament made frame-breaking a capital crime. Lord Byron opposed it in his first speech in the Lords: "These men never destroyed their looms till they were become useless, worse than useless." About 12,000 troops were sent, more, the historian Eric Hobsbawm wrote, than Wellington led into Portugal in 1808. At York in January 1813, 17 men were hanged, three for murdering a mill owner.

The case for the owners: the frames were lawful property, and cheaper goods served every buyer. Lord Liverpool, Secretary for War, argued that frame-breakers were hard to catch, so "the terror of the law" would often work where lesser punishments would not. Britain grew rich. From 1840 to 1900 output per worker rose 90% and real wages 123%.

The case against: the knitters struck only after their trade was gone, and death for a broken machine priced a frame above a life. The riches came late. From 1780 to 1840 output per head rose about 46% and working-class wages 12%, a lag now called Engels' pause.

Your call: when a new method makes a whole trade worthless, who should carry the loss, and for how long?

Lab: Count adjacency before you build

Predict first: pick the tile for your first Library. Write down how much Science adjacency it will get there.

  1. In Antiquity (Civ VII's first age), research Writing, which unlocks the Library, a Science building that gets +1 Science for each adjacent resource and each adjacent Wonder.
  2. In your capital, find two tiles where the Library can go. For each, count the touching tiles with a resource or a Wonder.
  3. Pick the Library in the Production menu and note the base Science it lists.
  4. When the game asks where to place it, hover each of your two tiles and note the Science gain the placement view shows. Place it on the better one.

Measure: for each tile, the Science gain the placement view shows minus the Library's base Science.

What you should see: +1 Science for each adjacent resource or Wonder you counted.

If your result differs: a lower number usually means you counted a tile two hexes away, or a feature such as a Mountain, which feeds Culture buildings. A higher one can come from a leader ability or Wonder that adds Science; the placement view's detailed breakdown itemizes the gain.

In the world: shops cluster for the same reason. A café beside a busy station earns more than the same café on a quiet street. Walk any high street and note which businesses sit next to which.

Check yourself

  1. A Quarter holds two Antiquity buildings, neither ageless, when Exploration begins. What happens to that tile, and what fixes it?

    Show answer

    Both go outdated: they lose adjacency and special effects but keep full maintenance, and the tile drops back to a district. Overbuild both with Exploration buildings; two current-age buildings make a Quarter again.

  2. In Civ VII you overbuild your own outdated buildings. In Schumpeter's creative destruction, who usually brings the new, and who loses?

    Show answer

    Outsiders usually bring the new, by innovating, and incumbents often lose. In the game you own both the old and the new building, so it hides the firms and workers who lose out.

  3. A print shop's old press still costs money to maintain after most customers have moved to digital printing. What does this lesson tell the owner to count?

    Show answer

    The old press's upkeep against what it still earns. Like an outdated building at full maintenance, an obsolete asset keeps costing money after its edge is gone, so replacing it early can pay.

In the game: after each age change, overbuild your outdated Quarters first; they pay full upkeep without their adjacency bonuses.

In the world: when something new makes your assets obsolete, keeping them running is a cost, so count it.

Growth, Specialists and Happiness

Your capital just grew. Improve a tile, or make the citizen a Specialist (a citizen working a building instead of a tile)? The Specialist looks richer, but it costs Food and Happiness, and each one adds less than the last.

Growth VII

Food fills a growth bar. Each bar needs more Food than the last, and since 1.2.0 that cost rises along a gentle quadratic curve. Each time the bar fills, the settlement grows and you make one choice:

  • Improve a tile. You pick a tile and the right improvement (farm, mine and so on) appears on it. This can also claim unowned tiles within 3 hexes.
  • Add a Specialist (cities only). The citizen works inside an urban tile instead.

Specialists changed in 1.4.0. A Specialist has no base yield. It adds 100% of its tile's adjacency (extra yield from what sits next door) yields. Each one costs upkeep in both Food and Happiness.

Specialist placed onAntiquityExplorationModern
Current-age or unique ageless buildings (Food and Happiness each)246
Warehouse or outdated buildings (Food and Happiness each)111

Happiness after the 1.4.1 rework VII 1.4.1

Each settlement has its own local Happiness. Buildings and other sources add it; Specialists, war weariness (unhappiness that grows the longer you fight) and being over the settlement limit (how many settlements you can hold before penalties) take it away. Each point below zero cuts that settlement's yields by 5%, down to a limit of −80%. Unhappiness does not cut Food 1.4.0. Patch 1.4.1 sorted local Happiness into five stages. The positive stages scale up in later ages.

StageLocal Happiness
Ecstatic40 or more per age
Joyous20 to 39 per age
Happy0 to 19 per age
Unhappy−1 to −19
Angry−20 or lower

The stages do nothing alone; government abilities and other effects check them. Classical Republic, for example, gives +1 Culture in settlements that are Happy or better.

Excess Happiness across your empire builds toward a Celebration (a Civ VII bonus period from surplus Happiness). Each Celebration needs more than the last, lasts 6 turns at Standard speed, and gives +1 social policy slot plus one of two bonuses your government offers.

Each age you adopt one of three governments (in Antiquity: Classical Republic, Despotism or Oligarchy). Each now has a passive effect, two Celebration options and two Traditions only it can unlock.

Sources: Patch 1.4.1 notes; Patch 1.4.0 notes; Celebration (Civ7); Borders (Civ7); Patch 1.2.0 notes.

A citizen on an improved tile costs no upkeep. A Specialist on a strong Quarter can add more, at a price in Food and Happiness. Your first Specialist takes your best tile; each later one takes a weaker tile and pushes Happiness lower, so each is worth less than the last.

Concept lens: stacking Specialists is diminishing returns at work. Look at the next one, not the average. Full lesson · Marginal analysis

Lab: Watch the growth bar stretch

Predict first: write down whether each growth will need more, the same or less Food than the one before, and whether the jumps between them will grow.

  1. Start a game. Open your capital and note the Food it needs for its next growth and its Food per turn.
  2. Each time it grows, improve a tile, then note the turn and the new Food needed.
  3. Repeat for three growths without adding Specialists.

Measure: the Food needed for each growth, and the turns between growths.

What you should see: each growth needs more Food than the last, and each jump is a little bigger than the one before, the quadratic curve. With steady Food per turn, the gaps between growths stretch slowly.

If your result differs: faster growth usually means a new improved tile or building raised your Food per turn. A smaller Food requirement means a Growth Rate bonus arrived mid-run; some wonders and policies give one, and it cuts the Food needed instead of adding Food.

In the world: many apps and loyalty schemes make each new level cost more than the last. Write down the points needed for three levels of one you use, and check whether the gaps grow.

Check yourself

  1. A city at −4 Happiness makes 20 Science and 10 Food. What does it actually produce, and what should you fix before adding a Specialist?

    Show answer

    Each point below zero cuts yields by 5%, so −4 means −20%: 16 Science. Unhappiness does not cut Food, so 10 Food. Raise Happiness first, because a Specialist costs Happiness too.

  2. In Antiquity your first Specialist adds 6 Science on a full Quarter. The next spot would add 3. Which idea says to judge the second by its own numbers, and what are they?

    Show answer

    Marginal analysis: compare the second Specialist's 3 Science with its own upkeep of 2 Food and 2 Happiness. Diminishing returns explains why it adds less than the first did.

  3. A café's first barista serves 40 customers an hour. A second at the same machine would add 15 more. How should the owner decide?

    Show answer

    Compare the 15 extra customers with the second hire's wage. The average per barista hides that the second one shares the machine, so each new hire adds less than the last.

In the game: keep every city at Happy or better before adding Specialists; each point below zero costs 5% of everything it makes.

In the world: a hire that needs upkeep and support from the rest of the team is worth less than its own output suggests.

Influence, Independent Powers and Diplomacy

A neutral Independent Power (a Civ VII minor faction you can befriend or fight) sits 4 hexes from your best site. Settle there and it turns hostile. Befriend it first, and in 15 turns it can be your city-state (a minor power that cannot win).

Influence VII

Influence (a diplomacy currency) is a yield your whole empire pools and spends on diplomatic actions. These are Treaties (such as Open Borders), Endeavors (deals that raise yields for both sides), Sanctions (penalties on a rival), Espionage, War Support (see Commanders and War) and befriending Independent Powers. Improve Trade Relations raises your trade route limit with a civ, and each use costs 25% more than the last 1.4.0.

Your relationship with each leader is a score. Good relationships make leaders accept and propose deals; poor ones make them refuse, and every refusal hurts the score more.

RelationshipScoreWhat it allows
Helpfulabove +60Alliances
Friendly+20 to +60
Neutral−20 to +20
Unfriendly−60 to −20
Hostilebelow −60Formal war without a War Support penalty

Allies are pulled into each other's wars. Since 1.5.0 you can pay Influence to stay neutral and keep the alliance, renewed every 10 turns on Standard speed. You cannot stay neutral if two of your allies fight each other.

Independent Powers VII

Independent Powers are small factions that appear on turn 2 of each age, roughly Civ VI's barbarians, and the seeds of city-states. Each is hostile or neutral, and each has one of six types (Militaristic, Economic, Cultural, Scientific, Diplomatic, Expansionist) that sets its rewards. Since 1.5.0 they no longer spawn inside your borders.

  • Befriend: start the project for 2 Befriend points per turn. The first player to 60 points becomes its suzerain (the player who controls a city-state) and it becomes a city-state. A neutral power starts at 30 points. A hostile one turns neutral at 30. Add Support spends Influence for +1 point per turn. Each befriend you have running, and each city-state you already lead, raises the cost by 20% 1.4.0.
  • Disperse: beat its central unit and disperse it for 125 yields per age of its type, plus 25 experience for an adjacent Commander (a Civ VII unit that leads an army).
  • Watch out: founding a town within 5 hexes of a neutral power turns it hostile, unless you already started befriending it.

As suzerain you pick one bonus from the city-state's list, such as +1 settlement limit (how many settlements you can hold before penalties) (Hegemon) or a free civic. A city-state works like a town, and declaring war on one means declaring war on its suzerain.

Sources: Influence (Civ7); Diplomacy (Civ7); Independent Power (Civ7); City-State (Civ7); 1.5.0 notes.

You need a steady flow of Influence more than a stockpile, most of all when a war is coming.

Concept lens: relationships that improve with each accepted deal and sour with each refusal are a repeated game, where cooperation pays because you will meet again. Full lesson · Endeavors are a small case of complex interdependence.

Lab: How fast can you win a city-state?

Predict first: write down how many turns it will take to become suzerain of a neutral Independent Power, first with no Add Support, then with one.

  1. Early in Antiquity (Civ VII's first age), find a neutral Independent Power with your Scout (a fast unit for exploring). Do not found a town within 5 hexes of it.
  2. Start the Befriend action on it. Note the turn and its Befriend points.
  3. Note its points each turn. Once they pass 45, spend Influence on Add Support once.
  4. Note the turn it becomes your city-state.

Measure: Befriend points per turn before and after Add Support, and turns from start to suzerain.

What you should see: it starts at 30 points and gains 2 a turn, then 3 after Add Support, so it takes about 13 turns instead of 15. A small yield reward arrives at 45 points.

If your result differs: it started at 0 because the power was hostile, or turned hostile when you settled within 5 hexes. You lost it because a rival reached 60 first; since 1.4.1 the game shows which leaders are befriending a power and whether they are ahead of you.

In the world: trust with a new neighbor or client grows the same way, through small steady contacts, and extra effort speeds it up. Count how many small favors pass before a new neighbor starts offering favors back.

Check yourself

  1. You start befriending a neutral Independent Power and add no support. How many turns until you become suzerain, if no rival gets there first? How many with Add Support from the start?

    Show answer

    A neutral power starts at 30 of the 60 points. At 2 points a turn, the last 30 take 15 turns. With Add Support from the start it gains 3 a turn: 10 turns.

  2. A neighbor has accepted three of your Endeavors. You are tempted to reject their next proposal to save Influence. What does repeated-game thinking say?

    Show answer

    Each refusal hurts the relationship more, and you will deal with this leader all game. Keeping cooperation going is usually worth more than the one deal you would save by refusing.

  3. Two neighboring countries trade grain every year. One bans exports for a season to force a better price. What risk does the repeated-game view point to?

    Show answer

    The partner remembers. A one-time gain can cost future deals and trust, just as each rejected proposal in Civ VII lowers the relationship score and makes later deals harder.

In the game: start a Befriend on the nearest neutral Independent Power in your first 20 turns, before you settle near it.

In the world: goodwill is built in small, repeated deals, and one public refusal can cost more than the deal was worth.

Commanders and War

In Antiquity you declare a surprise war on a Neutral neighbor. Before a shot is fired, your units lose 3 Combat Strength and your capital 9 Happiness. Your own people's support is a combat stat.

Declaring war VII

  • Formal war needs a Hostile relationship and carries no War Support penalty.
  • Surprise war can target anyone, but costs War Support: −5 against a Helpful leader, −4 Friendly, −3 Neutral, −2 Unfriendly.
  • Joint war, joining an ally's war, costs no War Support.

The moment war starts, Endeavors between you are cancelled, trade stops, city-states (minor powers that cannot win) join their suzerain (the player who controls a city-state), and allies must choose a side. Neither side can offer peace for 10 turns.

War Support and war weariness

War Support measures how far your people back the war. You can raise it by spending Influence (a diplomacy currency), and other leaders can back or oppose your war once each. If you have less War Support than your enemy, the gap becomes war weariness (unhappiness that grows the longer you fight). Each point costs your military units 1 Combat Strength, up to −20, and costs Happiness in every settlement:

Happiness lost per point, by who founded the settlementAntiquityExplorationModern
You357
A power you are not at war with579
A power you are at war with7911

Commanders VII

A Commander (a Civ VII unit that leads an army) is a support unit that cannot attack. It can Assemble up to 4 military units and 1 civilian into its army (more with promotions), move them as one stack, and Deploy them on arrival. Its commands boost units within its command radius. Commanders are the only units that gain experience and promotions, and they keep both from age to age. A defeated Commander returns after 20 turns. Fleet Commanders arrive in Exploration.

Fortified districts and capture VII

Building walls or another fortification turns a district (a specialized city tile that holds buildings) into a fortified district. Its strength equals the strongest unit you have trained, it strikes back at melee attackers, and it repairs itself. To capture a settlement you must take and hold the city center and every fortified district. Then you choose:

  • Keep it. It is in Unrest for 10 turns (no buying buildings, no growth, no focus change), and you lose 4 Influence per turn per age for the rest of the age. "Per age" means the number grows in later ages.
  • Raze it. It loses 1 district per age per turn 1.4.0. You lose 4 Influence per turn per age while it burns, then 8 per turn per age for the rest of the age.

Sources: War (Civ7); Commander (Civ7); Combat (Civ7); Settlement (Civ7); Patch 1.4.0 notes.

A war plan

  1. Before Declare formally on a Hostile leader or join an ally's war, to avoid a War Support penalty. Bank Influence.
  2. Build Group melee, ranged and siege units under one leveled Commander. Check your settlement limit (how many settlements you can hold before penalties) has room.
  3. Strike Take every fortified district of a target near your border fast; a slow siege bleeds Happiness.
  4. Stop Make peace once you hold what you came for.

Concept lens: your neighbor's walls look like a threat to you, and yours to them: the security dilemma. A Commander's stack of 4 beating units sent in one at a time is Lanchester's square law. War weariness (unhappiness that grows the longer you fight) pushes you toward Clausewitz's culminating point, past which advancing weakens you, so pick your stop turn before you march.

Lab: Price a surprise war

Predict first: write down the War Support gap and your capital's Happiness change after a surprise war on a Neutral leader.

  1. In Antiquity (Civ VII's first age), find a leader whose relationship with you is Neutral. Save the game.
  2. Note your capital's Happiness per turn.
  3. Declare a surprise war on that leader. Open that leader's diplomacy menu and note the War Support for this war. If the screen shows only the balance between you, note the balance.
  4. Next turn, note your capital's Happiness again. Then reload the save.

Measure: the War Support numbers or balance the screen shows, and the change in your capital's Happiness per turn.

What you should see: you start 3 War Support behind. That gap is war weariness: your capital, which you founded, loses 3 × 3 = 9 Happiness, and your units fight at −3 Combat Strength.

If your result differs: other leaders may back or oppose the war once each, and either side can buy War Support with Influence, so the gap moves. A Militaristic attribute point gives +1 War Support on all wars. Some leaders change it too: Harriet Tubman gets +5 on wars declared against her.

In the world: when a government starts a military operation, follow the polls on it for a few weeks. Ask whether the way it began, by surprise or after a long public dispute, shaped support. That is the real version of War Support.

Check yourself

  1. In Antiquity you declare a surprise war on a Friendly leader. Their War Support stays at 0 and nothing else changes yours. What do your units and your own settlements lose?

    Show answer

    A surprise war on a Friendly leader costs 4 War Support. That 4-point gap is war weariness: units lose 4 Combat Strength, and each settlement you founded loses 4 × 3 = 12 Happiness.

  2. Your army has taken two cities, but war weariness grows every turn. Which idea from Clausewitz warns you, and what should you do?

    Show answer

    The culminating point: past it, advancing makes you weaker. Stop at the turn you picked in advance and make peace once you hold what you came for. Peace opens after 10 turns of war.

  3. A government starts a war its voters did not back. Six months in, protests grow. Why does Civ VII treat a problem like this as military as well as political?

    Show answer

    Public support is part of military power. In the game low War Support cuts Combat Strength and Happiness. In the world, fading support shrinks recruits, budgets and patience, so the war gets harder to fight.

In the game: prefer formal or joint wars. A surprise war on a Helpful leader starts you 5 War Support behind.

In the world: public support is part of military power, and a war that loses it weakens every week.

Triumphs, Dedications and Winning

Halfway through Exploration you lead the world in Tourism (cultural pull that wins a Culture victory) and are nowhere near winning, because a Cultural victory needs six times second place. Late in Modern, 1.25 times will do.

Triumphs and Dedications VII 1.4.0

Triumphs (Civ VII goals within an age) replaced the old Legacy Paths in patch 1.4.0. They are goals in six themes: Militaristic, Cultural, Scientific, Economic, Diplomatic and Expansionist. The Default set has about 30 per age.

  • Minor Triumphs are easier and give an instant bonus for the rest of the age, such as a promotion for all Commanders (Civ VII units that lead armies) or extra War Support. In the Modern Age (Civ VII's third age) they also add victory points.
  • Major Triumphs take more work and unlock Dedications (bonuses picked when a new age or era begins) for the next age. They exist in Antiquity (Civ VII's first age) and Exploration only. Examples: Wonders (one-of-a-kind buildings only one civ can own) of the Ancient World (7 wonders) and Four Corners of the World (8 settlements) in Antiquity, and in Exploration Treasure Fleet (800 GDP from Treasure Convoys, the units that carry treasure from your settlements across the ocean back to your home continent).

Dedications are bonuses you pick at each age transition, 3 at a time. Five are always available. More unlock from Major Triumphs, leader level-ups and handling the last crisis well.

Victories VII 1.4.0

Each victory type has a score, and you can build it from Antiquity on.

VictoryScoreHow you win
MilitaryDominionLead second place by the multiple below, then hold it for a 5-turn countdown
CulturalTourism
EconomicGDP (Treasure Convoys add to it)
ScienceInnovationReach 100 points, then keep an active Launch Pad for 5 turns. No comparison with rivals.
DominationnoneEliminate every rival
ScoreoverallIf no one has won when Modern reaches 100%, the highest score wins
Window (Age Progress)TierLead over 2nd place
Exploration 50–80%Transcendent6×
Exploration 80% to Modern 20%Crushing4×
Modern 20–40%Decisive3×
Modern 40–60%Momentous2×
Modern 60–80%Substantive1.5×
Modern 80–100%Narrow1.25×

If a rival pushes you below the threshold during the countdown, it pauses. It does not reset, and it resumes when you regain the lead. A score win is decided first by countdown turns, then by placing across victories, then by Major Triumphs completed. These rules are unchanged in 1.5.0.

Sources: Patch 1.4.0 notes; Triumph (Civ7); Victory (Civ7); List of triumphs in Civ7.

The table rewards a huge early lead and accepts a small late one. Pick one or two scores by the end of Antiquity, feed them through Exploration, and be ready to close when the multiple comes within reach.

Concept lens: choosing a victory score and lining up every age behind it is grand strategy: ends, ways and means. Full lesson · Cultural victory scored in Tourism is soft power counted in points. Soft power

Lab: Can you move one victory score in 15 turns?

Predict first: write down which score, Dominion, Tourism or GDP, you can raise most against second place in 15 turns.

  1. Open the Victory screen from the Trophy / Age Progress button. For Dominion, Tourism and GDP, write your score and second place's. If the screen shows only your standing, write your rank and how far the bar shows you ahead or behind.
  2. Pick one score to feed. Dominion comes from the settlements you control. Tourism comes from Wonders, Natural Wonders and Celebrations (Civ VII bonus periods from surplus Happiness), among other things. GDP comes each turn from slotted Resources and Gold buildings in your cities.
  3. Play 15 turns feeding that score, for example by founding a settlement or finishing a Wonder.
  4. Read the Victory screen again.

Measure: for each score, your total divided by second place's, before and after.

What you should see: the ratio for the score you fed should have climbed more than the others. Compare it with the 6× bar that applies from 50% of Exploration to see how far off an early win is.

If your result differs: second place fed the same score, so your ratio held still. Some ways to earn points open only in later ages, so a score can be slow to move in Antiquity. A captured Wonder built by another leader is worth less Tourism.

In the world: market share works the same way, since a firm's sales mean little without its rivals'. Next time a news story gives two rival brands' sales, divide one by the other and ask what one big launch would do to the ratio.

Check yourself

  1. At 50% of the Modern Age your Tourism is 300 and second place has 140. Does a Cultural countdown start, and what would pause it?

    Show answer

    Between 40% and 60% of Modern you need 2× second place, which is 280. At 300 the 5-turn countdown starts. It pauses if the rival climbs above 150.

  2. Halfway through Antiquity you lead in Dominion, are third in Tourism and last in GDP. How does grand strategy (ends, ways, means) shape your next two ages?

    Show answer

    Pick the end you can reach, here a Military victory, then line up ways (Triumphs, wars, new settlements) and means (Production, Influence) behind it in every age, instead of feeding every score a little.

  3. Two shops compete for the same customers. Shop A sells 1,000 a week and Shop B 900. Why should A's owner worry more than the totals suggest?

    Show answer

    In a contest judged against a rival, the lead matters. A is only about 11% ahead, so a small swing flips it, the same logic as Civ VII's 1.25× bar late in Modern.

In the game: at the start of each age, open the Victory screen, find your closest race with second place, and pick Triumphs that feed it.

In the world: in a contest judged against a rival, the size of your lead matters more than your raw total.

Your First Civ VII Game

Turn 3 of your first game, and you have spent twenty minutes comparing first builds. Firaxis wrote a script for this moment. Follow it, accept good enough, and save your attention for learning.

Firaxis's recommended setup VII

SettingChoice
Leader and civHatshepsut, Egypt
Starting ageAntiquity
DifficultyScribe (the lowest)
SpeedStandard
MapContinents Plus, Small
TutorialsOn

Firaxis's opening, from its launch-era beginner's guide:

  1. T1 Found your capital where your Founder stands.
  2. First build A Scout (a fast unit for exploring), before the Medjay (Egypt's unique unit). Explore and use the Scout's Search action.
  3. First tech Sailing.
  4. First building A Granary on a tile next to a Navigable River if you can. Queue a Medjay after it.
  5. By T10 The civic Arrival of Hapi, which boosts Food on Navigable Rivers.
  6. Government Oligarchy.

Then spread your towns along Navigable Rivers. Patches have changed many numbers since launch, so treat this as a learning script.

Source: Civilization VII beginner's guide (2K).

Beginner picks after patch 1.4

Community consensus (CivFanatics thread, May–June 2026, after the 1.4.0 update):

  • Easy to start with: Hatshepsut with Egypt; Tecumseh, with whom Independent Powers (Civ VII minor factions you can befriend or fight) start friendly; Harriet Tubman with the Maya; Trung Trac; Augustus; Himiko; Benjamin Franklin.
  • Leave for later: Alexander, Napoleon, Ibn Battuta and Confucius as leaders; Carthage and Aksum as civs. Posters cite constant wars (Napoleon), Specialist (a citizen working a building instead of a tile) upkeep that can cripple a careless player (Confucius) and a low settlement limit (how many settlements you can hold before penalties) (Aksum).

Source: CivFanatics: most and least beginner-friendly civs and leaders.

Do

  • Start a Befriend on a nearby neutral Independent Power early.
  • Stay at or under your settlement limit of 3 until you see what Happiness can bear.

Avoid

  • Founding towns within 5 hexes of neutral Independent Powers.
  • Surprise wars on Friendly or Helpful leaders.

Difficulty and mods

VII Since 1.2.2 you can set a custom AI difficulty, and mods install through the Steam Workshop. Play your first game unmodded so what you learn matches this book.

Concept lens: a first game has more choices than you can weigh. Following a fixed opening and accepting "good enough" is satisficing, Herbert Simon's answer to bounded rationality. Full lesson · To improve after it, see deliberate practice.

Lab: Does the script beat improvising?

Predict first: write down which run will have more Science plus Culture per turn at turn 30, the scripted one or the free one, and which will take more real minutes.

  1. Start the recommended setup and save on turn 1.
  2. Play to turn 30 following the opening above exactly. Note your Science and Culture per turn, your number of settlements, and the real minutes you spent.
  3. Reload the turn 1 save and play to turn 30 choosing freely. Note the same numbers.

Measure: Science and Culture per turn, settlements and real minutes, for each run.

What you should see: satisficing predicts in-game numbers that are close and a scripted run that is quicker to play. A good-enough plan gets most of the result for much less effort.

If your result differs: a clearly better free run may mean your Civ VI patterns still apply, or the map favored another opening. A lagging scripted run may reflect patches since launch, which changed costs, so a timing such as Arrival of Hapi by turn 10 may no longer hold.

In the world: cook a new dish once from a recipe and once freely, and time both. Then ask whether each result was good enough, and what the recipe freed you to notice.

Check yourself

  1. You play Hatshepsut on Scribe and own 2 settlements in Antiquity (Civ VII's first age). A neutral Independent Power sits 4 hexes from your best town site. What do you do first?

    Show answer

    Start a Befriend on it before you settle. A town founded within 5 hexes turns a neutral power hostile unless befriending has begun. The third town still fits your limit of 3.

  2. On turn 3 you spend twenty minutes comparing every possible first build. What would Herbert Simon suggest, and why?

    Show answer

    Satisfice: take a good-enough option, such as the script's Scout first, and move on. Your attention is limited, which is bounded rationality, so spend it on learning what the game teaches.

  3. A new university student faces dozens of course, club and schedule choices in the first week. How does satisficing help?

    Show answer

    Set a good-enough bar, take the first option that clears it, and revisit later. With limited time and information, a trusted default frees attention for the few choices that matter most.

In the game: play Hatshepsut on Scribe with tutorials on, and finish one full Antiquity age before you change anything.

In the world: when everything is new, a script you trust frees your attention to learn what matters.

What's Coming

About half of Civ VII's Steam reviewers recommend it, and Civ VI still draws about three times as many players. A fourth age and a space expansion are due in 2027.

Announced for 2027 VII

  • Earthrise, the first paid expansion, announced 5 September 2026 for 2027. It adds orbital missions in the Modern Age (Civ VII's third age), a space agency in the Atomic Age, units and technologies that change terrain, disasters that worsen as temperatures rise, and tools to reverse the warming.
  • Arc of Tomorrow, a free update, also due in 2027. It adds a fourth age, the Atomic Age, covering roughly the 1950s to the 2050s.
  • Patch 1.4.1's notes also mention a planned harder "Collapse Mode" age transition, with no details or date.

Once Arc of Tomorrow is out, check the patch notes before relying on any number in this part.

Where the game stands in October 2026 VII VI

Civ VII launched on 11 February 2025 to a Metacritic score of 79. On Steam about 51% of its roughly 30,000 English-language user reviews are positive (Mixed). In September 2026 it averaged about 10,500 players at once on Steam. Civ VI, released in 2016, averaged about 28,000–33,000, with monthly peaks of 48,000–54,000. Many players have stayed with Civ VI, one reason this book teaches both.

Sources: CivFanatics: Earthrise announcement; Patch 1.4.1 notes; Steam store page; SteamCharts Civ VII; SteamCharts Civ VI; Wikipedia: Civilization VII.

Concept lens: players who stay with the game they already know, and its mods and friends, show how an installed base can hold a market. Path dependence

In the game: read the patch notes before each new game; Civ VII's rules are still moving.

In the world: a better product also has to beat the cost of switching to it.

Part 8

Multiplayer: Game Theory for Real

Against people, every promise can be a bluff and every border a standoff. This part covers how online Civ works and the four ideas from game theory that explain it.

Playing Against People

Your friend promises peace in chat, then parks three Knights on your border. People bluff, remember and plan around you, and game theory was built to explain them.

Turn modes VI

The host picks one of two turn modes in the lobby.

  • Simultaneous. All human players move at the same time. AI players still move one after another.
  • Dynamic. Players at peace move at the same time. Players at war with each other take turns in order, so each sees the other's moves before replying.

Under Simultaneous turns, whoever clicks first acts first. The classic trick is the double move. You move units at the very end of one turn, then attack at the very start of the next, before the defender can react. The community's competitive league, the Civilization Players League (CPL), plays free-for-alls on Simultaneous at Online speed, which ends at turn 250.

Sources: CivFanatics: Simultaneous/Dynamic turn mode; GameSkinny: Civ 6 multiplayer details; Better Multiplayer Turn Modes (Steam Workshop); CivFanatics: double turning; CPL in-game rules

Lobbies, mods and hotseat VI

  • Internet and LAN games happen live in a lobby. Play By Cloud is turn-by-turn: you get a notice when it is your turn, play it, and submit. Hotseat means several players share one PC and take turns at it.
  • Better Balanced Game (BBG) is the community balance mod for competitive play. Versions change often. It is on the Steam Workshop and CivFanatics.
  • Etiquette, community consensus (CPL rules and forums). Do not quit without the host's agreement. Do not throw the game by letting yourself be conquered. CPL free-for-alls also ban trading Gold and strategic resources and ban military alliances. A relobby, restarting from a save after a crash, needs at least two-thirds of the other players to agree, and everyone must repeat their important moves.

Sources: CPL in-game rules; Steam: Play By Cloud; Co-Optimus: Civ VI (PC hotseat)

Civ VII multiplayer VII

Civ VII has crossplay across PC and consoles if you link a 2K account. Since patch 1.2.1, every age allows the same number of human players: 4 on a Tiny map, 6 on Small and 8 on Standard. A multiplayer game holds at most 8 players, AI included. The original Switch supports only Tiny and Small maps, so crossplay games with its players use those sizes. A match can cover a single age instead of all three. Hotseat arrived in patch 1.4.1 on 23 June 2026.

Sources: Civilization VII: Crossplay and Progression; launch press release; Patch 1.2.1 notes; Patch 1.2.2 notes; CivFanatics: update 1.4.1

Game theory

Nash equilibrium

Until 1967 Swedes drove on the left, though every neighbor drove on the right. In a 1955 referendum, 83% voted to keep it that way. Parliament switched anyway. On 3 September 1967 every vehicle on the road had to stop at 4:50 a.m., cross to the right, stop again and wait for 5:00. No driver could switch alone: among cars on the left, the left is the only safe side. The whole country moved at once.

Nash equilibrium: a set of choices, one per player, where no player can do better by changing only their own choice.

Open in the PDF, page 154

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Two neighbors who both keep a defensive army and never attackA stable standoff between rivals
A lobby's "standard" settings and banned civsConventions everyone follows because others do

Where the map breaks

  • An equilibrium can be bad for everyone, yet no one gains by leaving it alone.
  • Many games have several equilibria. Left and right both work for driving, and the theory cannot say which one a country lands on.

Read next

  • Accessible: the INFORMS biographical profile of John F. Nash Jr.
  • Primary: Nash (1950), two pages long.

John F. Nash Jr., "Equilibrium Points in n-Person Games," Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences 36(1) (1950); "Non-Cooperative Games," Annals of Mathematics 54(2) (1951).

Game theory

Prisoner's dilemma

In 1950 two mathematicians at the RAND Corporation, Merrill Flood and Melvin Dresher, built a simple game. Whatever the other player did, each did better by betraying, yet two betrayers ended up worse off than two cooperators. They asked two colleagues, the economist Armen Alchian and the mathematician John Williams, to play it 100 times. The pair often cooperated. Albert Tucker, a Princeton mathematician, later told the game as a story about two prisoners, and the name stuck.

Prisoner's dilemma: a game where each player does better by betraying, whatever the other does, yet both end up worse off than if both had cooperated.

Open in the PDF, page 154

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Two players who both build armies instead of CampusesAn arms race
Everyone burning Coal for Production while sea levels rise (Gathering Storm)Carbon emissions and climate change

Where the map breaks

  • The textbook dilemma is played once. A Civ game lasts hundreds of turns, and repetition makes cooperation pay (repeated games).
  • Civ has more than two players and only one winner, so helping a partner can still lose you the game.

Read next

  • Accessible: William Poundstone, Prisoner's Dilemma (1992).
  • Reference: the Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy entry "Prisoner's Dilemma."

Merrill Flood and Melvin Dresher, experiments at RAND (1950); the prisoners story and name credited to Albert W. Tucker (1950).

Game theory

Credible commitment and cheap talk

In February 1519 Hernán Cortés sailed from Cuba for Mexico with about 11 ships and 500 men, defying a governor who had cancelled his expedition. That summer, on the coast at Veracruz, he scuttled his ships. Now no one could sail home, and retreat was off the table. Thomas Schelling, an economist who studied bargaining, later put the logic in a line: "to burn bridges behind one may suffice to undo an opponent".

Credible commitment and cheap talk: a promise or threat works only if others believe it. Talk that costs nothing is cheap talk; a commitment is credible when backing out would cost you.

Open in the PDF, page 155

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
"I won't attack you" in chatCheap talk
Units parked on a border you said you would defendA tripwire force that makes a threat believable

Where the map breaks

  • In Civ, the rules enforce some deals for you. Between real states, nobody enforces anything.
  • Your lobby reputation lasts a few games. A state's reputation can last generations.

Read next

  • Primary and readable: Schelling, The Strategy of Conflict (1960), chapters 2 and 3.

Thomas C. Schelling, The Strategy of Conflict (1960).

Decision science

Expected value and risk

In 1713 the mathematician Nicolas Bernoulli posed a puzzle in a letter. Toss a coin until it lands heads; the prize doubles with every tails. Each possible run adds the same amount to the average prize, and the runs never end, so the expected value is infinite. Yet in 1738 his cousin Daniel wrote that "any fairly reasonable man would sell his chance, with great pleasure, for twenty ducats" (in translation). His answer was that each extra ducat matters less the more you have.

Expected value and risk: expected value is each outcome's value times its probability, added up: the average payoff over many tries. Risk is how widely outcomes spread around it.

Open in the PDF, page 156

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
An attack with a 60% chance to take a capitalA risky investment
Keeping Gold to buy units in an emergencyInsurance: paying a little to avoid ruin

Where the map breaks

  • Civ shows you predicted combat results. Real probabilities are guesses.
  • In a game you can play again. Real ruin is final, so people rightly avoid bets with a good average and a small chance of disaster.

Read next

  • Accessible: Peter Bernstein, Against the Gods (1996), chapter 6.
  • Primary: Bernoulli (1738), in the 1954 English translation.

Daniel Bernoulli, "Specimen theoriae novae de mensura sortis" (1738); John von Neumann and Oskar Morgenstern, Theory of Games and Economic Behavior (1944).

Lab: Can you trust a promise in chat?

Predict first: before turn 1, each player writes down whose Science per turn will be higher at turn 50, and whether each turn-50 promise will be kept by turn 100.

  1. Play a two-player Civ VI game with a friend on a small map, Simultaneous turns.
  2. Before turn 1, each of you writes down privately: "army" or "economy" for the first 50 turns. Reveal at turn 50.
  3. At turn 50, each makes one promise in chat. Write down whether you believe it, and why.

Measure: both players' Science per turn at turn 50, and whether each promise was kept by turn 100.

What you should see: if both chose "army," both have less Science than if both had chosen "economy." Promises backed by units on the border are kept more often than promises in chat.

If your result differs: one "army" player may have taken a city early, which turns a military lead into Science. A promise broken despite units on the border may mean the other player gambled that a double move under Simultaneous turns would land before you could react.

In the world: watch two rival petrol stations on one road for a week. Do their prices move together, and does either one break the pattern alone? That is an equilibrium you can see from the pavement.

Do

  • End every turn as if your opponent moves first next turn.
  • Ask for the lobby's mods, speed and rules before you join.
  • Back promises with something that costs you to break.

Avoid

  • Trusting chat promises from strangers.
  • Quitting a game you are losing without the host's agreement.
  • Fighting a neighbor while the leader runs away with the game.

Check yourself

  1. Under Simultaneous turns you end a turn with a damaged unit two tiles from an enemy army. What can happen before you can react, and how do you prevent it?

    Show answer

    A double move: the enemy moves at the very end of this turn, then attacks at the very start of the next. Pull the unit back or cover it before ending the turn.

  2. Two neighbors both keep Walls and Archers and never attack, though both would rather skip the cost. Neither gains by dropping defenses alone. Which concept is this, and is it good for both?

    Show answer

    A Nash equilibrium: each is making its best reply to the other. It need not be good for both. Both would rather save the cost, but neither can cut alone safely.

  3. A shop owner tells a rival she will never cut her prices. The rival does not believe her. What would make the promise credible?

    Show answer

    Something that makes breaking it costly, such as a signed agreement with a penalty. Words that cost nothing are cheap talk; a commitment becomes credible when backing out would cost the promiser.

In the game: under Simultaneous turns, never leave anything weak in reach at end of turn.

In the world: a promise is worth what it would cost the promiser to break it.

Part 9

Reference

Look things up here: every term in the book, the mistakes almost every new player makes, one-page checklists, the best places to keep learning, and a short history of the earlier games.

Glossary

Game terms first, then the real-world concepts. Concept entries link to their full lesson.

Game terms

1UPT
"One unit per tile." From Civ V onward, only one military unit can stand on a hex. VI Corps and Armies merge two or three units into one, and a tile can also hold one civilian, one support and one religious unit. VII Commanders pack units together instead.
4X
Explore, expand, exploit, exterminate: the four activities of empire-building strategy games.
Adjacency bonus
VI Extra yield a district gets from what sits next to it. VII Buildings get adjacency bonuses from nearby features instead. Districts
Age
VII One of the three chapters of a game (Antiquity, Exploration, Modern), each ending in a transition. VI "Golden" and "Dark" Ages are something different: era-by-era bonuses earned through Era Score. Ages and Crises
Agenda
VI What an AI leader likes and dislikes. Each has one visible agenda and a hidden one, and Gathering Storm adds a second hidden one later. Diplomatic visibility reveals the hidden ones.
Amenities
VI City-level contentment from luxuries, entertainment and some policies. Too few slows growth and cuts yields. Housing and Amenities
Apex civ
VII A civilization in the age it was designed for.
Barbarians
VI Hostile units that spawn from camps in unclaimed land. Barbarians
Beeline
Researching straight toward one tech or civic, skipping everything not on the path.
Boost (Eureka / Inspiration)
VI Completing an in-game task cuts the cost of a related tech (Eureka) or civic (Inspiration) by 40%. Civ VII has no Eurekas or Inspirations. Tech and Civics
Builder
VI Civilian unit with limited charges that builds tile improvements and harvests resources. Civ VII has no Builders. (Civ IV and V called this unit a Worker.)
Casus belli
A justification for war. VI Declaring war with one reduces the diplomatic penalty. Why Wars Start
Celebration
VII A period of bonuses triggered by enough excess Happiness. Growth and Happiness
Chop
VI Removing Woods with a Builder for a one-time lump of Production. Removing Rainforest (Food and Production) or Marsh (Food) also pays out. Builders and Chopping
City Center
The tile the city is founded on. VI It always yields at least 2 Food and 1 Production.
City-state
A minor power that can't win but gives bonuses to whoever has influence over it. VI You compete with Envoys. VII Independent Powers become city-states when you befriend them. City-States
Civic
VI VII A node on the culture tree. Civics unlock policies, some buildings and, in Civ VI, governments.
Commander
VII A leader unit that carries several units as one army and gains experience across ages. Commanders and War
Corps / Army
VI Two or three identical units merged into one stronger unit. Timing Pushes
Crisis
VII A forced challenge late in an age (plague, invasion, revolt) that makes you slot crisis policies.
Dedication
VII A bonus you pick at an age transition. Five are always on offer, and Triumphs unlock more 1.4.0. VI The same word names the bonuses you choose when entering a Golden, Dark or Heroic Age.
Diplomatic Favor
VI A currency earned from your government, alliances and city-states, spent in the World Congress. Diplomacy
District
VI A specialized tile of a city (Campus, Commercial Hub, Holy Site and so on) that holds its buildings.
Envoy
VI A token you send to a city-state. More envoys bring more bonuses, and the most envoys (at least 3) makes you suzerain.
Era Score
VI Points from historic moments. It decides whether your next era is Dark, Normal, Golden or Heroic. Loyalty and Ages
Fog of war
The parts of the map you can't currently see. In strategy theory, the uncertainty of war in general. Clausewitz
Fortify
VI A land unit that stays put digs in: +3 defense after one turn, +6 after two or more. Combat
Golden Age
VI An era with bonus loyalty and a strong dedication, earned with high Era Score.
Goody hut
VI A tribal village. The first unit to enter gets a random reward, which is why early scouting pays.
Government Plaza
VI A one-per-empire district that unlocks special buildings and boosts neighboring districts.
Governor
VI A named official you assign to a city for its bonuses. Governors
Great Person
A unique unit (scientist, general, merchant and others) earned with Great Person points, used once for a big effect. Wonders and Great People
Grievances
VI A running record of who has wronged whom. It shapes AI attitudes and how much Favor war costs you.
Housing
VI The soft population cap of a city. Growth slows as you approach it.
Improvement
A farm, mine, pasture or similar built on a tile to raise its yield.
Independent Power
VII A minor faction on the map. It can be hostile, neutral, or befriended into a city-state.
Influence
VII The diplomacy currency: treaties, endeavors, sanctions, and befriending Independent Powers. Influence
Lens
A map overlay that shows one kind of information, such as settler spots, appeal, loyalty or religion.
Loyalty
VI How firmly a city belongs to you. At zero it rebels and becomes a free city.
Luxury resource
VI A resource that gives amenities to your cities when improved. Extra copies are good trade goods.
Mastery
VII An optional second research of a tech or civic for an extra bonus.
Overbuilding
VII Replacing an outdated building with a current-age one on the same tile. Quarters
Pantheon
VI An early faith bonus you pick before founding a religion.
Policy card
VI A swappable bonus slotted into your government. Swaps are free when a civic finishes. VII Civ VII calls them social policies.
Quarter
VII An urban tile holding two current-age buildings. Two unique buildings make a Unique Quarter.
Rush
An early attack built before the opponent can defend. It also means paying gold or faith to finish something instantly.
Settlement limit
VII The number of towns and cities you can hold before each extra one costs Happiness in every settlement. Settlements
Specialist
A citizen assigned to a building or district instead of a tile.
Suzerain
The player who controls a city-state and gets its bonus. VI The civ with at least 3 envoys and more than anyone else; it can pay Gold to levy the city-state's army. VII The first player to finish befriending an Independent Power.
Tall / wide
Few large cities versus many cities. VI Community consensus favors wide. VII The settlement limit is the brake.
Timing push
An attack timed to a short window when your units outclass the enemy's. Timing Pushes
Tourism
Cultural pressure on other civs, used for the Culture victory. Culture Victory
Town
VII A settlement that turns production into Gold and picks a focus. It can be upgraded to a city.
Trade route
A Trader unit's repeating route between two cities, giving yields to one or both ends. Trade Routes
Triumph
VII A goal within an age. Minor Triumphs give instant rewards, and Major ones unlock Dedications. It replaced Legacy Paths in patch 1.4.0.
Unique unit / building
A civ's special version of a unit or building.
War weariness
Growing unhappiness or penalties the longer and harder you fight.
World Congress
VI A recurring global vote on resolutions, paid for with Diplomatic Favor.
Yield
What a tile, building or citizen produces: Food, Production, Gold, Science, Culture, Faith.
Zone of control
VI The tiles next to a military unit, where enemy units must stop moving.

Real-world concepts

Each concept has a full lesson with its origin, how the game models it, where the model breaks, and an experiment. This list is the short version.

"War made the state"
The idea that modern states were built by the need to fight wars. Rulers who wanted armies had to tax, borrow and count their people, and the offices they built to do that became the state.
Agglomeration economies and clusters
Firms and workers are more productive near each other, thanks to shared suppliers, a shared pool of skilled labor and ideas that spread by contact. Concentration then feeds itself.
All models are wrong
Every model simplifies, so every model is false somewhere. The useful question is whether it is wrong in ways that matter for what you are using it to do.
Anarchy and the security dilemma
With no world government to call for help (anarchy), each state arms to feel safe. Its neighbors cannot tell defense from preparation to attack, so they arm too. Everyone ends up less safe than before, though nobody wanted a fight.
Arrow's impossibility theorem
With three or more options, no ranked voting rule can always turn individual rankings into a group ranking while meeting four mild conditions together: any rankings allowed, unanimity respected, irrelevant options ignored, and no dictator. Every rule breaks at least one.
Balancing vs bandwagoning
States usually ally against the state they fear most (balancing); joining it (bandwagoning) is rarer. Fear depends on power, nearness, offensive capability and apparent intentions: a balance of threat. Weak, isolated states bandwagon more often.
Bounded rationality and satisficing
Real decision-makers have limited information, time and computing power. Instead of finding the best option, they set an aspiration level and take the first option that clears it. Simon called this "satisficing". How well it works depends on the environment as much as the mind.
Collapse of complex societies
Societies solve problems by adding complexity: more officials, soldiers and rules. Each new layer costs more and returns less. When returns fall low enough, a society can suddenly shed much of its complexity. That shedding is collapse.
Comparative advantage
Two parties both gain from trade when each makes what it gives up least to make, even if one of them is better at everything.
Complex interdependence
When states are tied by many channels (governments, firms, people) and force between them is too costly, power comes from uneven dependence. Whoever would suffer less if the ties were cut holds the leverage.
Compounding
A quantity compounds when each period's growth is added to the base that grows the next period. Steady percentage growth doubles the quantity about every 70 divided by the rate in percent. Small early gains therefore matter far more than equal gains later.
Creative destruction
Capitalism grows as new products, methods and firms replace old ones. The new makes the old worthless, so growth and destruction are one process.
Credible commitment and cheap talk
A promise or threat only changes what others do if they believe you will carry it out. Words that cost nothing to say are cheap talk. Commitments become credible when backing out would cost you.
Deliberate practice
Effortful practice designed to fix one specific weakness, just beyond your current ability, with fast feedback, usually guided by a teacher. Ericsson and colleagues argued that accumulated hours of it, more than talent, explain expert performance.
Deterrence and compellence
Deterrence is a threat that stops someone from starting something ("attack me and you will pay"). Compellence is a threat that makes someone stop or do something ("leave, or else"). Both work only if the other side believes you can and will carry it out.
Diffusion of innovations
New ideas spread along an S-shaped curve: slowly among a few early adopters, fast through the majority, then slowly among the last holdouts. Speed depends on how much better the idea is and how easily people can see and try it.
Diminishing marginal returns
Hold the other inputs fixed and keep adding one input: each extra unit eventually adds less output than the one before. Total output still rises, just more slowly. The law says nothing about growing all inputs together.
Dirty hands
Sometimes the right political act is also a wrong one, so the leader who does it is guilty as well as right. Walzer says a good leader feels that guilt, and we trust them because they do.
Division of labor
Splitting work into specialized tasks raises output per worker through skill, saved switching time and better tools. Smith's limit: specialization pays only when the market is big enough to take the output.
Ethic of conviction
Doing what you believe is right and leaving the results to God or fate. Weber set it against the ethic of responsibility and said politics needs both.
Ethic of responsibility
Judging an act by its foreseeable results and answering for them. Weber's other ethic of politics, which he said must work together with the ethic of conviction.
Exit, voice and loyalty
When a firm, party or state declines, members can exit (leave) or use voice (complain, organize, vote). Loyalty delays exit and gives voice time to work. Easy exit can drain voice, so a harder exit sometimes helps an organization recover.
Exogenous and endogenous growth
In Solow's model, investing more lifts income per head to a higher level, but the faster growth fades, because capital hits diminishing returns. Lasting growth per head comes from technology, which the model takes as given. Romer made technology the result of deliberate research.
Expected value and risk
The average payoff of a choice if you could make it many times: each outcome's value times its probability, added up. Risk is how widely the outcomes spread around that average.
Experiential learning cycle
Kolb describes learning as a repeating four-stage cycle: have a concrete experience, reflect on it, form an abstract idea that explains it, then test the idea by acting. Each pass changes what you notice in the next experience.
Externalities
An externality is a cost or benefit of an action that falls on people who had no part in it and is not priced. Pigou proposed taxing harmful ones and subsidizing helpful ones so that private and social costs line up.
Fog of war, friction and the culminating point
Clausewitz argued that most information in war is uncertain or wrong (now called the fog of war), that countless small problems make simple plans hard to carry out (friction), and that every attack weakens as it advances until it reaches a culminating point, after which pressing on invites defeat.
General-purpose technologies
A general-purpose technology is used across many sectors, keeps improving and makes other inventions possible: steam, electricity, computing. Its payoff comes late, because users must reorganize around it first.
Geography and development
Diamond argues that differences in domesticable plants and animals, the orientation of continents and exposure to disease, not differences between peoples, explain why some regions built states and conquered others. Critics answer that institutions and colonial history explain more than environment does.
Grand strategy (ends, ways, means)
A state's plan for using all its resources, military and civilian, to reach its most important goals over the long run. Ends are the goals, means are the resources, and ways are how you turn one into the other.
Hard question
The box in many lessons where winning and doing right pull apart: a real case, the strongest honest argument on each side, and a question back to you. The book never gives the answer.
Heartland and rimland
Mackinder argued that whoever holds inner Eurasia (the Heartland), safe from sea power, can dominate the world. Spykman replied that the prize is the coastal belt around it (the Rimland), where most people and industry are.
Imagined communities
A nation is an imagined political community. Its members will never meet most of the others, yet feel they are one people. Anderson credits print capitalism, mass-printed books and newspapers in a shared language, with spreading that feeling.
Imperial overstretch
When a great power's military and territorial commitments grow faster than the economy that pays for them, the power declines.
Inclusive and extractive institutions
Inclusive institutions spread political power and protect property for most people, which rewards investment and lets new firms replace old ones. Extractive institutions concentrate power and income in an elite that blocks change. The authors argue extractive growth does not last.
Information asymmetry
When one side of a deal knows more about quality than the other, the uninformed side offers an average price, good sellers withdraw and average quality falls, sometimes until the market fails. Inspection, warranties and signals exist to close the gap.
Institutions: the rules of the game
Institutions are the rules people make to shape how they deal with each other: laws and constitutions, unwritten norms, and how both are enforced. Organizations are the players; institutions are the rules. Rules change slowly because those who gain from them defend them.
Lanchester's laws
Simple equations for how fast two forces wear each other down. When every soldier can fire at any enemy (the square law), fighting strength grows with the square of numbers, so a bigger force wins by far more than its head start.
Learning by doing
Productivity rises with experience: the more of something an economy has made, the cheaper the next unit. Arrow treated this as a source of growth, because what one producer learns spills over to others.
Marginal analysis
Decide by comparing the extra benefit of one more unit with its extra cost, not by looking at averages or totals. Keep adding while the extra benefit beats the extra cost.
Monopoly of legitimate violence
Weber defines the state by its means, as a community that successfully claims the monopoly of the legitimate use of physical force within a territory. That legitimacy can rest on tradition, on a leader's charisma or on legal rules.
Nash equilibrium
A set of choices, one per player, where no player can do better by changing only their own choice. Everyone is making their best reply to everyone else.
Network spread of religion
Religions grow mainly through existing social ties such as family, friends and trading partners; mass preaching matters less. A steady growth rate of about 40% per decade, compounded for centuries, is enough to turn a small sect into a majority.
Offensive realism
Mearsheimer argued that because states can never be sure of others' intentions, great powers try to gain as much power as they can, ideally becoming the dominant power in their region. In this view, only the strongest are safe.
Opportunity cost
The cost of a choice is the value of the best alternative you gave up. It applies even when no money changes hands: every tile, build and research pick has one.
Path dependence
When returns increase with adoption, small early events can lock a system onto one path, and switching later is costly even if another path would have been better. Where you end up depends on the order things happened, not only on the final payoffs.
Prisoner's dilemma
A game where each player does better by betraying, whatever the other does, yet both end up worse off than if both had cooperated.
Realpolitik
Politics follows the distribution of real power the way nature follows physics. Whoever holds actual social force rules, and ideals succeed only when force backs them. Rochau, a liberal, meant it as advice about means; he kept his liberal goals.
Repeated games and the evolution of cooperation
When the same players face a prisoner's dilemma again and again, with no known last round, cooperation can last if they reciprocate: start nice, punish a betrayal once, forgive, stay predictable. The future must matter enough to outweigh a one-time cheat.
Revolution in military affairs
A revolution in military affairs (RMA) is a jump in how war is fought, when new technology combines with new organization and doctrine so that older forces become obsolete almost at once.
Sea power
Mahan argued that national greatness rests on sea power: a strong navy, merchant shipping, overseas bases and control of key sea lanes. A navy should seek command of the sea by defeating the enemy's main battle fleet.
Soft power
The ability to get others to want what you want, through the appeal of your culture, your political values and your policies, instead of through force or payment.
Stationary vs roving bandit
A roving bandit takes everything and moves on, so nobody invests. A bandit who settles and rules gains from his subjects' prosperity, so he taxes instead of looting and supplies order. Both sides end up better off, and secure, long rule matters.
Stocks, flows and feedback loops
A stock is an amount that has built up, like water in a bath. A flow changes it each period, like the tap and the drain. Feedback loops link the stock back to its flows: reinforcing loops amplify, balancing loops stabilize.
Structural realism and the balance of power
Waltz argued that state behavior is shaped less by leaders or ideas than by the structure of the system: no world government, and power spread unevenly. To survive, states watch relative power and tend to balance against whoever grows too strong.
Sun Tzu, The Art of War
An ancient Chinese manual arguing that the best victories come with the least fighting: know yourself and the enemy, shape the situation before battle, attack weakness rather than strength, and use terrain and deception.
The is–ought gap
No description of how the world is, on its own, proves what anyone ought to do. To get from facts to duties you need a value, and you should say which one.
The Malthusian trap
When population can grow faster than food, any gain in living standards is eaten by more mouths, until people are back at subsistence. Extra output raises the number of people, not income per head, until technology or institutions break the link.
The Melian Dialogue
Thucydides' account of the talks between Athens and the island of Melos in 416 BC. The Athenians argue that "the strong do what they can and the weak suffer what they must" (Crawley's translation). The Melians refused; Athens put the grown men to death and enslaved the women and children.
The OODA loop
