Observe, Orient, Decide, Act: the cycle every fighter or organization runs. Boyd argued that the side which cycles faster and orients more accurately can act inside the opponent's loop, so the opponent keeps reacting to a situation that has already changed.
The principal–agent problem
When one party (the principal) relies on another (the agent) to act for it, and the agent has its own interests and better information, the principal bears agency costs: monitoring, incentives, and the losses from decisions that still go the agent's way.
The sunk-cost fallacy
A sunk cost is spending you cannot get back. A good decision ignores it and compares only future costs and benefits. The fallacy is letting past spending push you to continue, which Arkes and Blumer showed in experiments.
Time value and discounting
A benefit now is worth more than the same benefit later, because the early one can be invested and because people are impatient. Discounting converts future benefits into present value so that choices across time can be compared.
Tragedy of the commons, and governing the commons
Hardin: each user of an open resource gets the full gain of taking more but shares the cost, so the resource is overused. Ostrom: communities often prevent this with clear boundaries, local rules, monitoring, graduated sanctions and cheap ways to settle disputes.
Transfer of learning
Transfer is using something learned in one setting in a different one. "Low-road" transfer happens automatically when the new setting looks like the old one. "High-road" transfer needs you to deliberately abstract a principle and look for where else it applies.

The Mistakes Almost Everyone Makes

Most veterans made these once. Beginner threads on CivFanatics, Steam and gaming sites keep listing the same ones, and each links to the lesson that fixes it.

Civ VI VI

  1. Too few cities. Nothing in Civ VI directly punishes you for more cities. Reach turn 100 with three and you are playing uphill. Districts
  2. Building every wonder. Most wonders don't help your victory, and losing a race to the AI can waste much of the Production (the yield that builds units and buildings) you put in. Wonders
  3. Placing districts without a plan. A district can't be moved, so a poor spot costs you adjacency (extra yield from what sits next door) all game. Districts
  4. Founding a religion you don't use. It costs Faith (the yield spent on religion) and Holy Sites (faith districts) you could spend elsewhere. Religion
  5. Ignoring barbarians. A scout that reaches you brings a raiding party. Barbarians
  6. Sitting on Gold, Favor (a currency spent in the World Congress) or spare luxuries. A stockpile does nothing until you spend it. Buy tiles, Builders (units that build farms, mines and the like) and Settlers (the units that found cities), and sell spare luxuries. Time value
  7. Forgetting policy cards (swappable bonuses slotted into your government). Swap them every time a civic completes. Governments
  8. Researching before the boost. Finish a tech before its Eureka (a 40% research discount for a related task) triggers and the discount is wasted. Tech and Civics
  9. Leaving units exposed. A ranged unit in the open is a gift to the enemy. Combat
  10. Wars without an end goal. Long wars pile up Grievances (a running record of who wronged whom) and war weariness (unhappiness that grows the longer you fight). Why Wars Start
  11. Switching victory type late. Pick one by the middle of the game and commit. Choosing Your Victory
  12. Starving cities of Food. Production feels good, but population drives everything else. Tiles and Growth
  13. No defense at all. The AI targets weak neighbors. Security dilemma
  14. Ignoring Housing (a city's soft population cap) and Amenities (city contentment from luxuries and entertainment) until growth stops. Housing and Amenities
  15. Settling on bad land to grab a resource. Fresh water and Food come first. Where to Settle

Sources: CivFanatics "Rookie Mistakes"; CivFanatics "General advice for those starting out"; Steam discussion on going wide.

Civ VII VII

  1. Settling far past the settlement limit (how many settlements you can hold before penalties). Each settlement over the limit costs Happiness, with no cap since 1.4.0. Settlements
  2. Upgrading every town to a city too early. Towns earn Gold, and once focused they send Food to your cities and add focus bonuses.
  3. Hoarding Influence. Spend it on Independent Powers (Civ VII minor factions you can befriend or fight), treaties and endeavors. Influence
  4. Ignoring Independent Powers. Hostile ones raid you. Befriended ones become your city-states (minor powers that cannot win).
  5. Forgetting to overbuild. Old buildings lose their bonuses at an age change. Quarters
  6. Wasting Commanders. Their experience carries across ages. Commanders
  7. Trying to conquer everyone early. War Support and war weariness bite hard.
  8. Ignoring the age meter. The crisis arrives at 70% age progress. Ages and Crises
  9. Following pre-May-2026 guides. Patch 1.4.0 replaced Legacy Paths with Triumphs and changed how victories work. Winning

Sources: TheGamer; GameRant; 2K patch notes 1.4.0.

One-Page Checklists

Print these or keep them on a second screen. Each line is a habit. Break it when you have a reason.

First game of Civ VI? Use only the every-turn list. The every-era list uses ideas from Parts 2 to 5, such as Era Score (points for historic moments that set your next era) and trade routes, so add it once you have read those. Until then, "my victory" just means your turn-60 target.

Civ VI: every turn VI

  • Did a civic finish? Re-check policy cards (swappable bonuses slotted into your government).
  • Can a Eureka (a 40% research discount for a related task) or Inspiration (a 40% civic discount for a related task) be triggered before I research this?
  • Is any city building something that no longer serves my victory?
  • Is any city close to its housing cap or short of amenities?
  • Is gold piling up? Buy a tile, a Builder (a unit that builds farms, mines and the like) or a Settler (the unit that founds a city), or patronize a Great Person (a famous figure you recruit for one big effect).
  • Are any units standing in the open next to an enemy?

Civ VI: every era VI

  • Is my victory type still the right one? Pivot rules
  • Is my Era Score (points for historic moments that set your next era) on track for a Golden Age (a bonus era earned with high Era Score), or at least not Dark?
  • Who is the runaway AI, and who is weak next to me?
  • Do I need walls, or a few modern units to stop looking like prey?
  • Are my trade routes full?

Civ VII: every turn VII

  • Is Influence piling up? Befriend an Independent Power (a Civ VII minor faction you can befriend or fight) or sign a treaty.
  • Is any town ready to pick a focus (7 population)?
  • Am I over the settlement limit (how many settlements you can hold before penalties), and is the happiness cost worth it?
  • Is a growth event better spent on a new tile or a specialist?

Civ VII: every age VII

  • Before 70% age progress: get ready for the crisis policies.
  • Which Triumphs can I still finish this age?
  • Which civ do I want next, or do I keep this one?
  • Which buildings will I overbuild first next age?

Reading ladder

The original source behind every concept in this book, grouped by discipline. Each concept lesson also suggests an easier first read.

Decision science
Bounded rationality and satisficingHerbert A. Simon, "A Behavioral Model of Rational Choice," Quarterly Journal of Economics 69(1): 99–118 (1955); "satisficing" named in "Rational Choice and the Structure of the Environment," Psychological Review 63(2) (1956)

Agglomeration economies and clustersAlfred Marshall, Principles of Economics (1890); Paul Krugman, "Increasing Returns and Economic Geography," Journal of Political Economy 99(3) (1991); Michael E. Porter, The Competitive Advantage of Nations (1990).

Strategy

Systems / economics

Technology
Diffusion of innovationsEverett M. Rogers, Diffusion of Innovations (1962). Classic evidence: Zvi Griliches, "Hybrid Corn: An Exploration in the Economics of Technological Change," Econometrica 25(4) (1957).
General-purpose technologiesTimothy F. Bresnahan and Manuel Trajtenberg, "General Purpose Technologies: 'Engines of Growth'?," Journal of Econometrics 65(1) (1995).

How the Series Got Here: Civ IV and V

You will meet advice written for older games. This page tells you which rules it assumes.

GameReleasedMap and armiesEconomyWhat carried forward
IV Civilization IV2005Square tiles; unlimited unit stacking ("stacks of doom")Cottages that grow into towns vs a Great-Person "specialist economy"; civicsReligion, Great People
V Civilization V2010Hexes; one unit per tile (1UPT)Empire-wide Happiness that punishes many cities; social policy trees ("Tradition four-city" was a classic plan)Hexes, 1UPT, city-states
VI Civilization VI2016Hexes, 1UPT with corps and armiesDistricts and adjacency, Eurekas, local amenities and housing, policy cardsDistricts became VII's quarters
VII Civilization VII2025Hexes, Commanders carry unitsAges, changing civs, towns vs cities, settlement limitToday's game

When a guide talks about cottages, a specialist economy or stacks of doom, it is a Civ IV guide. When it talks about global happiness or Tradition vs Liberty, it is Civ V.

Sources: Civilization IV, Civilization V, Civilization VI, Civilization VII (Wikipedia).

Part 10

Where Civ Is Wrong, and Where It Is Silent

This part turns the book's method on the game itself. The first lesson lists what Civ gets wrong about history. The second asks the question the game never asks: what winning has to do with doing right.

The Six Assumptions Built Into Every Civ Game

Every Civ game runs on six rules that no real society ever lived under. Play long enough and they start to feel like facts about the world.

What the game assumes VI VII

Turn 250. Every civ on the map is climbing the same tech tree, one rung at a time. Your Settlers (the units that found cities) have walked onto land that belonged to nobody. The camps you cleared had no names. One leader has run your people since the first turn. None of this ever happened to any real people, and after a few hundred turns none of it feels strange.

Every model leaves things out, and Civ must, or it would be unplayable. The trouble starts when you forget what was left out and the gaps feel like facts about the world. Scholars who study games as history name six big ones.

  1. History is one ladder. The tech tree runs in one direction, from Pottery to rockets, and every civ climbs the same rungs. Kacper Pobłocki argued in 2002 that this makes the West's path look like "the only logical development" of humankind. Real technologies were lost, rediscovered, skipped and combined in orders no tree allows. Roman concrete was forgotten for centuries. Mesoamerican societies built cities and calendars without draft animals or iron tools.
  2. The land is empty until you settle it. Your Settler walks onto open ground and founds a city. Postcolonial critics call this the game's version of terra nullius, the legal fiction that land with no recognized state belonged to no one. Real frontiers were almost always someone's home.
  3. "Barbarians" (hostile raiders from camps) have no history. In Civ VI they appear from camps, raid, and exist to be cleared. Dom Ford's 2016 study of Civ V points out that the game frames anyone outside the state system as an obstacle. Civ VII's Independent Powers (Civ VII minor factions you can befriend or fight) are a small step away from this, since you can befriend them and they can become city-states (minor powers that cannot win).
  4. States are the only real actors, and one leader runs each for 6,000 years. Merchants, priests, peasants, rebels and migrants appear only as yields and pop counts. That is the "great man" view of history, which most historians abandoned long ago.
  5. Peoples are fixed brands. In Civ VI a civ keeps the same bonuses all game, as if "being Roman" were a permanent trait. Civ VII's age changes admit that societies transform, though it still packages each phase as a ready-made civ.
  6. Everything can be measured and controlled. Every outcome in Civ comes from numbers you can see and change. Alexander Galloway argued in 2006 that the game's real lesson is that winning means learning the algorithm. Real rulers faced uncertainty they could not price, and most of what happened in their countries happened without them.

Sources: Pobłocki, "Becoming-state: the bio-cultural imperialism of Sid Meier's Civilization," Focaal 39 (2002): 163–177 (record); Ford, "eXplore, eXpand, eXploit, eXterminate," Game Studies 16(2), 2016; Galloway, Gaming: Essays on Algorithmic Culture, ch. 4 "Allegories of Control," University of Minnesota Press, 2006 (overview).

Statistics · Philosophy of science

All models are wrong

In 1859 Urbain Le Verrier, the French mathematician who had predicted Neptune by calculation in 1846, turned the same method on Mercury. Its orbit slowly turns around the Sun, and it turned slightly faster than Newton's laws allowed. The gap is tiny, 43 arcseconds a century. Le Verrier trusted the model and blamed something unseen: a planet, or a belt of asteroids, inside Mercury's orbit. That year a country doctor, Edmond Lescarbault, wrote that he had watched a small black dot cross the Sun on 26 March. Le Verrier announced the planet and called it Vulcan. Astronomers hunted it for decades and never confirmed it. In 1915 Einstein's general relativity explained the gap with no new planet at all. Newton's model had been wrong all along. It is still right enough to plan most spaceflight.

All models are wrong: every model simplifies, so every model is false somewhere. The useful question is whether it is wrong in ways that matter for what you are using it to do.

Open in the PDF, page 172

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Each citizen eats exactly 2 Food VIA simplification that is useful for play and wrong about how farming societies actually fed themselves
Three Ages with clean transitions VIIA way of dividing history into periods that no historian would defend, which is what makes the game teachable
Every "where the map breaks" list in this bookBox's question applied to each concept, one at a time

Where the map breaks

  • Box was writing about statistical models fitted to data. A game is a designed model with no fitting step, so it can be wrong on purpose, and often is, for fun.
  • "All models are wrong" gets used to dismiss any model someone dislikes. Box meant the reverse: keep the model and find where it fails. Le Verrier made the opposite mistake. He kept Newton and invented a planet.
  • The scholars quoted in this part go further than Box. They argue that Civ's errors all point one way, toward Western technological progress as the only path.

Read next

  • Accessible: "All models are wrong" (Wikipedia), a short history of the phrase; "Vulcan (hypothetical planet)" (Wikipedia).
  • Primary: Box, "Science and Statistics" (1976), nine readable pages.

George E. P. Box, "Science and Statistics," Journal of the American Statistical Association 71(356): 791–799 (1976). The longer saying, "all models are wrong, but some are useful," comes from Box's later writing (first in 1978–79, then Box & Draper, 1987).

What the game does get right

Civ still teaches, because its systems push on you the way the world pushes on states. Food limits growth. Rivals arm when you arm. Trade ties your interests to other people's. The game models pressures well and people badly.

Adam Chapman's Digital Games as History (Routledge, 2016) argues that games are a form of history in their own right, one you do rather than read. Kurt Squire studied students learning world history through Civ III (2004). Writing later with Shree Durga, he described two styles of play: "expert gaming," which treats Civ as a system to master, and "historical gaming," which treats it as a way to replay and question the past. Each style teaches something the other misses. This book asks you to do both.

Diane Carr, in "The Trouble with Civilization" (2007), warns against the opposite mistake, treating a game's rules as what players take from them. Players argue with games, mock them, mod them and tell their own stories through them. Your own critical play is part of what the game means.

Lab: Play one game as a historian

Predict first: guess which list will be longer at the end: things your society did that no button allows, or things the game made easier than history did.

  1. Start any game. Before turn 1, write down one real society you know a little about, such as Song China, the Inca, or the Mali Empire.
  2. Every 50 turns, stop and list anything that society actually did that no button in the game lets you do. Examples: move your whole population somewhere else, split into two states, lose a war and survive as a culture, let merchants override the ruler.
  3. At the same stops, keep a second list of things the game made easier than history did, such as instant borders, perfect information about your own cities, or loyal armies.

Measure: how many items are on each list by the end.

What you should see: the "impossible" list grows faster than the "easier" list. Most of the gaps are about people, uncertainty and collapse, which are the three hardest things to turn into rules.

If your result differs: a long "easier" list may mean you know your society mainly through its rulers, which is the game's own lens. In Civ VII the "impossible" list may grow more slowly, since age changes let a society transform and Independent Powers can be befriended into city-states.

In the world: pick one model you use often, such as a budget spreadsheet, a fitness app or a news map. List three things it cannot show you, and one decision you made that it would have got wrong.

Do

  • Use the game to feel pressures: scarcity, rivalry, distance, compounding.
  • Ask of every rule, "who does this leave out?"
  • Read one real history of a civ you play, and compare.

Avoid

  • Saying "history proves" something because it works in Civ.
  • Treating a civ's game bonus as a fact about real people.
  • Assuming progress is automatic or goes one way.

Read next

  • Accessible: Kanishk Tharoor, "What Sid Meier's video game empire got right and wrong about civilization" (Kill Screen, via Longreads, 2016).
  • Scholarly: Dom Ford, "eXplore, eXpand, eXploit, eXterminate" (Game Studies, 2016), free online.
  • Book length: Adam Chapman, Digital Games as History (Routledge, 2016); Souvik Mukherjee, Videogames and Postcolonialism: Empire Plays Back (Palgrave Macmillan, 2017).

Check yourself

  1. Your size-5 Civ VI city needs exactly 10 Food a turn to feed its citizens. What simplification is that, and what would Box ask about it?

    Show answer

    Each citizen eats exactly 2 Food, which is wrong about how farming societies fed themselves. Box asks whether it is wrong in ways that matter here. For learning that food limits growth, it is useful.

  2. A classmate says Civ shows that technology always moves forward in one order. Which of the six assumptions is that, and what real cases break it?

    Show answer

    "History is one ladder." Roman concrete was forgotten for centuries, and Mesoamerican societies built cities and calendars without draft animals or iron tools, orders no tech tree allows.

  3. A city plans a new road with a traffic model that counts only cars. What would Box's question tell the planners to do?

    Show answer

    Ask whether the model is wrong in ways that matter here. Leaving out buses, cyclists and walkers may matter a lot for a road, so they should check it against what it cannot see.

In the game: win by its rules, and keep a running list of what those rules leave out.

In the world: any model, from a spreadsheet to an ideology, is a game with hidden rules. Ask what it cannot see before you trust what it shows.

Winning and Doing Right

Civ lets you wipe a captured city off the map, and the only bill is what other leaders think of you. Real rulers have faced that choice with people inside. What wins, and what is right?

The Raze button VI VII

Turn 190. Your army takes a rival's city of size 8. It sits on the river you wanted, its districts (specialized city tiles that hold buildings) are in the wrong places, and it will take many turns to turn loyal. A panel asks whether to keep it or raze it, and you must decide this turn.

In Civ VI most captured cities can be razed. Razing destroys the city completely, with every district and improvement it owned. The land clears, so you can found a city on a better spot or let your own borders grow into the gap. The bill is diplomatic. Grievances (a running record of who wronged whom) are the game's running score of who has wronged whom. Capturing a city earns up to a base of 50 with its owner, and razing it up to 150, three times as much. An original capital can never be razed. A Free City, one that has rebelled from its owner, can be razed with no Grievances at all.

Civ VII offers Keep or Raze, and both cost Influence (a diplomacy currency), the diplomacy currency, for the rest of the Age. Once a settlement is razed, it costs twice as much each turn as one you keep.

Nobody in the city appears on screen. Population is a number, and it goes to zero. That is a model, and like every model in this book it leaves something out. What it leaves out is the subject of this lesson.

Sources: City (Civ6); Grievances (Civ6); Settlement (Civ7).

What the strong said

In 416 BC an Athenian army landed on Melos, a small island that had tried to stay out of Athens's war with Sparta. Thucydides, the Athenian who wrote that war's history, set down the talks as a dialogue. The envoys refused to argue about justice. In Richard Crawley's translation, they said that "right, as the world goes, is only in question between equals in power, while the strong do what they can and the weak suffer what they must." The Melians trusted the gods and Sparta, and said no. That winter the city fell. The Athenians "put to death all the grown men whom they took, and sold the women and children for slaves," and sent 500 colonists to take the land. The hard question in How the AI Thinks and Cheats puts Melos to you directly. Here it stands for one view at its plainest: power decides.

Nineteen centuries later a sacked official turned that view into advice. Niccolò Machiavelli served the republic of Florence from 1498 to 1512. When the Medici family returned to power, he was dismissed, accused of plotting against them, imprisoned and tortured. Released, he retired to a small property near Florence and in 1513 wrote The Prince, which he dedicated to a Medici. Chapter 15 refuses to describe the ideal ruler. In W. K. Marriott's translation, "how one lives is so far distant from how one ought to live, that he who neglects what is done for what ought to be done, sooner effects his ruin than his preservation." A prince who wants to last must know "how to do wrong, and to make use of it or not according to necessity."

That is the realist case, written by a man who had watched his republic fall. Rulers who ignore how power works tend to lose, and their people pay. The three ideas below come from thinkers who took that case seriously and still refused to stop there.

Political philosophy

Ethic of conviction versus ethic of responsibility

Munich, 28 January 1919. Germany had lost the war, and in November Bavaria had overthrown its king. Max Weber, a sociologist of 54, had just lost his own run for parliament. A students' union asked him to lecture on politics as a vocation. Politics works through force, he told them, and whoever takes it up "contracts with diabolical powers." Then he drew a line. You can act by conviction, as in "The Christian does rightly and leaves the results with the Lord." Or you can act by responsibility, and "give an account of the foreseeable results of one's action." He would not choose for them. Only the two together, he said, make a person with "the calling for politics." (Gerth and Mills's translation.)

Ethic of conviction versus ethic of responsibility: the first does what is right and leaves the results to God or fate; the second judges an act by its foreseeable results and answers for them. Weber said politics needs both.

Open in the PDF, page 176

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
Keeping a promise to an ally when breaking it would win the warConviction: the rule holds, whatever it costs
Breaking it, counting the Grievances, and winningResponsibility: judged by results, and answerable for them

Where the map breaks

  • Weber's example of pure conviction was a revolutionary, the "convinced syndicalist" who blames the world when good intentions turn out badly. Today "responsibility" gets used as a licence for whatever works. He ruled that out: it is not "unprincipled opportunism," and "Naturally nobody says that."
  • Civ scores every consequence for you. Weber's politician must foresee results nobody will ever tally, and own them anyway.
  • Gerth and Mills called the first ethic the "ethic of ultimate ends." This book uses the other common rendering, "conviction."

Read next

  • Accessible: Weber, "Politics as a Vocation," in From Max Weber: Essays in Sociology, trans. H. H. Gerth and C. Wright Mills (1946), pp. 77–128, free at the Internet Archive.
  • Primary: Weber, Politik als Beruf (Munich: Duncker & Humblot, 1919).

Max Weber, "Politics as a Vocation" (Politik als Beruf), lecture in Munich, 28 January 1919; published 1919.

Political philosophy

Dirty hands

In 1973 the political philosopher Michael Walzer built an argument on a line from a play. In Sartre's Dirty Hands (1948), the Communist leader Hoederer says, in Lionel Abel's translation: "I have dirty hands right up to the elbows. I've plunged them in filth and blood. Do you think you can govern innocently?" Walzer's answer was no. His test case is a decent candidate who can win only by making a deal with a dishonest ward boss over school-building contracts. We want him to make it, Walzer wrote, precisely because he has scruples: "We know he is doing right when he makes the deal because he knows he is doing wrong." If he is a good man, Walzer added, he will feel guilty afterwards. The right act can leave a stain.

Dirty hands: sometimes the right political act is also a wrong one, so the leader who does it is guilty as well as right. Walzer says a good leader feels that guilt, and we trust them because they do.

Open in the PDF, page 177

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
A surprise war costs 150 Grievances, which decay with each turn of peace VIThe game's stain washes out on a timer. Walzer's never fully does
Reloading the save after a choice you regretNo reload: "The innocent man, afterwards, is no longer innocent"

Where the map breaks

  • Walzer's harder case is a leader who orders a captured rebel tortured to find bombs set to go off within twenty-four hours. A thought experiment fixes every fact in advance. Real officials rarely know that the prisoner knows, and "guilty but right" can be stretched to excuse almost anything.
  • Civ never asks you to feel anything. Walzer's test is a feeling: the leader who makes the deal without a flicker of guilt has failed it.
  • Walzer calls Weber's version of this figure "a tragic hero" who suffers alone, and doubts that the problem can be settled inside one person's conscience.

Read next

  • Accessible: Jean-Paul Sartre, Dirty Hands (Les Mains sales, 1948), the play Walzer starts from.
  • Primary: Walzer, "Political Action: The Problem of Dirty Hands," Philosophy & Public Affairs 2, no. 2 (1973), on JSTOR.

Michael Walzer, "Political Action: The Problem of Dirty Hands," Philosophy & Public Affairs 2, no. 2 (Winter 1973): 160–180.

Philosophy

The is–ought gap

David Hume was in his twenties when he wrote A Treatise of Human Nature, most of it written in La Flèche, a small town in France. It came out in 1739 and 1740, and by his own account it "fell dead-born from the press." Early in its third book, on morals, he added an observation that "may, perhaps, be found of some importance." Every system of morality he had read, he wrote, reasons for a while about God or human affairs, and then "of a sudden" every proposition is "connected with an ought, or an ought not." The change is "imperceptible; but is, however, of the last consequence," and nobody explains it. A writer can describe the world perfectly and slip in a duty without noticing.

The is–ought gap: no description of how the world is, on its own, proves what anyone ought to do. To get from facts to duties you need a value, and you should say which one.

Open in the PDF, page 178

How the game maps it

In the gameIn the world
"The AI attacks the weak." A fact about the rulesAn is
"The AI attacks the weak, so you should too." A rule with a value hidden inside itAn ought, smuggled in

Where the map breaks

  • Hume asked that the step be "observed and explained," and said that how an ought could be deduced from an is "seems altogether inconceivable." Readers still argue whether he meant it can never be done or only that it must be shown.
  • In Civ the gap closes by design. The rules fix the goal, so "you ought to settle on rivers" is simply true for anyone who wants to win. In the world, nobody wrote the victory conditions.
  • The gap cuts both ways. Realists who read Thucydides as "the strong win, so be strong" skip the step. So do idealists who read the Marshall Plan as "kindness pays, so be kind."

Read next

  • Accessible: Hume, A Treatise of Human Nature, Book 3, Part 1, Section 1, last paragraph, free at davidhume.org; his short autobiography, "My Own Life" (1776).
  • Primary: the same passage, cited as T 3.1.1.27.

David Hume, A Treatise of Human Nature (1739–40), Book 3, Part 1, Section 1.

Where winning and doing right meet

The realist case has a gap of its own: it describes one round. Most of what states do to each other they will do again next year, with the same neighbors watching. Around 1980 Robert Axelrod ran a tournament of computer programs playing the prisoner's dilemma. The simplest entry, Tit for Tat, won two tournaments in a row: cooperate first, then copy whatever the other side did last time. When the game repeats, being decent, firm and easy to read wins. Repeated games tells the story.

Joseph Nye's soft power points the same way: being admired gets you what force cannot buy. So does Thomas Schelling's credible commitment, which makes a record of kept promises an asset. Then there is the Marshall Plan. On 5 June 1947, at Harvard, George Marshall called for a program to rebuild Europe. Fanned by fear of Communist expansion, Congress approved funding that rose to over $12 billion, and the aid also opened markets for American goods. The generous act and the selfish one were the same act. The hard question in Diplomatic Victory asks whether a good deed still counts when it also pays.

Where they split

Now go back to Melos. Athens did what it said the strong do, and it worked. That same winter it voted to send an armada to conquer Sicily. By 413 BC about 200 ships and thousands of men were lost, and many of Athens's subject allies revolted. In 405 BC the Spartan commander Lysander expelled the Athenian colonists from Melos and brought the surviving Melians home, a gesture that doubled as propaganda. In 404 BC Athens surrendered its walls, its fleet and every overseas possession. In the text as it has come down to us, the Melian Dialogue ends one book of Thucydides, and the next opens with Athens deciding to sail for Sicily.

It is tempting to call that justice. A realist will say Athens lost a long war against a coalition for many reasons, and that the Melians' stand on principle got the Melians killed. An idealist will answer that an empire which tells its subjects it rules by force alone has told them when to leave, and after Sicily they left. Both are right about something. At Melos force won and the appeal to justice lost, and the story did not end there.

The hard question: is mercy a weakness or a weapon?

In the game a captured city is a number to keep or erase. In 428 BC Mytilene, on the island of Lesbos, rebelled against Athens and lost. In Crawley's translation, the assembly voted "to put to death not only the prisoners at Athens, but the whole adult male population of Mitylene, and to make slaves of the women and children," and sent a galley with the order. "The morrow brought repentance with it," and the assembly met again. The show of hands was "almost equal." A second galley, its rowers eating at the oars, arrived just after the decree was read. More than a thousand men named as the revolt's leaders were still put to death.

The case for severity: Cleon said the empire was "a despotism," held by strength, not loyalty, and that Mytilene's revolt was "of malice and deliberate." Spare it, and every ally learns that a failed revolt costs little.

The case against: Diodotus claimed no pity. The question, he said, was "how to make the Mitylenians useful to Athens." A city that expects death never surrenders, so harshness buys longer sieges and "a ruined town" that pays no tribute.

Your call: Diodotus saved a city with an argument from profit. If the sums had come out the other way, should Athens have killed them all?

The question is yours

This lesson stops short of a verdict, on purpose. You have seen the realist case at full strength: Athens at Melos, Machiavelli after his fall, and a game that pays you to play by their rules. You have seen the other case too: decency winning Axelrod's tournament, Diodotus saving a city, Western Europe rebuilt with another country's dollars. Weber says a politician needs both ethics. Walzer says even the right choice can stain. Hume adds the warning that matters most. Thucydides shows you what the strong did, and that is an is. Whether you should do the same is an ought. Describing how the world is never, on its own, settles what you should do. You cross that gap with a value, named out loud and defended. The game never asks you to name one. The world does.

Read next

  • Accessible: Thucydides, History of the Peloponnesian War, trans. Richard Crawley, Book 5 (Melos) and Book 3 (Mytilene), free at MIT Classics.
  • Accessible: Machiavelli, The Prince, trans. W. K. Marriott, chapter 15, free at Project Gutenberg.

Check yourself

  1. You capture a size-8 city in Civ VI. Razing it would clear the land for a better city site. What does the game charge you, and what does it never count?

    Show answer

    Grievances with the owner: up to a base of 150, three times the cost of capturing. It never counts the people. Population is a number that goes to zero.

  2. A leader breaks a treaty to prevent a war she believes would kill thousands, and sleeps badly for years. Which two concepts describe her, and how do they differ?

    Show answer

    Weber's ethic of responsibility: she judged the act by its foreseeable results and owns them. Walzer's dirty hands: the act was still a wrong, and her guilt shows she was fit to make it.

  3. A friend says: "History shows that ruthless countries come out on top, so ours should be ruthless." What has the sentence skipped, and what would Hume ask for?

    Show answer

    It jumps from an is (what some countries did) to an ought (what ours should do). Hume asks for the hidden value to be stated and defended: that coming out on top is worth what it costs others.

In the game: before you press Raze, say out loud what you are buying and what the model is not counting.

In the world: facts about power tell you what winning costs. Only a value you can name tells you whether to pay it.

Part 11

Use It on the Real World

Nine real cases read with the concepts from the game, three hard questions where winning and doing right pulled apart, and a grand strategy memo for a real country.

Systems: The 2020–23 chip shortage

In 2021 car makers could not buy enough chips. By 2023 the chip industry was selling less. Reacting to a delay, careful people amplified both.

The case

In spring 2020 car makers expected sales to fall to the levels of the 2007–09 Great Recession. They cut their parts orders, and automotive chip vendors canceled orders with the foundries, the plants that make chips. Car-industry chip sales collapsed in the second quarter of 2020, while demand for computers and electronics more than made up the loss as work and school moved online. Then car sales rebounded, and chip output could not meet car makers' demand. Fires and droughts at large chip plants made it worse. A United States Commerce Department survey found that, for the chips buyers found hardest to get, median inventory had fallen from 40 days' worth in 2019 to under five days in 2021, while median demand ran as much as 17 percent above 2019. AlixPartners estimated 7.7 million vehicles of lost production in 2021. Then sales turned down, and global chip sales fell 8.2 percent in 2023, from a record $574.1 billion to $526.8 billion.

Sources: ECB (2021); NBC News (2021); IHS Markit (2020); US Commerce Department (2022); AlixPartners (2021); SIA (2024).

Concept lens: inventory is a stock, orders are flows, and the time from order to delivery is the delay, which makes this stocks, flows and feedback at world scale. Logistics managers at Procter & Gamble named the amplification the "bullwhip effect" after studying orders for Pampers; Lee, Padmanabhan and Whang (1997) traced it partly to firms that forecast from the swings they see and over-order when they fear rationing. Sterman's 1989 experiments with the beer game, a classroom supply-chain game, showed that people misread delayed feedback, which is bounded rationality.

Where the concepts strain

  • The beer game models one product moving down one chain. Chips are thousands of products, and Commerce named wafer capacity as the main bottleneck, a real shortfall that better forecasting alone would not have fixed.
  • Sterman's evidence comes from a game with steady rules. Real buyers also faced lockdowns in Malaysia, which AlixPartners blamed for its 2021 revision, and fires at chip plants; how much of the swing was behavior is still argued.

Back in the game

In Gathering Storm (the second Civ VI expansion) VI strategic resources such as Iron add to a stockpile every turn, and a new Iron mine adds nothing until a Builder (a unit that builds farms, mines and the like) has been trained and walked to the tile. In Civ VII VII each settlement past the settlement limit (how many settlements you can hold before penalties) costs 5 Happiness in every settlement, a balancing loop that bites once the Settler (the unit that founds a city) is already spent.

Check yourself

  1. Your Iron stockpile looks full, so you queue several Iron units. It runs dry before a Builder can reach the second Iron tile. What went wrong?

    Show answer

    You read the stock and ignored the flow and the delay. Watch the per-turn change, and send the Builder before the stock runs low.

  2. Which effect turns small changes in shoppers' demand into large swings in factory orders, and why?

    Show answer

    The bullwhip effect. Each firm forecasts from the swings it sees and over-orders when it fears rationing, so swings grow upstream.

  3. A product is scarce and buyers are hoarding. What will you read a year or two later?

    Show answer

    Likely a glut. Hoarding lifts orders above real use, new capacity arrives after the delay, and stocks overshoot. That is the bullwhip's prediction; chip sales did fall 8.2 percent in 2023.

In the game: read a stockpile as a flow with a delay; build the inflow before the stock runs low.

In the world: when everyone is hoarding, the shortage may already be turning into next year's glut.

Economics: Concorde and the money already spent

Britain and France kept paying for a plane that would never pay back. Biologists named a fallacy after it. Only part of the story fits.

The case

In February 1967 Britain's aviation minister, John Stonehouse, gave Parliament Concorde's new bill. Development had been estimated at £150 to £170 million when Britain and France signed their treaty on 29 November 1962. It now stood at £450 million plus a £50 million contingency. Quitting was costly too: the treaty included a clause, first asked for by Britain, imposing heavy penalties for cancellation. Concorde first flew on 2 March 1969 and carried paying passengers from 21 January 1976; by then the program had cost over £1.3 billion. Twenty were built, only Air France and British Airways bought them, and the last commercial flight was in 2003. In 1976 the biologists Richard Dawkins and T. R. Carlisle named the "Concorde fallacy," continuing an investment because of what has been spent rather than what it will return. In humans it is called the sunk-cost effect, and in 1999 the psychologists Hal Arkes and Peter Ayton argued no animal clearly shows it, while young children show it less than adults.

Sources: Hansard (1967); Concorde (Wikipedia); Dawkins & Carlisle (1976); Arkes & Ayton (1999); The National Archives blog (2019); FRUS (1965).

Concept lens: the penalty clause made quitting expensive for both partners, a commitment device that kept each promise believable. Each year's Concorde budget was something else left unfunded, an opportunity cost. And "we have come too far to stop" is the sunk-cost fallacy that Arkes and Blumer measured in 1985.

Where the concepts strain

  • Some of the staying was rational. The penalties were a real future cost, which rational choice weighs. The label may fit the rhetoric better than the decision.
  • Arkes and Blumer's evidence came from small laboratory scenarios, and field evidence on the effect's size is mixed. Prestige and jobs were also goals; if they counted as returns, paying on could be a judgment about future value rather than a fallacy.

The hard question: should you keep a promise that has turned into a bad deal?

In Civ VI VI a broken promise adds 100 Grievances, the game's tally of grudges, and play moves on. Britain found its Concorde promise harder to break. Harold Wilson's Labour government took office in October 1964 and decided to quit the treaty. Wilson sent his aviation minister, Roy Jenkins, to Paris to tell the French. By January 1965, Jenkins admitted, strong French pressure had reversed the decision, and Britain kept building. In 1969 the attorney general warned that walking away alone carried a "fifty-fifty chance" of "unacceptable costs" if France sued. Tony Benn reminded the cabinet that 16,000 workers were building Concorde, 8,000 of them in Bristol.

The case for keeping it: Britain had signed, and had itself asked for the penalty clause. A country that drops a treaty when it turns expensive teaches every partner that its signature is cheap, and thousands of jobs rode on the work.

The case against: a previous government signed, and taxpayers kept paying for a plane that only two airlines would buy. A promise to go on wasting money is still waste, and every extra year's spending was money that could not be spent on anything else.

Your call: when a promise made by your predecessors starts costing your own people, does it still bind you?

Back in the game

In Civ VI VI a rival may finish a Wonder, a one-of-a-kind landmark, while your city is twenty turns into it. Check how much of that effort comes back, then choose the next build on future value alone. In Civ VII VII a new age leaves old buildings outdated; their cost is spent either way, so build over them without regret.

Check yourself

  1. You are 25 turns into a Wonder (a one-of-a-kind building only one civ can own) when a rival finishes it. A new Wonder needs 20 more turns, a Campus (the science district) 12. What do you compare?

    Show answer

    Only the future. The 25 turns are gone either way; weigh 20 turns for the Wonder's effect against 12 for the Campus, after checking what Production carries over.

  2. A friend says, "I have paid for three years of this degree, so I must finish." Which concept, and what should they ask instead?

    Show answer

    Sunk cost. Ask whether the remaining year beats its best alternative use. Fees paid are gone either way; a penalty for leaving is a future cost and counts.

  3. A city has spent half a stadium's budget and finishing will now cost double. Which two numbers should decide?

    Show answer

    The remaining cost to finish, including any penalty for stopping, and the value the finished stadium will deliver. Money already spent is the same either way, so it cannot decide.

In the game: when a Wonder race is lost, choose the next build as if the lost turns had never happened.

In the world: judge a project by what it still costs and what it will still return; spent money gets no vote.

Political economy: The two Koreas

One people, one peninsula, and a thirtyfold income gap. The land and language are the same. The rules are not.

The case

The average North Korean, by the count of Daron Acemoglu and James Robinson, can expect to live ten years less than a cousin south of the 38th parallel. Korea is a central case of their Why Nations Fail (2012). North and South share a language, a history and a peninsula, yet the authors note that South Koreans have living standards similar to those of Portugal and Spain, and North Koreans about one tenth of that. The North has published no regular national accounts since the 1960s, so the Bank of Korea estimates them. It puts the North's 2023 income per person at 1.589 million South Korean won, about one thirtieth of the South's, and some North Korea experts call its estimates too optimistic. The authors' explanation is institutions, the rules that decide who holds power and who keeps what they earn. In their account the South's became inclusive, with property protected and markets open, while the North's stayed extractive, run by an elite that takes and blocks change.

Sources: Wikipedia; Foreign Policy (2012); Korea Times (2024); KEIA (2025).

Concept lens: a textbook case for inclusive versus extractive institutions, built on Douglass North's institutions as the rules of the game. The authors argue that Korea holds geography and culture roughly constant, so the rules are the prime suspect. Critics such as Jeffrey Sachs answer that geography, geopolitical threats and debt matter too.

Where the concepts strain

  • Much of South Korea's fast growth came under Major General Park Chung-hee, who took power in a 1961 coup and ruled until his assassination in 1979; direct presidential elections returned only in 1987. Sachs's 2012 review lists 1960s Korea with Prussia, nineteenth-century Japan and 1980s China as deep reforms led by unelected rulers; the authors reply that extractive growth does not last.
  • Francis Fukuyama called the pair too coarse, and critics point to China, which has grown for decades under rules the book calls extractive; the authors answer that China disproves them only if it gets rich while staying authoritarian.

Back in the game

Civ has no domestic politics; you are the elite. The closest it comes VI is a conquered city with Unhappy citizens and no Governor, which tends to lose Loyalty (how firmly a city stays yours) each turn and at zero revolts into a Free City. In Civ VII VII each settlement past the settlement limit (how many settlements you can hold before penalties) costs 5 Happiness everywhere.

Check yourself

  1. You capture a rich city and give it nothing (no Amenities, no Governor, no builds). What will its Loyalty tend to do, and what is the fix?

    Show answer

    It tends to fall, and at zero the city revolts into a Free City, because you are running an extractive province. Invest instead, with Amenities, a Governor and builds, the game's version of inclusive rules.

  2. Two towns share a river, language and climate. One has secure property and open courts; the other's mayor seizes any business that succeeds. Which theory predicts the gap?

    Show answer

    Inclusive versus extractive institutions. The first town rewards investment and lets new firms rise; the second's elite takes and blocks change, so investment stops and the gap widens.

  3. A country grows fast for twenty years under one unelected ruler. What do Acemoglu and Robinson predict, and what would Sachs reply?

    Show answer

    They predict the growth stalls unless the rules open up, since extractive growth does not last. Sachs would answer that Prussia, Japan, South Korea and China all made deep reforms under unelected rulers.

In the game: treat a captured city as a province to win over, or expect to lose it.

In the world: when similar places diverge, ask first who makes the rules and who keeps the gains, then look at the map.

International relations: The Anglo-German naval race

British crowds chanted for more warships, and a German admiral planned a fleet Britain would fear to fight. Each side called its own ships defensive.

The case

In the winter of 1908–9 British crowds petitioned Parliament under the slogan "We want eight and we won't wait." The race behind the slogan was a decade old. In March 1898 Germany's parliament had passed a Navy Law for 19 battleships; a second law in 1900 raised the plan to 38. Admiral Tirpitz, who drove the program, had called Britain Germany's "most dangerous enemy," and a February 1900 memo from his office set out the idea historians call the "risk fleet," a navy Britain would fear to engage. Britain's standard since 1889 was a fleet equal to the next two navies combined. In December 1906 it commissioned HMS Dreadnought, which made every older battleship, Britain's included, obsolete. Germany followed, and a 1908 law sped up its building. Eight capital ships (battleships and battlecruisers) were authorized for 1909–10. In 1909 Britain would settle for a 60 percent lead; Tirpitz would concede only 4:3. Talks in 1912 failed. By July 1914 Britain had 29 capital ships in service and 13 building, Germany 18 and 8.

Sources: Sondhaus; GHDI; Royal Museums Greenwich; Wikipedia.

Concept lens: often taught as the textbook security dilemma. Each side called its own fleet defensive and read the other's as a threat, and a battleship can defend a coast or attack one, so offensive and defensive forces looked alike, which Jervis says sharpens the dilemma. Tirpitz's risk fleet was meant as deterrence; Britain's answer, more ships and then new partners, was balancing.

Where the concepts strain

  • A security dilemma needs both sides to want only safety. Tirpitz wanted the fleet as "a political power factor" against Britain, and the 1900 memo aimed to make Britain afraid to fight, so the model fits London's response better than Berlin's opening move.
  • The dilemma suggests arming spirals toward war, yet by 1912–13 Germany had in effect dropped out. The Reichstag authorized only five new capital ships for 1912–14 while funding a big army expansion in 1913, and the German fleet sat in port for most of the war; how much the race caused 1914 is still argued.

Back in the game

In Civ VI VI units near a rival's border earn a relationship penalty and leaders with the Paranoid agenda dislike strong militaries, so a buildup meant for defense raises the temperature. In Civ VII VII paying Influence to stay neutral when an ally goes to war signals defensive intent at a cost.

Check yourself

  1. Your neighbor's military score jumps, so you build an Encampment (the military district) and Knights. Next turn they denounce you. Explain in Jervis's terms.

    Show answer

    A security dilemma. Your defensive buildup read as offensive, because the AI judges strength rather than intent, so your step toward safety lowered theirs and they answered with hostility.

  2. Why did Dreadnought sharpen the dilemma rather than ease it?

    Show answer

    It made every older battleship obsolete, so the count restarted and each new hull mattered more. A battleship can defend a coast or attack one, so offense and defense looked alike, which Jervis says sharpens the dilemma.

  3. Two neighbors each announce a "purely defensive" missile program this year. What should you expect, and what could soften it?

    Show answer

    Each reads the other's program as offensive and buys more, so a race follows. Softening needs visibly defensive forces, costly signals of restraint, or an agreed ratio, which Britain and Germany never found.

In the game: before you arm, ask how the buildup reads to your rival, and arm in ways that look defensive.

In the world: your safe choice is someone else's threat; expect the reply, and show you want only safety.

Geopolitics: The Red Sea and the long way round

A ship from Shenzhen to Rotterdam takes about 31 days through Suez and 41 around Africa. In 2024 most shipping lines chose the long way.

The case

From late 2023, Yemen's Houthi movement fired missiles and drones at merchant ships in the Red Sea, calling the attacks solidarity with Palestinians in Gaza. The UN Security Council demanded that they stop, and American and British warships shot down missiles and drones. Most shipping lines still went around the Cape of Good Hope. The Suez Canal carries about 10 percent of world maritime trade by volume and 22 percent of container trade, and, says UNCTAD, the United Nations trade body, there is no ideal alternative to it for Asia to Europe trade. In UNCTAD's February 2024 assessment, monthly Suez transits were down 42 percent from their peak, container tonnage through the canal had fallen 82 percent, and container spot freight rates from Shanghai had risen 122 percent in two months. By June 2024, against mid-December 2023, Suez transits were down 70 percent and Cape arrivals up 89 percent. Citing Clarksons Research, UNCTAD put the detour's extra demand at 3 percent for ships in general and 12 percent for container ships. Figures are as of UNCTAD's 2024 reports.

Sources: UNCTAD, rapid assessment (February 2024); UNCTAD, Review of Maritime Transport 2024 (press release); UN Security Council (2024); Al Jazeera (2024).

Concept lens: a chokepoint is a narrow passage much traffic must use, and sea power, in Mahan's sense, is control of the sea lanes that run through such places. Who bears the cost when one closes is asymmetric interdependence, where whoever would suffer less if the ties were cut holds the leverage. The extra hulls are stocks and flows, since a 56-day India to Europe loop needs 8 ships and a 63-day one needs a ninth.

Where the concepts strain

  • Mahan expected a battle fleet to decide command of the sea. Here no battle between fleets took place; warships shot down missiles and drones, yet traffic through Suez was still down 70 percent in June 2024. Attacks on merchant ships, a kind of raiding Mahan treated as secondary, imposed costs that strong navies could not quickly remove.
  • Keohane and Nye wrote about ties between states where force was off the table; here an armed group outside any government's direct control used force. Trade adjusted by taking the long way, with added costs UNCTAD put at about $1.7 million per Asia to Europe round trip around the Cape against $1 million through Suez, spread thinly over many containers.

Back in the game

Civ VI VI has no blockade; a ship stops trade only by plundering a Trader, and raiders such as Privateers (naval raiding units) pillage coasts but cannot take cities. You cannot choose a Trader's path, only its destination. That is the Red Sea in miniature.

Check yourself

  1. You are at war, and enemy Privateers keep plundering Traders (units that run trade routes) on the six sea routes that cross one strait. What are your options, and what does each cost?

    Show answer

    You cannot choose a Trader's path, only its destination, so send routes to cities that avoid the strait, guard it with warships, or make peace. Each costs yields, ships or war aims.

  2. Rerouting moved the same cargo. Why did UNCTAD still report 12 percent more demand for container ships?

    Show answer

    Ships at sea are a stock; cargo per month is a flow. A longer voyage keeps each ship out longer, so the same flow needs more hulls, like the India to Europe loop needing a ninth.

  3. Pick any strait or canal in the news. Who gains if it closes, and who ends up paying?

    Show answer

    Whoever can threaten the passage cheaply gains bargaining power. The cost falls on shippers and consumers along the route, spread thinly, and hardest on trade with no alternative route, like Asia to Europe.

In the game: map the straits your trade routes pass through, and guard or avoid any that one enemy ship could close.

In the world: trade does not stop at a closed chokepoint; it goes the long way round, and everyone pays a little.

Strategy: Napoleon's march to Moscow

Napoleon took Moscow and lost his army. A Prussian officer fighting for Russia watched, and later named the point where winning turns into losing.

The case

Carl von Clausewitz, a Prussian officer, opposed his country's forced alliance with Napoleon, left the Prussian army and joined the Russian one. On 24 June 1812 Napoleon's Grande Armée began crossing the Niemen into Russia, about 450,000 men in the first wave of a force put at 600,000 to 685,000. Minard's 1869 chart draws the army as a thinning band, starting at 422,000. The Russians fell back through stripped country. Clausewitz fought for Russia at Borodino on 7 September, and Napoleon entered Moscow on 14 September with about 100,000 men. He held the old capital and could neither force a peace nor stay. He left on 19 October. The winter retreat, crossing the Berezina on 26–29 November, brought the toll to about 380,000 dead, missing or captured; Minard's band ends at 10,000. Clausewitz wrote his own account. His unfinished On War, published by his widow Marie from 1832, named the culminating point of the attack, where the attacker has only enough strength left to hold on and wait for peace; beyond it, he wrote, the scale turns and a counterblow follows.

Sources: Wikipedia (invasion of Russia; Clausewitz; Peninsular War; Fire of Moscow); Linda Hall Library; Routledge; Clausewitz, On War, trans. J. J. Graham (Book II, chapter 5; Book VII, chapter 5).

Concept lens: the home case for Clausewitz's culminating point. Sun Tzu warned that "there is no instance of a country having benefited from prolonged warfare" and told generals to forage on the enemy. Fighting in Spain, where 230,000 French troops were tied down, and in Russia at once resembles imperial overstretch.

Where the concepts strain

  • The culminating point is obvious only afterward. By the usual signs Napoleon was winning in September, having fought the main Russian army and taken Moscow. Clausewitz himself wrote that everything hung on whether taking Moscow would force Tsar Alexander to make peace, and that calling the campaigns of 1805 and 1807 wise and this one rash is judging "by the result."
  • Sun Tzu's "forage on the enemy" assumes there is something to take; stripped country denied it, so the maxim describes a choice the enemy can veto. Overstretch, for Kennedy, is a theory of decades, and Minard thought the cold mattered enough to plot the temperature beneath the retreat.

The hard question: would you burn your own city to beat an invader?

In the game a city is yields and a population count. Moscow in 1812 had around 270,000 people. Most fled before the French arrived, but 6,200 to 10,000 civilians stayed, along with 10,000 to 15,000 sick or wounded Russian soldiers. The city's military governor, Count Fyodor Rostopchin, had its fire engines removed or destroyed. Fires burned from 14 to 18 September and destroyed about three-quarters of the city. Of some 8,000 wooden houses, about 500 were left. Most historians blame the first fires on Russia's scorched-earth strategy, though who gave which order is still argued.

The case for the torch: Russia was fighting for its survival. An invader who finds food and shelter can stay, and one who finds ashes must leave. Denying Napoleon supplies struck his army where it was weakest, its logistics, and he left within five weeks.

The case against: the people who paid were Russians: the civilians who stayed, the wounded, and every family whose home burned. And the gain is doubtful. The French general Marcellin Marbot argued that enough buildings survived to house the entire army.

Your call: if destroying what your own people own might save the country, who gets to decide, and should they ask first?

Back in the game

In Civ VI VI units heal only 5 HP a turn in enemy land and fighting abroad doubles war weariness (unhappiness that grows the longer you fight), so a city assault has a turn after which you deal less damage than the city repairs. That turn is your culminating point.

Check yourself

  1. You hold two captured cities, your army is at half strength far from home, and the enemy capital is in sight. What does 1812 say to check?

    Show answer

    Whether you are past your culminating point. Compare damage dealt with the capital's healing, count turns for reinforcements, and ask if you can hold what you have; if not, consolidate or make peace.

  2. Define the culminating point of the attack, and say why September 1812 made it hard to see.

    Show answer

    The point where the attacker has only enough strength left to hold on and wait for peace; beyond it a counterblow follows. Napoleon had fought the main Russian army and taken Moscow, so the signs pointed the wrong way.

  3. A company buys firms in distant countries, paying for each deal with the last. Which two concepts apply, and what should its board watch?

    Show answer

    Overstretch and the culminating point. Watch whether each new commitment adds more cost than strength, and whether retreat is still possible before the means to hold the gains run out.

In the game: decide the turn you will stop before the push starts and write it down; the front never says stop.

In the world: success on the way in hides the point where going on starts to cost more than it gains.

Technology: Electricity reaches the factory floor

Electric motors spread through American factories for decades before output per worker jumped. The payoff waited for managers to rebuild the factory around them.

The case

In 1987 the economist Robert Solow quipped that computers were everywhere except in the productivity statistics. In 1990 Paul David borrowed the quip to ask why, and answered with the electric motor. In 1899 electric motors supplied less than 5 percent of the mechanical drive in American factories. Warren Devine's 1983 study traced the climb, to 25 percent by 1909, past steam by 1920, and about 78 percent by 1929. At first factories simply put a motor where the steam engine had been, turning the same overhead shafts and belts. From the mid-1890s to about 1920 "group drive" was in vogue, with each motor turning a section of shafting for a group of machines. The payoff came when managers put a motor on each machine ("unit drive") and redesigned the building around it, lighter, with machines arranged in the order of the work. David dates the productivity surge to the early 1920s, when only slightly more than half of factory drive was electrified, four decades after the first central power station opened. Devine found output per worker-hour in manufacturing rising 1.3 percent a year before 1919 and 3.1 percent after.

Sources: David (1990); Devine (1983); Solow (1987).

Concept lens: electricity is the model general-purpose technology, used everywhere and paying off only after users reorganized. The climb from 5 to 78 percent over thirty years fits the rising part of a diffusion S-curve. Shafts, belts and the buildings around them held factories to the old layout until the plants wore out, a loose case of path dependence, since the lock came from durable buildings rather than the increasing returns Arthur described.

Where the concepts strain

  • David's analogy forecast that computers would pay off once firms reorganized, as factories had. Nothing here fixes how long a later lag must be; forty years is one case, and a general-purpose technology can also stall.
  • David's finding that motor growth "statistically" accounts for about half of the 1920s speed-up in manufacturing productivity comes from comparing industries. It cannot cleanly separate electricity from other changes of the decade, such as continuous-process manufacturing, which he says were tied to it only indirectly.

Back in the game

In Civ VI VI Apprenticeship and Industrialization each add +1 Production (the yield that builds units and buildings) to every Mine the turn they complete; the real lag has no mechanic. In Civ VII VII old buildings keep lower yields until you overbuild them, so new yields arrive only when the old plant is replaced.

Check yourself

  1. You research Apprenticeship and every Mine gains Production the same turn. Which step of the electricity case did the game skip?

    Show answer

    The reorganization lag. Real gains waited until factories were rebuilt around unit drive. A truer rule would phase the bonus in, or grant it only after you rebuilt, as Civ VII's overbuilding does.

  2. A technology spreads from 5 to 78 percent of users in thirty years, but measured benefits appear only past the halfway mark. Which two concepts explain the shape and the delay?

    Show answer

    Diffusion of innovations gives the S-shaped spread. The general-purpose technology idea explains the delay, because users must reorganize before gains appear, so benefits trail adoption by years.

  3. Firms are spending heavily on a new general-purpose tool while output per worker barely moves. What does the dynamo case predict, and what evidence should you seek?

    Show answer

    A lag while work is reorganized around the tool, then gains. Look for redesigned workflows and replaced old systems rather than purchases; buying the motor and keeping the shafts gave almost nothing.

In the game: a yield-boosting tech is worth most in cities you are about to rebuild.

In the world: judge a general technology by how much work has been reorganized around it; adoption shows in budgets before output.

Society: The Korean Wave

In 2020 a Korean film won the Best Picture Oscar. A few years earlier, Korean shows had faded from Chinese screens. Attraction is power, until a bigger neighbor objects.

The case

In February 2020 Parasite became the first film not in English to win the Best Picture Oscar. In 2021, 142 million Netflix households watched Squid Game in its first four weeks. The Korea Foundation, surveying through 149 Korean missions abroad, counted 178 million hallyu fans in 2022, up from 9.26 million in 2012. Hallyu, the Korean Wave, began in the late 1990s, with help from the state: a 1999 law on cultural industries put film, music, games and broadcasting on the list for support. Joseph Nye, who coined "soft power" for attraction rather than force or payment, wrote in 2009 that South Korea's soft power "is not prisoner to the geographical limitations" of its hard power. In 2016–17, after Seoul agreed to host a United States missile-defense system (THAAD), Korean content faded from Chinese screens. Beijing announced no ban. A 2017 KEIA report, citing Chinese and Korean media, described an unofficial order to pull Korean shows from streaming sites, a list of 42 barred Korean celebrities, and no K-pop concert of over 10,000 seats since October 2016.

Sources: Nye (2009); WIPO Lex; oscars.org; UW; Netflix (2021); Korea Times (2023); KEIA (2017); CBS News (2017).

Concept lens: soft power with numbers attached. In Keohane and Nye's terms, China's leverage in 2017 came from asymmetric interdependence, because Korean studios and retailers stood to lose more from cut ties than Chinese viewers did.

Where the concepts strain

  • Nye stresses that governments do not control their own culture, so soft power is hard to steer. Korea is cited both ways, since the 1999 law came first, yet the biggest hits came from private studios and an American platform.
  • Attraction did not protect Korea when hard interests clashed. Beijing never acknowledged a ban, yet Korean shows and big concerts faded from China, and regulators closed 75 stores of Lotte, the group that supplied the land for THAAD, citing inspection failures. Soft power moved Chinese audiences and did not move Beijing.

Back in the game

Civ VI's Culture victory VI is the soft-power victory, and attraction travels along contact, since Tourism (cultural pull that wins a Culture victory) to a civ rises 25 percent with open borders and 25 percent more with a trade route. A rival refusing open borders only denies you a bonus; it cannot shut your culture out of its market, as Beijing did in 2017.

Check yourself

  1. You are close to a Culture victory when your biggest Tourism target denounces you and refuses open borders. What do you expect, and what do you do?

    Show answer

    A slower flow rather than a stopped one, since Civ only removes a bonus. Keep trade routes to them, shift effort to other rivals, and remember a real government can shut the door.

  2. A country's films are loved abroad, yet its diplomats win few votes in international bodies. Does this refute Nye?

    Show answer

    No. Soft power is one resource among several, slow and hard to steer, and attraction to a culture need not carry over to policy where governments rather than publics decide.

  3. A government announces a fund to "build soft power" through pop music. Give one reason to expect success and two for doubt.

    Show answer

    Korea's 1999 law did precede its boom. Doubt because the biggest hits came from private studios and a foreign platform, and because a displeased neighbor shut it out of its market in 2017.

In the game: build Tourism along contact, with open borders and trade routes to the rivals you want to win over.

In the world: attraction is real power that governments can feed but neither own nor defend; it bends when hard interests collide.

Decision science: The Cuban Missile Crisis

By crew accounts, on 27 October 1962 a Soviet submarine captain, believing he was under attack, ordered a nuclear torpedo readied. Threats work only when believed, and the most dangerous can come true by accident.

The case

On 27 October 1962 American ships used practice depth charges to signal the Soviet submarine B-59 to surface. In crew accounts gathered by the National Security Archive, its captain, believing he was under attack, ordered the nuclear torpedo readied, until the brigade's chief of staff, Vasily Arkhipov, saw that the Americans were only signaling. That same day a U-2 spy plane was shot down over Cuba. It had begun on 14 October, when a U-2 photographed Soviet nuclear missile sites in Cuba. On 22 October President Kennedy announced on television "a strict quarantine on all offensive military equipment under shipment to Cuba" and declared that any nuclear missile launched from Cuba against a nation in the Western Hemisphere would require "a full retaliatory response upon the Soviet Union." On 26 October Khrushchev offered to remove the missiles for a United States pledge not to invade Cuba; on 27 October he also demanded that American Jupiter missiles leave Turkey. Kennedy answered the first letter and ignored the second, while his brother Robert privately told the Soviet ambassador the Jupiters would be removed soon, though this could not be part of any public settlement. On 28 October Khrushchev announced the missiles would be dismantled.

Sources: Office of the Historian; American Presidency Project; National Security Archive; Allison (RAND, 1968); Burr and Nuti, Wilson Center (2023).

Concept lens: the televised threat and quarantine were commitments Kennedy could not cheaply walk back, and keeping the Jupiter promise out of the public settlement let the threat look as if it had held. The episode mixes deterrence (do not launch) with compellence (remove the missiles). Graham Allison (1969) argued that neither government acted as a single calculating mind; in one of his models, organizations follow routines and take the first workable option, which is Simon's bounded rationality.

Where the concepts strain

  • Schelling's chapter "The Threat That Leaves Something to Chance" treats a risk that leaders do not fully control as a tool they can still create on purpose, and B-59 shows how real that risk was. Yet the Americans did not know of the incident at the time, so neither side could judge how much risk it was running.
  • Firmness won, says the public story; firmness plus a no-invasion pledge and a secret promise on the Jupiters, says the archive. They teach opposite lessons about commitment, and Allison's three models each explain only part of the outcome.

The hard question: may leaders lie to keep the peace?

In Civ every term of a deal is on screen. The deal that ended the Cuban crisis had a hidden half. Only nine American officials knew of Robert Kennedy's promise on the Jupiters, which left Turkey in April 1963. In January 1963 Kennedy and his defense secretary, Robert McNamara, denied to Italy's prime minister that there had been a trade, and Secretary of State Dean Rusk told a Senate subcommittee that the removal had nothing to do with Cuba. Those who knew did not fully acknowledge the deal until 1989.

The case for secrecy: Kennedy saw a deal as essential to cut the risk of war, and a public swap under threat could have told every ally that its protection was for sale. The American ambassador in Turkey warned that the United States would lose much if the Turks got "the impression that their interests as an ally were being traded off in order to appease an enemy."

The case against: officials misled allies and the Senate, and voters were taught that firmness alone had won, a lesson only half true. Khrushchev, unable to show what he had gained, looked as if he had simply backed down.

Your call: if a lie helps end a nuclear standoff, when, if ever, is the public owed the truth?

Back in the game

In multiplayer VI a declared Friendship blocks war declarations for its length, and units parked on a border you said you would defend are the tripwire that makes a threat real. In Civ VII VII Sanctions and Treaties cost Influence, so they say more than a message.

Check yourself

  1. A rival marches an army to your border and types that it is "just training." You build Walls and park units in view. Which of Schelling's ideas are in play?

    Show answer

    Their message is cheap talk. Your Walls and visible units are costly signals and a commitment, and deterrence works only if they believe you will fight, which visible cost makes believable.

  2. Kennedy's televised threat was a commitment. What did the secret side of the deal add, and what does it do to the claim that firmness alone won?

    Show answer

    A promise, kept out of the public settlement, to remove the Jupiters from Turkey soon. The public story credits firmness alone; the archive shows Kennedy also paid, quietly, so commitment did not win by itself.

  3. Two governments trade public ultimatums over a disputed island this month. What should you watch for beyond the speeches?

    Show answer

    Back channels and face-saving trades, forces operating close enough for accidents, and whether each side has left itself a way to step back without visible loss. The settlement is often the part nobody announces.

In the game: back every promise and threat with something that costs you if you break it.

In the world: a threat you cannot retreat from is powerful and dangerous in equal measure; leave the other side a door.

Capstone: A Grand Strategy Memo for a Real Country

In Part 5 you wrote a memo for your civ. Now write one for a real country.

The template

Grand strategy is the use of all a state's resources to reach its main goals; Lykke's test is whether ends, ways and means balance. Pick a country you do not live in, to argue from data rather than loyalty.

HeadingWhat to write
EndsTwo or three goals any government would keep, such as secure territory, rising living standards and a seat at the table.
MeansPopulation, income, trade, armed forces and budget, each with its source.
ConstraintsGeography (coasts, chokepoints, markets), economy (exports and buyers), institutions (how fast decisions change), neighbors (who fears whom).
WaysThree concrete actions (alliances, trade deals, posture, investment) that turn means into ends.
Security dilemma checkHow each way reads from the neighbor's capital, and what signal shows it is not a threat.
Pivot ruleOne observable event that would change the plan, written down in advance.
Check-inThe date you will reread the memo against the news.

Worked example, Britain 1900–1909

Ends. Keep the sea lanes and empire secure with a navy as strong as the next two combined (the 1889 two-power standard). Means. The world's largest fleet, deep finances, a small army; the Boer War of 1899–1902 took about 448,000 imperial and colonial troops. Constraints. Russia was expanding toward Manchuria and Korea, Germany had passed navy laws in 1898 and 1900, and Britain wanted to cut imperial overcommitment and bring the fleet home, an overstretch problem. "Splendid isolation," a phrase from 1896, no longer matched the means. Ways. The Anglo-Japanese Alliance of 30 January 1902 promised neutrality if either partner fought one power and help if it fought two, and is credited with deterring France from joining Russia's war with Japan. The Entente Cordiale of 8 April 1904 settled colonial disputes with France, Egypt for Morocco, short of an alliance. The Anglo-Russian Convention of 31 August 1907 settled Persia, Afghanistan and Tibet and later formed part of the Triple Entente. If these deals were aimed at Germany they were balancing in Walt's sense; historians debate that, and the 1904 entente was not framed against Germany. Security dilemma check. Seen from Berlin, three settlements with Germany's neighbors could look like a ring, whatever London intended; Sondhaus notes the German naval threat had helped drive Britain into the Triple Entente. Pivot rule. A rule history would have tested is "if Germany speeds up its building, outbuild it." Germany's 1908 law did, and Britain authorized eight capital ships for 1909–10.

Where to get the numbers

Use neutral public sources. World Bank Open Data covers population, income, trade and debt; the SIPRI Military Expenditure Database covers defense spending; UN Comtrade shows who sells what to whom; IMF PortWatch counts daily ship transits at Suez, the Cape and Panama; Our World in Data has long series; V-Dem and the Polity Project score institutions. The CIA announced on 4 February 2026 that it had ended its World Factbook, so use the sources above instead.

In the game: write the memo for your civ before turn 1 and for a real country before you next read the news.

In the world: a grand strategy is shared goals, measured means, ways your neighbors can read as defensive, and a rule for changing your mind.
